Sadlier Unit 1: The Times of Zheng He

Meet Zheng He, the Ming admiral whose enormous diplomatic fleets carried China’s influence across the seas—and learn all 16 Unit 1 words in context.

6:08 · Story, knowledge, and vocabulary

Download full episode

Words in This Episode

3:08 · Slow vocabulary review

Download words review
Words in this episode
interminable
seeming to have no end“The long wait at the airport felt interminable.”
adjacent
next to or very near something“Our classroom is adjacent to the library.”
barren
dry and empty, where almost nothing can grow“The desert was barren and hot.”
disrupt
to break up or throw into confusion; to interrupt“The fire drill disrupted our test.”
sullen
gloomy and silent, in a bad mood“He was sullen after his team lost.”
insinuate
to slowly and cleverly work your way into a place or a group“She insinuated herself into the club by being extra friendly.”
foretaste
a small sample of what is coming later“The movie trailer was a foretaste of the film.”
renovate
to repair something old and make it new again“They renovated the old library last summer.”
vicious
cruel and violent“The vicious dog barked at everyone.”
trickle
a slow, thin flow of liquid“Water came out of the broken faucet in a trickle.”
prestige
respect and admiration earned by success“Winning the science prize brought her great prestige.”
résumé
a short written list of your jobs, skills, and achievements“He sent his résumé to three companies.”
intimidating
frightening; making people feel nervous“The big exam was intimidating.”
alight
to step down from or land on something“The bird alighted on the fence.”
interrogate
to ask someone a lot of serious questions“The detective interrogated the witness for an hour.”
germinate
to begin to grow, like a seed sprouting“The bean seeds germinated in the warm soil.”
Original text

The Times of Zheng He

<Narrative Nonfiction>

(Sadlier Unit 1 reading passage — transcribed from textbook photo; boldface words are the Unit 1 vocabulary)

Zheng He was born in the year 1371, in the Chinese province of Yunnan. He was descended from a Persian who had been governor of Yunnan over a century before, in the early days of the Mongol-led Yuan Dynasty. The family retained its connections to the Mongol rulers of China through those years. But nothing is interminable in this world. A change was in the wind. About the time Zheng He was born, the Ming clan asserted itself as the new ruling dynasty in China. Ming armies pushed the Mongols out of China and back to their homeland in Mongolia, an adjacent country in the north, dry and rather barren.

While Zheng He was still a boy, pockets of Mongol loyalists remained in certain areas of China, not least in the southern province of Yunnan. Unrest erupted wherever loyalists lived. Zheng He was about ten years old when political turmoil disrupted his life, forever altering its course. His father was killed in 1381 during the Ming army's conquest of Yunnan. Zheng He was captured and sent to the court of Prince Zhu Di, son of the Ming emperor. If the boy was sullen and withdrawn at first, he did not remain so for long. He became one of the prince's trusted advisors, gradually insinuating himself into a position of respect. In time, Prince Zhu Di sought to depose his own nephew, who had become the second Ming emperor. When the hour came, he relied on Zheng He's assistance. And when the insurrection proved successful, Zhu Di had become the third Ming emperor of China, and Zheng He was now the right hand to the emperor.

This adventure was only a foretaste of what lay ahead for Zheng He. The new emperor had ambitious plans to renovate China after years of vicious war had reduced the population and stalled production to a trickle. The emperor planned to restore the economy, promote construction, maintain order and protect the borders, stimulate culture and learning, and extend China's influence throughout the world. As part of his vision, he sponsored a series of massive naval expeditions, appointing Zheng He to be chief envoy and leader of the fleet.

Zheng He led six expeditions for the emperor, his own prestige increasing with each notch in his résumé. The first expedition set off in 1405, with about 300 huge ships and nearly 30,000 men. At the time, it was the largest fleet ever to have sailed from China.

The main purpose of the expeditions was not exploration, or conquest, or commerce, but diplomacy. Zheng He's giant fleet and the soldiers who manned it made a strong impression wherever they went. They projected an image of Chinese wealth and power. The troops were rarely called on for a display of force; the size of the fleet was intimidating enough. Wherever Zheng He alighted, he had an opportunity to interrogate the locals for news, meet with local elites to exchange gifts and information, and represent the interests of the Chinese emperor.

The emperor died in 1424. His successors did not continue his maritime policy. Zheng He did one more fleet in 1433. He died during this last great expedition and was buried at sea. Seeds of foreign relations planted during his lifetime germinated even after he was gone.

Boldface words (16): interminable, adjacent, barren, disrupted, sullen, insinuating, foretaste, renovate, vicious, trickle, prestige, résumé, intimidating, alighted, interrogate, germinated.

Original reading passage · Sadlier Vocabulary Workshop, Unit 1

3-question quiz

  1. The word barren most nearly means:

    • dry and empty
    • full of flowers
    • deep and wide
    • soft and warm
    Check answer

    dry and empty

  2. If a meeting feels interminable, it feels:

    • super short
    • like it will never end
    • really exciting
    • very loud
    Check answer

    like it will never end

  3. To renovate an old house means to:

    • knock it down
    • repair it and make it new again
    • paint it a funny color
    • move it to a new street
    Check answer

    repair it and make it new again

Words in this episode
combatants
people or groups fighting against each other“The combatants in the advertising war are the food companies and the worried parents.”
pelting
throwing lots of things at someone again and again“The companies are pelting kids with ads for candy and soda.”
firebrand
a person who stirs up strong feelings and arguments“She said, "I am no troublesome firebrand," meaning she is not trying to start a fight.”
indignation
anger about something unfair“He felt indignation when he saw the unfair rule.”
indifference
not caring about something“The company's indifference to kids' health made her angry.”
caters
provides what someone wants, or tries to please a certain group“That TV channel caters to young children.”
sophistication
the knowledge or experience needed to understand tricky things“Little kids don't have the sophistication to see through clever ads.”
hazard
something dangerous“Being very overweight is a health hazard.”
indispensable
absolutely necessary“Good health is indispensable for a happy life.”
poised
ready, or about to happen“Our generation is poised to make healthier choices.”
available
ready to use, something you can get“The advertisers use every available website and TV channel.”
unethical
wrong or unfair“Tricking kids into eating junk food is unethical.”
hinder
to slow something down or make it harder“Sugary ads hinder kids from making healthy choices.”
transparent
open and honest, hiding nothing“She wants food companies to be transparent about their products.”
customary
usual, what people normally do“As a nurse, it is customary for her to care about children's health.”
dissuade
to talk someone out of doing something“She tries to dissuade kids from drinking soda.”
plague
to bother or annoy someone again and again“She will continue to plague the junk-food sellers with complaints.”
mutually
shared by both sides“The two companies mutually benefit from the deal.”
Original text

In Poor Taste
<Letter to the Editor>

To the Editor:
RE: “Fast-Food Ads Target Kids” (Business section, October 29):

Our children are sitting ducks in an advertising war. The “combatants” are the food and beverage companies, advertising agencies, and media corporations pelting kids with a dangerous diet of junk food and soft drink ads.

I am no troublesome firebrand. I am a nurse at a middle school. I am also the mother of three school-age children. I am writing to express my indignation and frustration at the indifference of those trying to get one over on the public and, specifically, our kids.

Turn on any children’s TV show or visit a website that caters to kids. You’ll be bombarded with cute characters proclaiming how cool it is to eat Brand “X” cereal or Brand “Y” convenience food. Web sites are full of banner ads and interactive games with commercial content. The problem is, most young children don’t have the sophistication to understand the power of persuasive commercials. They think if their favorite animated dragon says eating sugar-laden gooey treats is good for you, it must be so.

According to the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention, childhood obesity in the United States has nearly tripled in the past 30 years. Being significantly overweight is a dangerous health hazard. Plus, rates for both diabetes and asthma are soaring. Experts say the rise in both is linked to the rise in childhood obesity. And good health is indispensable for a long and happy life. Our youngest generation is poised to become the unhealthiest in history.

Despite these alarming facts, the food industry and their advertisers use every available media outlet to promote and sell their products. Although their marketing techniques don’t break any laws, pushing food and drinks low in nutritional value and high in sugar, salt, and fat is unethical. Instead of encouraging kids to make good food choices, most of these companies use misleading tactics that hinder healthy eating goals. In fact, a recent university study found that, on any given day, one-third of American children and teens eat fast food.

In an ideal world, I’d like all food advertisements aimed at kids banned from children’s programming. But I know that’s not realistic. After all, we’re talking about a multibillion-dollar industry. So I demand that companies involved in the making, marketing, and selling of food be required to be far more transparent in their advertising to children. I also demand that regulating agencies such as the Federal Trade Commission set far tougher policies. It’s painfully clear that self-regulating guidelines for the food industry are not working.

As a nurse and mom, it is customary for me to be concerned about the health of kids. I will keep doing all I can to dissuade them from choosing junk food over healthy food. I will also continue to plague junk-food peddlers and the government officials who mutually benefit from leaving things as they are.

Human beings are, after all, just human. We’ll always have our bad habits. But cigarette advertising has been banned from the airwaves. Alcohol advertising is strictly regulated. So why do we allow advertising for junk food?

K.D. Nettles, R.N.
Yarmouth, Maine

Original reading passage · Sadlier Vocabulary Workshop, Unit 2

    • absolutely necessary
    • unfair
    • hidden
    Check answer

  2. If ads "hinder" healthy eating, what do they do?

    • slow it down or make it harder
    • help it along
    • ban it completely
    • pay for it
    Check answer

  3. What does it mean to "dissuade" someone?

    • to sell them food
    • to trick them
    Check answer

    to talk them out of doing something

Original text

Lunch at Delmonico’s
<Diary Entry>

October 18, 1876

I’ve had little rest since arriving in New York on business. Harriet and I feel at home at the Fifth Avenue Hotel, which has proved a fine and downright respectable establishment.

Today she set off early, in a quick and animated spirit, to meet with a certain ladies’ group downtown. No doubt the whole brood spent the afternoon discussing women’s suffrage. That topic has entirely absorbed my wife’s attention of late, whenever she has not been seething with excitement about going to the theater. I’ve promised Harriet I will indulge myself by joining her along with her sister and Mr. Treadwell at the theater this evening.

The food here in New York City is quite good. It seems the art of cooking, and with it the sport of eating, culminates on this very avenue at Delmonico’s. Of course, I’d heard it said that Delmonico’s is the finest restaurant in the city, or even in the country. But there’s no substitute for having a look at the facts yourself. I’ve been to Delmonico’s at Madison Square three times already for business luncheons. I daresay I’ve managed to verify the rumors.

Everything at Delmonico’s has the luster of elegance and good taste. The walls are lined with great mirrors. The furnishings are fine mahogany. Silver chandeliers hang from a painted ceiling. In the center of the main dining room there is a large fountain surrounded by flowers. This room enjoys a view of Fifth Avenue, and the well-placed diner can see a well-kept lawn and the trees and gardens of Madison Square. If he forgets to look up from his table, he’ll be delighted almost as much by the craft of every miscellaneous detail, from the tablecloth to the silverware. I kept my eye open for an Astor or Vanderbilt to walk in, but I have yet to see one of the many wealthy and powerful men who frequent Delmonico’s.

The menus are entirely in French. I confess at first I was somewhat peevish and put-off at being forced to choose between guessing what I should order and begging the help of some translator. The upright waiters of the establishment have been of some help in this regard, although my New York associates seem almost as well acquainted with the menu. A few constituents of the menu alarmed me—there was one dish consisting primarily of snails. But my companion on that occasion, Mr. Jameson, managed to goad me into trying it. After my initial shock, I found it a delicate and perfectly delicious dish. I was also persuaded to try the delicious Lobster à la Wenberg. I’m told this is a new concoction of the chef’s. I must say, however, that having sampled a great variety of dishes that were new and unique to me, I began last night to hanker for a good piece of beef. The Steak Delmonico I enjoyed for lunch this afternoon was as fine a cut as I’ve tasted anywhere out West. The chef cooks them to the customer’s preference. I ordered mine rare and slightly singed.

In fact, I’ve just returned from that meal, which I took alone. It’s left me in fine spirits (whoever said “the more the merrier” got it wrong). For the moment, I’ve forgotten about the work ahead of me tomorrow. I suppose an evening at the theater with Harriet and the Treadwells will do me some good after all.

LeisureEpisode 596:33

Reading Skills: How to Become a Super Reader

Build stronger reading skills by finding central ideas, making inferences, studying craft and structure, comparing viewpoints, and checking evidence.

Full episode

6:33 · Story, knowledge, and vocabulary

Download full episode

Words in This Episode

1:40 · Slow vocabulary review

Download words review
Words in this episode
explicit
stated directly and clearly“The author's explicit message was that reading takes practice.”
inference
an educated guess based on known information and careful reasoning“From the description of the beach, I made the inference that the writer loves the sea.”
central
the most important“The central idea of the article is that readers need skills.”
theme
an overall message about life in a story“The theme of the novel is that courage matters most.”
craft
the skill of making something well“Readers study the author's craft to see how the writing works.”
structure
the way the parts of something are organized“The story's structure moves from morning to night.”
point of view
a person's attitude about an idea“Each character has a different point of view.”
claim
a statement the author says is true“The writer's claim was that reading changes the brain.”
evidence
facts that support a claim“Good readers always check the evidence behind every claim.”
hypertext
text with clickable words that lead to more information“Hypertext lets you click a word to learn more about it.”
Transcript

Maya: Leo, I have a question for you. You spend hours reading every week. But how do you actually do it? I mean, what is happening in your head when your eyes move across a page?

Leo: Honestly, I never thought about it. Reading just feels automatic, like breathing.

Maya: That is the amazing part. Reading feels easy, but your brain is doing something incredibly complex. Today, let us talk about reading itself, and the skills that turn regular readers into super readers.

Leo: Reading about reading? I like it. Where do we start?

Maya: First, the history. Reading was once rare. Only the most powerful and educated people in a society could read or write. Then, in the fourteen hundreds, Johannes Gutenberg invented a way to print books quickly and easily. Suddenly, there was a lot to read, and a lot more people learned how.

Leo: So printing made reading a skill for everyone, not just the rich and powerful.

Maya: Exactly. And today, people read every day to find useful information, learn new skills, talk with others, and have fun. We read fiction, nonfiction, poems, and plays, and each kind of text needs its own skills.

Leo: Different texts, different skills. So what makes someone a really good reader?

Maya: Effective readers use three big skills. Skill one is getting the ideas. Every text is full of ideas, and your first job is to find and understand them.

Leo: That sounds simple. You just read what is there.

Maya: Sometimes. Some ideas are explicit, which means they are stated directly. But other times, you have to read between the lines and make an inference, an educated guess based on what you know and careful reasoning.

Leo: Like when a writer calls the beach beautiful and soothing, I can infer that the writer likes the beach, even if they never say it directly.

Maya: Perfect. Now, not all ideas matter equally. The most important ideas are called the central or main ideas. Everything else is details that support them. And in literature, there is often a theme, an overall message about life that the whole story teaches you.

Leo: So the central ideas are the skeleton, the details are the muscles, and the theme is the heart?

Maya: Nice image. Skill two is understanding craft and structure. Strong readers look deeper and ask how the author built the text. They pay attention to vocabulary, the words the author chose, and to structure, how the ideas are organized. Some authors compare and contrast ideas, others put ideas in order of time or importance.

Leo: And what about whose side the author is on?

Maya: That is part of it too. Every author has a point of view, an attitude about the ideas being discussed. That attitude shapes the author's purpose, whether it is to inform, entertain, or persuade. In stories, the characters have their own points of view as well, and noticing them helps you understand the story better.

Leo: Okay, skills one and two are about understanding the text. What is skill three?

Maya: Using the ideas and knowledge you gained. Reading means little if you never use what you learned. You can compare the text with other ideas, think about whether you agree with it, and even imagine the story in a different medium, like turning a book into a movie.

Leo: I do that all the time. The movie is never as good as the book.

Maya: Here is a big one for persuasive writing. Authors make claims, statements they say are true, and good authors support their claims with evidence. But not all evidence is strong or correct, so strong readers check whether the claims are really supported.

Leo: So do not just believe everything you read. Check the evidence.

Maya: Exactly. Now, a quick trip inside your head. How does the brain read? The temporal and frontal lobes are the brain parts that help us understand words and language. Two small parts called gyrus help the other brain parts work together, so letters become words and words become sentences.

Leo: My brain is working hard right now just hearing about it.

Maya: It is. And the good news is, you can train it. Find the important ideas, understand the craft and structure, and use what you learn. Master those skills, and you can take on new kinds of text and harder and harder books.

Leo: Sounds like a workout plan for the brain. Ready for our vocabulary spotlight?

Maya: Let us slow down and learn our words together.

Maya: Word one is explicit, which means stated directly and clearly. The author's explicit message was that reading takes practice.

Leo: Word two is inference, which means an educated guess based on known information and careful reasoning. From the description of the beach, I made the inference that the writer loves the sea.

Maya: Word three is central, which means the most important. The central idea of the article is that readers need skills.

Leo: Word four is theme, which means an overall message about life in a story. The theme of the novel is that courage matters most.

Maya: Word five is craft, which means the skill of making something well. Readers study the author's craft to see how the writing works.

Leo: Word six is structure, which means the way the parts of something are organized. The story's structure moves from morning to night.

Maya: Word seven is point of view, which means a person's attitude about an idea. Each character has a different point of view.

Leo: Word eight is claim, which means a statement the author says is true. The writer's claim was that reading changes the brain.

Maya: Word nine is evidence, which means facts that support a claim. Good readers always check the evidence behind every claim.

Leo: And word ten is hypertext, which means text with clickable words that lead to more information. Hypertext lets you click a word to learn more about it.

Maya: Great listening. Now let us see what you remember.

Maya: Quiz time. Question one. What is an inference?

Leo: Think for a moment. An inference is an educated guess based on known information and careful reasoning.

Maya: Question two. True or false. Authors who make claims always support them with strong, correct evidence.

Leo: Think it through. False. Not all evidence is strong or correct, so readers should check whether the claims are really supported.

Maya: Last one. The most important ideas in a text are called the central ideas, the details, the hypertext, or the gyrus?

Leo: Take a guess. They are called the central ideas. The details only support them.

Maya: You did great. So remember, reading is a workout for your brain. Find the important ideas, notice how the author built the text, and always check the evidence. Keep reading, and you will keep growing as a reader. See you next time on The World, Word by Word.

Episode transcript · Produced September 10, 2026

LeisureEpisode 586:20

Literature Devices: A Writer's Toolbox

Open a writer’s toolbox of simile, metaphor, personification, symbolism, allusion, foreshadowing, sound words, idioms, and exaggeration.

Full episode

6:20 · Story, knowledge, and vocabulary

Download full episode

Words in This Episode

1:45 · Slow vocabulary review

Download words review
Words in this episode
simile
a comparison that uses the word like or as“Her smile was as bright as the sun.”
metaphor
a direct comparison of two things that are not alike, without using like or as“His room is a pigsty.”
personification
giving human qualities to something that is not human“The wind whispered through the trees.”
symbolism
using a thing or an idea to stand for a larger meaning“The flag is a symbol of the country.”
allusion
a reference to a real person, place, or famous story“He thinks he is Superman, alluding to the famous hero.”
foreshadowing
giving small clues about what will happen later in a story“Dark clouds in the story foreshadow trouble ahead.”
alliteration
repeating the same beginning sound in nearby words“Peter Piper picked a pepper.”
onomatopoeia
a word that sounds like the noise it names“The bees went buzz, buzz around the flowers.”
hyperbole
a big exaggeration used for effect or humor“I told you a million times to clean your room.”
idiom
a group of words with a special meaning that you cannot guess from the single words“Break a leg means good luck on stage.”
Transcript

Maya: Welcome to The World, Word by Word, the show where we explore cool topics and learn great new words. I'm Maya.

Leo: And I'm Leo. Today, we're talking about the secret tools writers use to make their stories shine: literary devices.

Maya: Think of it this way. A writer is a little like a builder. A builder needs a toolbox full of hammers, nails, and drills. And a writer needs a toolbox full of literary devices.

Leo: These are techniques and tricks that help writers share ideas and feelings with their readers. They can create strong pictures in your mind, and they can set the mood of a story, whether it's happy, scary, or funny.

Maya: One big group of these tools connects ideas in interesting ways. The two most famous are the simile and the metaphor. Both compare two things that are not alike. A simile compares them indirectly, using the words like or as.

Leo: So if I say, that dog is as big as a horse, that's a simile, because I used the word as. But if I say, that dog is a horse, that's a metaphor, because I'm comparing them directly, without like or as.

Maya: Nice example. Another one is personification, which gives human qualities to something that is not human. Like, the long grass danced in the breeze. Grass can't really dance, but now you can see it moving like a dancer.

Leo: Then there's symbolism, where one thing stands for a bigger idea. A dove can symbolize peace. And in stories, dark storm clouds might symbolize bad luck or danger coming.

Maya: Writers also love allusion, which is a reference to a real person, place, or famous story. Saying a bully is as big and mean as Godzilla is an allusion to the famous movie monster.

Leo: And there are devices that shape the story itself. A flashback pauses the story to visit something that happened in the past, maybe a happy memory from an old home. Foreshadowing goes the other way: it gives you small clues about what will happen next. If the car's engine starts making a strange noise, you can guess the engine will break down later.

Maya: A second group of devices plays with words and sounds. Alliteration repeats the same beginning sound in words, like smooth slow slide. It makes writing catchy and memorable.

Leo: Onomatopoeia is even more fun. It means a word that sounds like the noise it names, like clickety-clack for horse hooves on a road. The writer James Joyce loved this trick. He once invented a one-hundred-letter word just to sound like a thunderclap.

Maya: There are also devices that make descriptions colorful. An idiom is a group of words that means something surprising, like it's raining cats and dogs, or that car cost an arm and a leg.

Leo: And hyperbole uses exaggeration, usually to be funny. Like, Max was so hungry he could eat a horse. Nobody can really eat a horse, but now you know Max is extremely hungry.

Maya: One word of advice from our source: great writers use these tools, but not too many at once. Too many devices can make writing messy. And they avoid clichés, which are phrases everyone has heard a million times.

Leo: So now you know the tools in a writer's toolbox. Want to learn the words? Let's slow down and meet our ten words of the day.

Maya: Here we go. Let's learn our words, nice and slow. Word one is simile, which means a comparison that uses the word like or as. For example, her smile was as bright as the sun.

Leo: Word two is metaphor, which means a direct comparison of two things that are not alike, without using like or as. For example, his room is a pigsty.

Maya: Word three is personification, which means giving human qualities to something that is not human. For example, the wind whispered through the trees.

Leo: Word four is symbolism, which means using a thing or an idea to stand for a larger meaning. For example, the flag is a symbol of the country.

Maya: Word five is allusion, which means a reference to a real person, place, or famous story. For example, he thinks he is Superman, alluding to the famous hero.

Leo: Word six is foreshadowing, which means giving small clues about what will happen later in a story. For example, dark clouds in the story foreshadow trouble ahead.

Maya: Word seven is alliteration, which means repeating the same beginning sound in nearby words. For example, Peter Piper picked a pepper.

Leo: Word eight is onomatopoeia, which means a word that sounds like the noise it names. For example, the bees went buzz, buzz around the flowers.

Maya: Word nine is hyperbole, which means a big exaggeration used for effect or humor. For example, I told you a million times to clean your room.

Leo: Word ten is idiom, which means a group of words with a special meaning that you cannot guess from the single words. For example, break a leg means good luck on stage.

Maya: Great job following along. That's all ten words. Now let's see how much you remember.

Leo: Time for our quiz. Three questions. Ready? Question one: The sentence, the world is a stage, compares two unlike things directly. Is that a simile or a metaphor?

Maya: Think about it. Did the sentence use like or as? No, it did not. So the answer is a metaphor. A simile would say, the world is like a stage.

Leo: Question two: A writer says, I am so hungry I could eat an entire restaurant. Which device is that, hyperbole or symbolism?

Maya: That's a big exaggeration, so the answer is hyperbole. Symbolism is when something stands for a bigger idea, not when you exaggerate.

Leo: Last one, question three: In a story, a character hears a strange scratching sound at the window, and the writer spells it out as skritch, skritch. What device makes the word sound like the noise?

Maya: That is onomatopoeia, a word that sounds like the noise it names. Skritch, skritch is a perfect example. Nicely done, everyone.

Leo: And that is the end of our episode on literary devices, the writer's toolbox for building amazing stories.

Maya: Next time you read a book, try to spot the similes, the foreshadowing, and the hyperbole hiding on the page. You will see them everywhere now.

Leo: Thanks for listening to The World, Word by Word. See you next time.

Maya: Bye, everyone.

LeisureEpisode 566:40

Novels

What makes a novel work? Explore fiction, prose, plot, setting, theme, point of view, protagonists, antagonists, and the quests that drive long stories.

Full episode

6:40 · Story, knowledge, and vocabulary

Download full episode

Words in This Episode

1:53 · Slow vocabulary review

Download words review
Words in this episode
fiction
Fiction means stories that are invented, not true“the novel about talking dragons is fiction.”
prose
Prose means the ordinary style of language used in writing and speaking, not poetry“most novels are written in prose.”
novella
Novella means a short novel, usually about thirty thousand words“she wrote a novella over the summer.”
protagonist
Protagonist means the main character of a story“the protagonist of Robinson Crusoe is the shipwrecked sailor.”
antagonist
Antagonist means a character who creates conflict in a story“the antagonist tries to stop the hero.”
plot
Plot means the action and the events in a story“the plot of the novel had many surprising twists.”
setting
Setting means the time and the place where a story happens“the setting is a small fishing village in the nineteen fifties.”
theme
Theme means the main idea or the point of a story“a common theme is the struggle between good and evil.”
point of view
Point of view means who is telling the story“the novel is told from the point of view of a twelve-year-old girl.”
quest
Quest means a long and difficult journey to complete a task“the hero goes on a quest to live like a knight.”
Transcript

Maya: Welcome to The World, Word by Word! I'm Maya.

Leo: And I'm Leo. Today's topic is something close to every book lover's heart: novels.

Maya: Think about the last long story you read, maybe a book you couldn't put down. Chances are, it was a novel.

Leo: So what exactly makes a book a novel? Let's start with the basics.

Maya: A novel is a book-length work of fiction. That means it tells a story that is invented, not true. And it usually has three big ingredients: characters, a plot, and actions.

Leo: Characters are the people, or sometimes animals, in the story. The plot is what happens, the chain of events. And actions are the things the characters do.

Maya: And here is a fun fact: the word novel comes from the Italian word novella, which means new story. So a novel is, quite literally, a new story told in a book.

Leo: There is also a shorter cousin called the novella. A novella is about thirty thousand words long, while a novel is usually eighty thousand to one hundred thousand words. And novels have more complex plots, more twists and turns.

Maya: Now, which book was the very first novel? That is a question experts still argue about.

Leo: Some say it was Le Morte d'Arthur, from fourteen eighty-five, about King Arthur and his knights. Others say it was Don Quixote, written in sixteen oh five by a Spanish writer named Miguel de Cervantes.

Maya: And many experts look even further back, to Japan, about the year ten ten. A book called The Tale of Genji, written by a court lady, is thought by some to be the world's first novel.

Leo: Then in England, the novel really took off in the seventeen hundreds. Daniel Defoe wrote Robinson Crusoe in seventeen nineteen, about a man shipwrecked on a deserted island who learns to survive all alone.

Maya: Defoe was born in London in sixteen sixty. Before he became a full-time writer, he sold wine and wool and was often in debt. But his books became some of the most popular novels in the world.

Leo: So we know what a novel is and where it came from. But what is a novel made of? Experts say there are five key elements.

Maya: Let's walk through them. First, the plot. That is the action and the events of the story. There is often a struggle, and the characters must grow and learn to deal with their problems.

Leo: Second, the setting. That is the time and place of the story. It can be the past, the present, or the future. Even small details, like the characters' clothes or the weather, are part of the setting.

Maya: Third, the theme. The theme is sometimes called the point of the story. It is the big idea the author wants you to think about, like the struggle between good and evil.

Leo: Fourth, the point of view. That is who is telling the story. In first person, the main character tells it, and you only know what that character knows. In third person, a narrator tells the story from the outside.

Maya: And fifth, the characters themselves. Many novels have a protagonist, the main character, and an antagonist, the character who creates conflict. When a character changes, the reader should understand why the story made them change.

Leo: Let me give you a great example from the article we read: The Adventures of Pinocchio. It is about a wooden puppet who comes to life, makes bad choices, and has wild adventures.

Maya: And it has so many themes: bad behavior leads to punishment, children should respect their parents, people deserve a second chance, and family love matters. One story, many big ideas.

Leo: That's the magic of novels. The best ones entertain you, but they also help you learn something about yourself and the world.

Maya: Now, before our quiz, let's slow down and learn our ten words of the day.

Leo: Here are ten useful words from today's topic. Listen carefully. We will go slowly.

Maya: Word one is fiction, which means stories that are invented, not true. For example: The novel about talking dragons is fiction, but the history textbook is not fiction.

Leo: Word two is prose, which means the ordinary style of language used in writing and speaking, not poetry. For example: Most novels are written in prose, so they look like normal paragraphs on the page.

Maya: Word three is novella, which means a short novel, usually about thirty thousand words. For example: She wrote a novella over the summer because it was shorter and faster to finish than a full novel.

Leo: Word four is protagonist, which means the main character of a story. For example: In Robinson Crusoe, the protagonist is the sailor who is shipwrecked on the island.

Maya: Word five is antagonist, which means a character who creates conflict in a story. For example: In many adventure stories, the antagonist tries to stop the hero from reaching the treasure.

Leo: Word six is plot, which means the action and the events in a story. For example: The plot of the novel had so many twists that I could not guess the ending.

Maya: Word seven is setting, which means the time and the place where a story happens. For example: The setting of the story is a small fishing village in the nineteen fifties.

Leo: Word eight is theme, which means the main idea or the point of a story. For example: A common theme in novels is the struggle between good and evil.

Maya: Word nine is point of view, which means the way the reader experiences the story, or who is telling it. For example: The novel is told from the point of view of a twelve-year-old girl.

Leo: Word ten is quest, which means a long and difficult journey to complete a task. For example: In Don Quixote, the hero goes on a quest to live like a knight from old stories.

Maya: Let's hear those ten words once more: fiction, prose, novella, protagonist, antagonist, plot, setting, theme, point of view, and quest.

Leo: Great. Now you are ready for the quiz.

Maya: And now, the quiz. Three questions. See how many you get right.

Leo: Question one. A novel is a book-length work of what? Is it fiction, or is it always true?

Maya: The answer is fiction. A novel tells an invented story, with characters, a plot, and actions.

Leo: Question two. What are the five key elements of a novel? Name as many as you can.

Maya: The five elements are plot, setting, theme, point of view, and characters. How many did you remember?

Leo: Question three. What do we call the main character of a story, and what do we call the character who creates conflict?

Maya: The main character is the protagonist, and the character who creates conflict is the antagonist.

Leo: Perfect score? Then you really know your novels.

Maya: That's all for today. Keep reading, keep learning, one word at a time.

Leo: See you next time on The World, Word by Word!

LeisureEpisode 557:05

Anne Frank: The Diary of a Young Girl

Learn how Anne Frank wrote through two years in hiding, how her diary survived, and why her words became a lasting testament read around the world.

Full episode

7:05 · Story, knowledge, and vocabulary

Download full episode

Words in This Episode

2:06 · Slow vocabulary review

Download words review
Words in this episode
diary
a book where you write down your private thoughts and feelings each day“Anne wrote in her diary almost every day while she was in hiding.”
hide
to go to a secret place so that other people cannot find you“the Frank family hid in secret rooms at the back of an office building.”
concentration camp
a prison camp where many people are kept in terrible conditions“Anne and her sister were sent to the Bergen-Belsen concentration camp.”
annex
a small extra part of a building, joined to a larger one“the Franks hid in a secret annex hidden behind a movable bookcase.”
persecute
to treat a group of people unfairly or cruelly, often because of their religion“the Nazis persecuted Jewish people in Germany and in the countries they controlled.”
betray
to give away someone's secrets to their enemies and hurt them“after two years in hiding, the family was betrayed and arrested.”
liberate
to free people from being controlled or held“the Bergen-Belsen camp was liberated by British soldiers in nineteen forty-five, just weeks after Anne died.”

to print and sell a book so the public can read it“Otto Frank published Anne's diary in nineteen forty-seven.”

something that clearly proves or shows an important truth“Anne's diary is a testament to the strength of the human spirit.”
legacy
something important that a person leaves behind after they die“Anne Frank's legacy lives on in her diary, which is read all over the world.”
Transcript

Maya: Welcome back to The World, Word by Word. I'm Maya.

Leo: And I'm Leo. Today we're diving into one of the most famous books in the world, a real diary written by a teenage girl during World War Two.

Maya: The book is called The Diary of a Young Girl. The girl was Anne Frank, a young Jewish girl who lived in the Netherlands.

Leo: Wait, a real diary? This isn't a story someone made up?

Maya: No, it is real. Anne wrote down her private thoughts and feelings almost every day while she was in hiding from the Nazis. Her diary was published after she died, and it has been translated into more than seventy languages.

Leo: Let's start from the beginning. Who was Anne Frank?

Maya: Anne was born on June twelfth, nineteen twenty-nine, in Frankfurt, Germany. She lived with her mother Edith, her father Otto, and her older sister Margot.

Leo: Why did they leave Germany?

Maya: In nineteen thirty-three, Adolf Hitler and the Nazis took power in Germany. They passed laws that took away the rights of Jewish people. Otto and Edith saw that Jewish life was no longer safe, so the family moved to Amsterdam in the Netherlands.

Leo: Did they find safety there?

Maya: Only for a few years. In nineteen forty, Germany attacked the Netherlands and took control of it. Soon the Nazis began to persecute the Jews there too.

Leo: What did the Frank family do?

Maya: In July of nineteen forty-two, they went into hiding. They moved into a secret annex, a set of hidden rooms at the back of Otto Frank's office building in Amsterdam. The entrance was hidden behind a movable bookcase.

Leo: How many people hid there?

Maya: Eight people in total. Anne was just thirteen years old. It was actually for her thirteenth birthday, in June of nineteen forty-two, that Anne received the diary she would become famous for.

Leo: How did they survive in hiding? They couldn't go out and buy food.

Maya: Brave helpers, including Otto's employee Miep Gies, brought them food and supplies. It was very dangerous work. If the helpers had been caught, they could have been punished too.

Leo: What was life like inside the annex?

Maya: Very difficult. The people in hiding had to stay quiet during the day, because workers were in the office downstairs. The windows were covered, so no one could see in. They could not flush the toilet during the day. They were scared, but Anne kept writing.

Leo: What did she write about?

Maya: Everything. Her fears, her hopes, her family, and her dreams. She even wrote that she wanted to become a famous writer or journalist one day. She heard a radio message asking people to keep records of life under the Nazis, and she began to edit her diary, hoping it would be published.

Leo: Did the war end before they were found?

Maya: Sadly, no. They hid for about two years. Then, on August fourth, nineteen forty-four, someone betrayed them to the Nazis. The police arrived, and all the people in hiding were arrested and sent to concentration camps.

Leo: What happened to Anne?

Maya: Anne and her sister Margot were sent to Bergen-Belsen, a camp in Germany. Both sisters caught typhus, a serious illness, and died there in early nineteen forty-five. Anne was only fifteen. The camp was liberated just weeks later.

Leo: That is heartbreaking. What about her father?

Maya: Otto Frank was the only one of the eight people in hiding to survive the war. After he returned to Amsterdam, Miep Gies gave him Anne's diary. She had picked it up from the floor of the annex right after the arrest and kept it safe, hoping to return it to Anne.

Leo: And then Otto published it?

Maya: Yes. At first he was not sure it was the right thing to do, but he wanted to fulfill his daughter's wish. The diary was published in the Netherlands in nineteen forty-seven under the title Het Achterhuis, which means The Secret Annex, the name Anne herself had chosen.

Leo: When did the rest of the world read it?

Maya: In nineteen fifty-two, it was published in English as Anne Frank: The Diary of a Young Girl. It became a huge success. A stage play based on the diary won the Pulitzer Prize in nineteen fifty-five, and a movie came out in nineteen fifty-nine. Today the secret annex is a museum called the Anne Frank House, and millions of people visit it.

Leo: Anne's story is sad, but her diary keeps her memory alive.

Maya: Exactly. Many people call it a testament to the strength of the human spirit. Now, let's shine the spotlight on our ten vocabulary words.

Maya: Word one is diary, which means a book where you write down your private thoughts and feelings each day. Anne wrote in her diary almost every day while she was in hiding.

Leo: Word two is hide, which means to go to a secret place so that other people cannot find you. The Frank family hid in secret rooms at the back of an office building.

Maya: Word three is concentration camp, which means a prison camp where many people are kept in terrible conditions. Anne and her sister were sent to the Bergen-Belsen concentration camp.

Leo: Word four is annex, which means a small extra part of a building, joined to a larger one. The Franks hid in a secret annex hidden behind a movable bookcase.

Maya: Word five is persecute, which means to treat a group of people unfairly or cruelly, often because of their religion. The Nazis persecuted Jewish people in Germany and in the countries they controlled.

Leo: Word six is betray, which means to give away someone's secrets to their enemies and hurt them. After two years in hiding, the family was betrayed and arrested.

Maya: Word seven is liberate, which means to free people from being controlled or held. The Bergen-Belsen camp was liberated by British soldiers in nineteen forty-five, just weeks after Anne died.

Leo: Word eight is publish, which means to print and sell a book so the public can read it. Otto Frank published Anne's diary in nineteen forty-seven.

Maya: Word nine is testament, which means something that clearly proves or shows an important truth. Anne's diary is a testament to the strength of the human spirit.

Leo: And word ten is legacy, which means something important that a person leaves behind after they die. Anne Frank's legacy lives on in her diary, which is read all over the world.

Maya: Great work on those words.

Maya: Now it's quiz time. Three questions to see what you remember. Question one. Where did Anne Frank and her family hide from the Nazis?

Leo: Think back to the secret rooms we talked about.

Maya: They hid in a secret annex, a set of hidden rooms at the back of Otto Frank's office building in Amsterdam. The entrance was hidden behind a movable bookcase.

Leo: Question two. Who saved Anne's diary after the arrest and gave it to her father after the war?

Maya: That was Miep Gies, one of Otto Frank's brave helpers. She picked up the diary from the floor of the annex and kept it safe in her desk.

Leo: Question three. When was Anne's diary first published?

Maya: It was first published in the Netherlands in nineteen forty-seven, with the title Het Achterhuis, meaning The Secret Annex. The English version followed in nineteen fifty-two.

Leo: Three for three? Nice job.

Maya: Before we go, remember what we learned. Anne Frank's diary shows us one family's courage in a dark time in history. Reading it helps us understand why treating all people with kindness matters.

Leo: Thanks for listening to The World, Word by Word. I'm Leo.

Maya: And I'm Maya. Keep reading, keep learning, word by word.

Oprah Winfrey

How did a girl from a tiny Mississippi town become one of the most influential women on the planet? Maya and Leo trace Oprah Winfrey's journey — from reading books at her grandmother's house, to a speaking scholarship, to twenty-five years of The Oprah Winfrey Show, plus ten media words and a quick quiz.

8:03 · Story, knowledge, and vocabulary

Words in This Episode

Words in this episode

Transcript

Maya: Here's a question for you, Leo. Who do you think is the most powerful woman on television?

Leo: The most powerful woman on television? That's a big claim. Who is it?

Maya: Oprah Winfrey. She hosted a talk show for twenty-five years, became a billionaire, acted in movies, and even won a medal from a president. Today, we're telling the story of how she got there.

Leo: I've heard her name everywhere. Where did she start?

Maya: In a very small town called Kosciusko, Mississippi. Oprah was born there on January twenty-ninth, nineteen fifty-four. Her parents were not married and soon went their separate ways, so baby Oprah was left in the care of her grandmother.

Leo: Her grandmother raised her?

Maya: Yes, in the early years. And her grandmother taught her to read at a very young age. Oprah says that through reading, she discovered how many possibilities the world held.

Leo: So books opened the door for her, even as a little kid.

Maya: Exactly. But her childhood was not easy. At age six, she moved to Milwaukee, Wisconsin, to live with her mother. The family had little money and lived in a poor neighborhood with a lot of crime. Her mother struggled to support the family and had little time to watch over the children.

Leo: That sounds really hard.

Maya: It was. Then, at twelve, Oprah moved to Nashville, Tennessee, to live with her father. She says her father made her feel happy and safe. She started giving speeches in churches and at social events, and she was so good that people soon paid her to speak.

Leo: Wait, people paid a teenager to give speeches? That's incredible.

Maya: That's right. And her father pushed her to do well in school. Her excellent performance in a speaking contest won her a full scholarship to Tennessee State University, which means she could go to college for free.

Leo: A full scholarship just for public speaking. That changed everything.

Maya: It opened every door. While she was still in college, a local C B S station offered her a job co-anchoring the evening news. After she graduated, she worked at a television station in Baltimore, and in nineteen seventy-seven, she moved to a morning talk show called People Are Talking.

Leo: And let me guess, the ratings went up?

Maya: Way up. Because of Oprah's popularity, the show's ratings soared. Then, in nineteen eighty-four, she got the offer that changed her life: Chicago, Illinois, and a morning talk show called A M Chicago. At the time, it had low ratings and mostly covered traditional women's topics.

Leo: So what did she do differently?

Maya: She changed the whole focus, talking about news and controversial topics that mattered to people. The ratings climbed fast. Then, in nineteen eighty-five, the show was expanded to a full hour and renamed The Oprah Winfrey Show. It was broadcast on one hundred twenty channels across the country, with an audience of ten million people.

Leo: Ten million viewers! How much money did that make?

Maya: In its very first year, the show earned one hundred twenty-five million dollars, and thirty million of that was paid to Oprah herself.

Leo: Thirty million dollars in one year. That is unbelievable.

Maya: And she didn't stop there. In nineteen ninety-six, she started Oprah's Book Club on the show. Each month she picked a book and discussed it on air. Most of the books she chose became best-sellers, which gave her real power in the publishing world.

Leo: So authors must have been lining up to get picked by her.

Maya: They were. The Oprah Winfrey Show ran for twenty-five years, from nineteen eighty-six to twenty eleven. It won Emmy Awards and became the highest-rated talk show in the United States.

Leo: But she also acted, right? I remember her in a movie.

Maya: Yes. In nineteen eighty-five, the music producer Quincy Jones saw her on television and wanted her for a film he was making with Steven Spielberg. The film was The Color Purple, based on Alice Walker's novel. Oprah was nominated for an Academy Award for playing Sofia, a strong woman who stands up to her husband.

Leo: An Oscar nomination for her first big movie role. Not bad at all.

Maya: Not bad at all. After that, she started her own production company, Harpo Productions. Harpo is just Oprah spelled backwards.

Leo: That's clever. So she could make her own movies and shows?

Maya: Exactly. She produced and starred in many works, like The Women of Brewster Place in nineteen eighty-nine and Beloved in nineteen ninety-eight, based on Toni Morrison's famous novel. She later helped produce Selma, a movie about Martin Luther King Junior, and played Mrs. Which in the twenty eighteen film A Wrinkle in Time.

Leo: She's done a bit of everything. What about the billionaire part?

Maya: In two thousand three, Oprah became a billionaire, the first Black female billionaire in the world. She's known for giving a lot of it away. That kind of charity work is called philanthropy, and she even founded an organization called Oprah's Angels.

Leo: And the medal from the president?

Maya: In twenty thirteen, President Barack Obama gave her the Presidential Medal of Freedom, one of the highest honors an American citizen can receive.

Leo: Even after her talk show ended, she kept interviewing people, right?

Maya: Yes, in a smaller way. In twenty eighteen, she interviewed former first lady Michelle Obama about her memoir, Becoming. And in twenty twenty-one, she made headlines around the world with her interview of Prince Harry and Meghan Markle. It was the couple's first interview after they stepped back from royal duties.

Leo: I remember that one. It was everywhere.

Maya: It was. Now, Oprah has a famous saying: "Be thankful for what you have; you'll end up having more." That really sums up her story, from a little girl reading books at her grandmother's house to one of the most influential people on the planet.

Maya: Now let's do our vocabulary spotlight. I'll say a word, tell you what it means, and use it in a sentence. Word one is influential, which means having the power to change how people think or act. Oprah is one of the most influential women in the world.

Leo: Word two is philanthropy, which means giving money to help others. Oprah is known for her philanthropy, and she has founded an organization called Oprah's Angels.

Maya: Word three is syndicated, which means a television show is sold to many different channels at once. The Oprah Winfrey Show was nationally syndicated on one hundred twenty channels.

Leo: Word four is ratings, a measure of how many people watch a television show. Because of Oprah's popularity, the show's ratings soared.

Maya: Word five is scholarship, money given to a student to help pay for school. Oprah won a full scholarship to Tennessee State University through a speaking contest.

Leo: Word six is mogul, a person who is important and powerful in business. Oprah is a media mogul who owns her own production company.

Maya: Word seven is venture, a new business project or activity. Starting Harpo Productions was one of Oprah's most successful ventures.

Leo: Word eight is portrayal, the way an actor plays a character in a film or a show. Oprah earned an Oscar nomination for her portrayal of Sofia in The Color Purple.

Maya: Word nine is memoir, a book someone writes about their own life. Oprah interviewed Michelle Obama about her memoir, Becoming.

Leo: And word ten is ghetto, a poor part of a city where many people have little money. As a child, Oprah lived in a ghetto in Milwaukee, Wisconsin.

Maya: Great work on those words.

Maya: Now a quick quiz. Question one. How long did The Oprah Winfrey Show run, twenty-five years or ten years?

Leo: Listeners, think for a moment. The answer is twenty-five years, from nineteen eighty-six to twenty eleven.

Maya: Question two. True or false. Oprah's production company is called Harpo because it is Oprah spelled backwards.

Leo: Think it through. True. Harpo is just Oprah spelled backwards.

Maya: Last one. Oprah became a billionaire in two thousand three. Was she the first Black female billionaire in the world, true or false?

Leo: Take a guess. True. She was the first Black female billionaire in the world.

Maya: You did great. So remember, Oprah grew up poor in Mississippi, won a speaking scholarship that changed her life, built the highest-rated talk show in America, and became a media mogul, an actress, and a philanthropist. See you next time on The World, Word by Word.

Michael Jackson

Who was the King of Pop? Maya and Leo trace Michael Jackson's path from the youngest member of the Jackson 5 to Thriller, the best-selling album of all time, plus the story of his signature moonwalk — with ten music words and a quick quiz.

Transcript

Maya: Here's a question for you, Leo. Who do you think of when you hear the words "King of Pop"?

Leo: King of Pop? That's Michael Jackson, right?

Maya: Right. Today we're talking about Michael Jackson, one of the most successful music artists who ever lived.

Leo: Where did his story start?

Maya: In Gary, Indiana. Michael was born on August twenty-ninth, nineteen fifty-eight. He was the youngest son of Joe and Katherine Jackson. His father wanted his sons to become music stars.

Leo: Did Michael start singing when he was very young?

Maya: Very young. His older brothers Tito, Jermaine, and Jackie were already in a band, and Michael joined them when he was just five years old. Later, his brother Marlon joined too, and they became the Jackson 5.

Leo: Five kids in one band. How did they get famous?

Maya: They started out playing local shows, but they were so good that they signed a deal with Motown, a famous record label, in nineteen sixty-nine. Their first album, Diana Ross Presents the Jackson 5, came out that same year.

Leo: And they had hits right away?

Maya: Yes. Songs like "A B C" and "I'll Be There" became big hits. In nineteen seventy-six, after they left Motown, the group changed its name to The Jacksons.

Leo: So when did Michael go solo?

Maya: When he was just thirteen. His first solo album, Got to Be There, came out in nineteen seventy-two. While he was still in The Jacksons, he released Off the Wall in nineteen seventy-nine, with the hit song "Don't Stop til You Get Enough." That album sold more than twenty million copies.

Leo: But his biggest album was Thriller, right?

Maya: Absolutely. In nineteen eighty-two, Michael released Thriller. It had chart-toppers like "Beat It" and "Billie Jean." Thriller became the best-selling album of all time, and it won Michael eight Grammy Awards.

Leo: Eight Grammys for one album? That's amazing.

Maya: It really was. The title song "Thriller" also became famous for its music video, which was like a short horror movie with amazing dancing.

Leo: What came next?

Maya: His next album, Bad, came out in nineteen eighty-seven, with songs like "Man in the Mirror" and "The Way You Make Me Feel." Then Dangerous arrived in nineteen ninety-one, with the song "Black or White." In between, in nineteen eighty-five, he co-wrote "We Are the World" with Lionel Richie to raise money for people in Africa who needed help.

Leo: Did he do big concerts too?

Maya: He was a huge live performer. In nineteen ninety-three, he even starred in the Super Bowl halftime show.

Leo: I have to ask about the dancing. What was the moonwalk?

Maya: The moonwalk was Michael's most famous dance move. When he did it, he slid backward across the stage while looking like he was stepping forward. He first did it in nineteen eighty-three, during a television special when he performed "Billie Jean."

Leo: So people went crazy for it?

Maya: They did. The moonwalk became his signature move, and fans copied it all over the world.

Leo: His life wasn't all happy, though, was it?

Maya: No. As he got more famous, his private life often made the news. He built a huge estate in California called Neverland Ranch, with wild animals and amusement park rides. He also faced legal trouble, but he was found not guilty at a trial in two thousand five.

Leo: How did his story end?

Maya: In two thousand nine, Michael was planning a big comeback tour called "This Is It." But on June twenty-fifth, while he was still preparing, he died suddenly at the age of fifty. A movie made from his rehearsal footage was released that October, and it was a big hit.

Leo: Even after he was gone, people still loved his music.

Maya: Exactly. Now let's shine the spotlight on our ten vocabulary words.

Maya: Now let's do our vocabulary spotlight. I'll say a word, tell you what it means, and use it in a sentence. Word one is album, which means a collection of musical recordings. Thriller is the best-selling album of all time.

Leo: Word two is solo, which means done by a single performer instead of a group. Michael started his solo career when he was just thirteen.

Maya: Word three is record label, which means a company that produces and sells music. The Jackson 5 signed a deal with the record label Motown in nineteen sixty-nine.

Leo: Word four is chart-topper, which means a song or album that reaches the top of the music charts. "Billie Jean" and "Beat It" were chart-toppers from Thriller.

Maya: Word five is moonwalk, which means a famous dance move where you slide backward while looking like you are stepping forward. Michael first performed the moonwalk on television in nineteen eighty-three.

Leo: Word six is Grammy Award, which means an award given for an achievement in the recording industry. Thriller won Michael eight Grammy Awards.

Maya: Word seven is estate, which means a large property with a big house and land. Michael built a huge estate in California called Neverland Ranch.

Leo: Word eight is comeback, which means a return to success after a break or a difficult time. Michael was planning a comeback tour called "This Is It."

Maya: Word nine is signature, which means a special style or move that people strongly associate with a person. The moonwalk was Michael's signature dance move.

Leo: And word ten is breakout, which means a sudden rise to great success. Thriller was Michael's true breakout as a solo superstar.

Maya: Great work on those words.

Maya: Now a quick quiz. Question one. Which Michael Jackson album became the best-selling album of all time, Thriller or Bad?

Leo: Listeners, think for a moment. The answer is Thriller.

Maya: Question two. True or false. Michael Jackson joined the Jackson 5 when he was twenty years old.

Leo: Think it through. False. He was just five years old, the youngest member of the band.

Maya: Last one. Michael Jackson's famous dance move, the moonwalk, was first performed on, a Super Bowl halftime show, a nineteen eighty-three television special, or a movie set?

Leo: Take a guess. It was a nineteen eighty-three television special, where he performed "Billie Jean."

Maya: You did great. So remember, Michael Jackson went from the youngest member of the Jackson 5 to the King of Pop, Thriller became the best-selling album of all time, and his moonwalk made him famous for dancing too. See you next time on The World, Word by Word.

LeisureEpisode 527:38

A Wrinkle in Time

Who is Meg Murry, and what is a wrinkle in time? Maya and Leo follow Meg, Charles Wallace, and Calvin across the universe to the planet Camazotz, where a giant brain called IT controls everyone — plus ten book words and a quick quiz.

Full episode

7:38 · Story, knowledge, and vocabulary

Download full episode

Words in This Episode

1:57 · Slow vocabulary review

Download words review
Words in this episode
celestial
relating to outer space“Mrs Whatsit is a celestial being who travels between the stars.”
novel
a long book that tells a story“A Wrinkle in Time is a novel that readers have loved for more than sixty years.”
disembodied
coming from a body that cannot be seen“IT is a disembodied brain, a mind with no body at all.”
telepathic
sharing thoughts without speaking“IT tries to control Meg's mind in a telepathic way, without saying a word.”

caring about other people's pain and wanting to help them“Meg shows great compassion for her little brother.”

being different from everyone else in your own special way“the book teaches readers to value their individuality.”

a movie or play made from a book“the two thousand eighteen film is an adaptation of L Engle's novel.”

a prize given for doing something great“the novel won the Newbery Medal, a famous award for children's books.”

a person who writes books“Madeleine L Engle is the author of more than fifty books.”
Transcript

Maya: Here's a question for you, Leo. If you could fold space itself like a sheet of paper, so that two faraway places touched each other, how fast could you travel?

Leo: That fast? I'd just step from one place to the other, instantly, with no traveling at all.

Maya: That idea is at the heart of today's episode. We're talking about A Wrinkle in Time, a famous fantasy novel for young readers.

Leo: A wrinkle in time? What does that even mean?

Maya: In the book, a "wrinkle" is a shortcut through space and time. Instead of traveling across the universe the normal way, you fold space and tesser, which means you jump from one point to another in no time at all.

Leo: Tesser? Is that a real word?

Maya: Not in real life. The author made it up for the book. The story is by American author Madeleine L Engle, and it was first published in nineteen sixty-two.

Leo: So who's the hero of the story?

Maya: A teenage girl named Meg Murry. Meg is awkward at school and gets bullied for her looks. Her father, a scientist, has been missing for more than a year, and nobody knows what happened to him.

Leo: That's a sad start. Who helps her?

Maya: One stormy night, a strange woman named Mrs Whatsit shows up at the Murry house. Meg thinks she's a homeless person, but Mrs Whatsit turns out to be a celestial being, a traveler from outer space who only looks human.

Leo: A celestial being? Like an alien?

Maya: Sort of, but a friendly and powerful one. Mrs Whatsit reads Meg's mind to prove what she can do. Then she introduces Meg to two more celestial friends, Mrs Who and Mrs Which.

Leo: Three mysterious Mrses. What do they tell Meg?

Maya: They tell her that her father used a wrinkle in time to travel across space, and that he ran into an evil force called the Dark Thing. The Dark Thing took him prisoner on a faraway planet called Camazotz.

Leo: So Meg has to go rescue him?

Maya: Yes. Meg, her five-year-old brother Charles Wallace, and their friend Calvin travel through the wrinkle to Camazotz. Mrs Whatsit warns them to stay together at all times.

Leo: What is Camazotz like?

Maya: It's a terrifying place. The whole planet is controlled by a giant disembodied brain called IT. Disembodied means it has no body you can see, just a pulsing brain. Everyone on the planet thinks and acts exactly the same, like robots.

Leo: That sounds creepy. Does everyone stay safe?

Maya: No. Charles Wallace is amazingly smart for a five-year-old, but even he cannot fight IT. He falls under IT's control, and then he leads Meg and Calvin straight to IT, which tries to control their minds telepathically, without a single spoken word.

Leo: Yikes. How do they escape?

Maya: At the last minute, Meg's father appears and tessers them away to a nearby planet, but poor Charles Wallace is left behind, still under IT's spell. And during the escape, Meg is hurt by the Black Thing and becomes paralyzed. Strange furry creatures with no eyes nurse her back to health.

Leo: That is a wild adventure. Does Meg go back for her brother?

Maya: She does. The three Mrses tell her that only she can save Charles Wallace, because she has one thing IT does not have. And that thing turns out to be love. Meg uses her love for her brother to break IT's hold on him.

Leo: Love beats an evil giant brain? That's a pretty powerful message.

Maya: It is. And it's one of the book's big themes. The novel is really about the struggle between good and evil. Love and compassion are the qualities of good, while selfishness and the desire to control others are the qualities of evil.

Leo: And the planet where everyone acts the same, that's about being yourself?

Maya: Exactly. Another big theme is individuality. Meg spends the whole book wishing she could fit in, but on Camazotz she sees how horrible it is when everyone is identical. She learns to value what makes her different.

Leo: What about the author? Did the book do well right away?

Maya: Not at first. The manuscript was rejected twenty-six times before a publisher finally said yes in nineteen sixty-two. Then it became a bestseller, won the Newbery Medal in nineteen sixty-three, and has never gone out of print.

Leo: Twenty-six rejections? She really didn't give up.

Maya: She didn't. L Engle was born in New York in nineteen eighteen. Her father was a writer and her mother was a pianist, so she grew up around creative people. She wrote her first book in nineteen forty-five, and before she passed away in two thousand seven, she published more than fifty books.

Leo: And there's a movie too, right?

Maya: Yes, a film adaptation came out in two thousand eighteen, directed by Ava DuVernay. It starred Storm Reid as Meg, with Oprah Winfrey, Reese Witherspoon, and Chris Pine.

Leo: Oprah Winfrey in a fantasy movie? That's cool.

Maya: One more fun fact. The book's ideas about time travel were inspired by the writings of Albert Einstein. So there is real science hiding inside the fantasy.

Maya: Now let's do our vocabulary spotlight. I'll say a word, tell you what it means, and use it in a sentence. Word one is celestial, which means relating to outer space. Mrs Whatsit is a celestial being who travels between the stars.

Leo: Word two is novel, which means a long book that tells a story. A Wrinkle in Time is a novel that readers have loved for more than sixty years.

Maya: Word three is disembodied, which means coming from a body that cannot be seen. IT is a disembodied brain, a mind with no body at all.

Leo: Word four is telepathic, which means sharing thoughts without speaking. IT tries to control Meg's mind in a telepathic way, without saying a word.

Maya: Word five is compassion, which means caring about other people's pain and wanting to help them. Meg shows great compassion for her little brother.

Leo: Word six is individuality, which means being different from everyone else in your own special way. The book teaches readers to value their individuality.

Maya: Word seven is adaptation, which means a movie or play made from a book. The two thousand eighteen film is an adaptation of L Engle's novel.

Leo: Word eight is controversy, which means strong public disagreement about something. The magic in the book caused controversy among some parents.

Maya: Word nine is award, which means a prize given for doing something great. The novel won the Newbery Medal, a famous award for children's books.

Leo: And word ten is author, which means a person who writes books. Madeleine L Engle is the author of more than fifty books.

Maya: Great work on those words.

Maya: Now a quick quiz. Question one. In what year was A Wrinkle in Time first published, nineteen sixty-two or nineteen seventy-two?

Leo: Listeners, think for a moment. The answer is nineteen sixty-two, after twenty-six rejections.

Maya: Question two. True or false. IT is a giant disembodied brain that controls the planet Camazotz.

Leo: Think it through. True. IT keeps everyone on Camazotz thinking and acting exactly the same.

Maya: Last one. What power helps Meg defeat IT and free her brother, love, magic spells, or super speed?

Leo: Take a guess. It is love. Meg's love for Charles Wallace is the one thing IT does not have.

Maya: You did great. So remember, A Wrinkle in Time is a nineteen sixty-two novel by Madeleine L Engle about a girl who tessers across the universe to rescue her father, the giant brain IT stands for the evil of conformity and control, and in the end, love and individuality win. See you next time on The World, Word by Word.
