LeisureEpisode 518:02

The Adventures of Tom Sawyer

Follow Tom from the famous whitewashed fence to a graveyard mystery, a courtroom confession, a cave escape, and a hidden box of treasure.

Full episode

8:02 · Story, knowledge, and vocabulary

Download full episode

Words in This Episode

1:52 · Slow vocabulary review

Download words review
Words in this episode
novel
It means a long, fictional story written as a book“The Adventures of Tom Sawyer is one of the most famous novels in American literature.”
mischievous
It means playful in a way that causes trouble“the mischievous boys skipped school to go fishing instead.”
orphan
It means a child whose parents have died“Tom is an orphan who lives with his aunt.”
trinket
It means a small object of little value, like a marble or a toy“the boys traded Tom their trinkets for a turn with the paintbrush.”
vagrant
It means a person without a settled home“Huckleberry Finn is a vagrant boy who lives free and answers to no one.”
witness
It means to see something happen with your own eyes“Tom and Huck witness a murder in the graveyard.”
oath
It means a solemn promise“the boys swear an oath never to tell anyone what they saw.”
scripture
It means religious texts, like the Bible“Tom wins the prize Bible even though he knows almost nothing about scripture.”
hypocrisy
It means claiming to follow moral standards but not actually following them“Twain uses the novel to point out the hypocrisy of the town's adults.”
treasure
It means valuable objects, like gold or jewels“the boys find a box of treasure hidden in the cave.”
Transcript

Maya: Leo, if you were punished with a boring chore, would you trick your friends into doing it for you?

Leo: Trick my friends into doing my chore? That sounds like the opposite of fair. But maybe if I made it look really fun.

Maya: That is exactly what Tom Sawyer does in today's story. We are talking about The Adventures of Tom Sawyer, a novel by the American author Mark Twain, published in eighteen seventy-six.

Leo: Mark Twain. Wasn't that just his pen name?

Maya: Right. His real name was Samuel Langhorne Clemens. He was born in Missouri in eighteen thirty-five. Before he became a famous writer, he worked as a printer, a riverboat pilot, and even a silver miner.

Leo: A riverboat pilot? On the Mississippi River?

Maya: Yes. That experience gave him the setting for this book. The story takes place in a small town called St. Petersburg, on the Mississippi River. Twain based it on his own childhood home of Hannibal, Missouri. He even admitted that parts of the novel are autobiographical, meaning they are based on his own life.

Leo: So Tom Sawyer is really about Twain as a boy? Who is Tom, then?

Maya: Tom is an orphan, a boy whose parents have died. He lives with his aunt in that small Missouri town. He is restless, mischievous, and always skipping school to go on adventures.

Leo: Mischievous meaning he loves causing trouble in a playful way?

Maya: Exactly, and you will learn more about that word later. Now, the famous fence scene. Tom's aunt catches him misbehaving and punishes him by making him whitewash her long fence. Tom hates the job, but then he gets an idea. He pretends that painting the fence is the most fun, most special thing in the world.

Leo: And the other boys believe him?

Maya: They do. He tricks them so well that they actually pay him trinkets, little objects like marbles and toys, just for the chance to paint the fence for him. By the end of the day, the fence has three coats of paint, and Tom has collected a pile of treasures.

Leo: That is brilliant. And a little evil.

Maya: Tom is clever, that is for sure. But he is not always so smart. At Sunday school, he wins a prize Bible by collecting tickets, even though he knows almost nothing about scripture, the religious texts. He does it just to impress Becky Thatcher, the judge's daughter, who he has a crush on.

Leo: Did impressing her work?

Maya: Not for long. The judge asks Tom some simple questions about the Bible, and Tom cannot answer a single one. Total embarrassment.

Leo: Ouch. And Becky stops liking him?

Maya: She does for a while, until Tom wins her back by taking the blame and the punishment for something she did. Tom's heart is in the right place, even if his plans go wrong.

Maya: Then the story turns serious. Tom's best friend is Huckleberry Finn, a vagrant boy, meaning he has no settled home. Huck is the son of the town drunk, but he lives free and does whatever he wants. One night, Tom and Huck are in the graveyard when they witness a murder. They watch a grave robber named Injun Joe kill a man named Dr. Robinson.

Leo: They saw a real murder? What do they do?

Maya: They are terrified. They swear an oath, a serious promise, never to tell anyone what they saw. Because here is the twist. Injun Joe frames Muff Potter, another man who was passed out drunk at the scene, for the murder. The town believes Injun Joe.

Leo: That is awful. An innocent man about to be punished.

Maya: Tom cannot live with it. When Muff Potter goes to trial, Tom stands up in court and tells the truth. He points at Injun Joe as the real killer. Injun Joe escapes through a window before anyone can catch him, and Tom and Huck spend the summer scared of his revenge.

Leo: So Tom finds his courage and does the right thing. That is real growth.

Maya: It is. And the adventure is not over. Tom and Huck overhear Injun Joe talking about hidden gold in an old house. Later, during a picnic, Tom and Becky get lost in the local caves. Becky almost dies of dehydration, from losing too much water, before Tom finally finds a way out.

Leo: And Injun Joe is hiding in those same caves?

Maya: Yes. To keep everyone safe, the judge has the cave sealed shut. Tom is shocked when he hears this, because he knows Injun Joe is still inside. When the cave is reopened, they find Injun Joe's body. Tom guesses the treasure must be hidden in the cave too, and the boys find a box of gold.

Leo: A box of gold. They get rich?

Maya: They split it between them. Huck gets adopted by the widow he once saved, and even though he hates the tidy, refined life at first, he slowly accepts it, with Tom's help.

Leo: What is the bigger meaning of the book, beyond the adventures?

Maya: Twain uses Tom's adventures to comment on society. One theme is coming of age. Tom starts out selfish and undisciplined, but he learns to put others first, like when he saves Muff Potter and Becky. The other theme is hypocrisy, when people claim to follow moral standards but do not actually follow them. Twain shows that the adults in town are often more childish than the children.

Leo: The book was not popular at first, right?

Maya: Right. It was not commercially successful when it came out in eighteen seventy-six, but it later became one of Twain's best-selling works and a classic of American literature. Its sequel, The Adventures of Huckleberry Finn, came out in eighteen eighty-four.

Maya: Now let's do our vocabulary spotlight. I'll say a word, tell you what it means, and use it in a sentence. Word one is novel, which means a long, fictional story written as a book. The Adventures of Tom Sawyer is one of the most famous novels in American literature.

Leo: Word two is mischievous, which means playful in a way that causes trouble. The mischievous boys skipped school to go fishing instead.

Maya: Word three is orphan, which means a child whose parents have died. Tom is an orphan who lives with his aunt.

Leo: Word four is trinket, which means a small object of little value, like a marble or a toy. The boys traded Tom their trinkets for a turn with the paintbrush.

Maya: Word five is vagrant, which means a person without a settled home. Huckleberry Finn is a vagrant boy who lives free and answers to no one.

Leo: Word six is witness, which means to see something happen with your own eyes. Tom and Huck witness a murder in the graveyard.

Maya: Word seven is oath, which means a solemn promise. The boys swear an oath never to tell anyone what they saw.

Leo: Word eight is scripture, which means religious texts, like the Bible. Tom wins the prize Bible even though he knows almost nothing about scripture.

Maya: Word nine is hypocrisy, which means claiming to follow moral standards but not actually following them. Twain uses the novel to point out the hypocrisy of the town's adults.

Leo: And word ten is treasure, which means valuable objects, like gold or jewels. The boys find a box of treasure hidden in the cave.

Maya: Great work on those words.

Maya: Now a quick quiz. Question one. Tom gets punished and has to paint a fence. What do the other boys give him in exchange for painting it? Do they give him money, or trinkets like marbles and toys?

Leo: Listeners, think for a moment. The answer is trinkets like marbles and toys. Tom pretends the job is fun, and the boys pay him for the privilege.

Maya: Question two. True or false. At Muff Potter's trial, Tom stays silent because he swore an oath never to tell.

Leo: Think it through. False. Tom breaks his oath and tells the truth in court, pointing at Injun Joe as the real murderer.

Maya: Last one. Twain based the fictional town of St. Petersburg on, his childhood home of Hannibal, Missouri, or a town he read about in a travel book?

Leo: Take a guess. His childhood home of Hannibal, Missouri. He even admitted that parts of the novel are autobiographical.

Maya: You did great. So remember, Tom Sawyer is a mischievous orphan on the Mississippi River who tricks his friends into painting a fence, stands up for an innocent man in court, and finds a box of gold in a cave. Twain uses his adventures to show a boy growing up, and to poke fun at the hypocrisy of adults. See you next time on The World, Word by Word.

Autism Spectrum Disorder

What is autism spectrum disorder? Maya and Leo explore why it's called a spectrum, the two groups of symptoms, when signs appear, the vaccine myth scientists refuted, the history from 1911 to today, and why early intervention matters most — plus ten words and a quick quiz.

Download full episode

Words in This Episode

2:23 · Slow vocabulary review

Words in this episode
spectrum
a wide range of how something can appear, from mild to severe“autism is called a spectrum because its symptoms can be very mild or very severe.”
developmental disorder
a condition that affects how a child grows, learns, or develops new skills“autism is a developmental disorder that first appears in early childhood.”
social cue
a signal in someone's face, body, or voice that shows what they feel or mean“children with autism may have trouble reading social cues like a smile or a frown.”
diagnose
to identify an illness or condition by studying its symptoms“a psychologist can diagnose autism by carefully watching a child's behavior.”
regress
to lose skills one had learned before and move back to an earlier stage“some toddlers with autism regress and lose words they had already learned to say.”
intervention
an action taken to improve a situation“early intervention, like speech therapy, can make a real difference for young children with autism.”
stereotyped behavior
a repeated movement that does not fit the situation“hand flapping is an example of stereotyped behavior.”
stimulus
something in the surroundings that the senses pick up, like a sound, a light, or a touch“a loud room full of conversations can be an overwhelming stimulus for a child with autism.”
refute
to prove that a claim or belief is wrong“scientists have refuted the idea that vaccines cause autism.”
routine
a set of actions done in the same order every time“treatment programs give children a predictable routine so each day feels safe and clear.”
Transcript

Maya: Leo, quick question to start. What do you already know about autism?

Leo: I know it has something to do with how the brain works, and that kids on the autism spectrum see the world a little differently. That's about it.

Maya: That is a solid start. Autism spectrum disorder, or A S D, is a developmental disorder that first appears in early childhood. It affects how people communicate and interact with the world around them.

Leo: Why do they call it a "spectrum"? Like a rainbow?

Maya: Sort of like a rainbow, yes. A spectrum means a wide range. Some people with mild autism function very well in everyday life, showing very few signs of the condition. Others cannot take care of themselves and need constant support.

Leo: So one word covers a lot of different experiences.

Maya: Exactly. Now, the symptoms usually fall into two groups. The first group is communication. Many children with autism have trouble with body language. They may avoid eye contact, struggle to read facial expressions, or miss the change in someone's tone of voice that shows emotion.

Leo: So a friendly joke or a worried look could go completely unnoticed.

Maya: It can. Some children find it hard to start conversations or respond to social cues. And speaking itself can be difficult. In fact, about forty percent of children with autism are unable to speak.

Leo: That's a big number. What's the second group of symptoms?

Maya: Behaviors. This includes very narrow, intense interests, like a single-minded focus on one object, toy, or game. It also includes repeated movements such as hand flapping, spinning coins, lining up objects, or repeating key phrases. And it includes very strict routines and strong sensitivity to things like sounds, lights, or touch.

Leo: Routines? Like needing everything done the exact same way every time?

Maya: Exactly. A child might insist that a small task be done in the same order every time, and get extremely upset if anything changes. And a child who is very sensitive to stimulus might cover their ears and scream when too many conversations are happening in a room.

Leo: When do parents usually notice these signs?

Maya: Usually when the child is between twelve and eighteen months old. Most children with autism develop normally for the first year of life. But over the following months, symptoms appear, and some children even regress, losing words or skills they had already learned.

Leo: That must be frightening for a family. Do scientists know what causes it?

Maya: They are still not certain. Most experts believe it comes from a mix of genetic and environmental factors. Children born to older parents, and siblings of children with autism, are more likely to be diagnosed. And one thing scientists are very clear about. Some people have claimed that vaccines cause autism, but the medical community has entirely refuted that claim, and no study has ever found such a connection.

Leo: Good to hear that settled. How is autism treated?

Maya: It depends on the person. Some people with mild autism need no treatment at all. Others need therapy to learn everyday tasks. The big lesson from research is that early intervention is key. Young children can learn new behaviors more easily than teenagers or adults.

Leo: What does the therapy actually look like?

Maya: Most of it is about teaching new skills and reducing harmful behaviors. Programs use predictable schedules filled with structured activities. Children practice communication step by step and slowly ease their way into more complicated social situations.

Leo: Let's go back in history for a minute. When did people first talk about autism?

Maya: The word "autism" comes from the Greek word for "self." It was first used in nineteen eleven by a doctor named Eugen Bleuler. But back then, he used it to describe patients with a different condition, schizophrenia, who isolated themselves and avoided social contact.

Leo: So when did it become about children?

Maya: Decades later. In nineteen forty-three, an American child psychiatrist named Doctor Leo Kanner published a paper describing the symptoms of eleven children. He called the condition early infantile autism. Then, during the nineteen sixties, psychologists separated autism from schizophrenia completely and recognized it as its own condition.

Leo: Were the early treatments any good?

Maya: Not really. Because nobody understood the cause, doctors tried some extreme things to reduce the symptoms. That included electroshock therapy, the use of pain as a punishment device, and even controlled doses of the drug L S D.

Leo: Whoa. That sounds terrible.

Maya: It was. Thankfully, treatment has come a long, long way since then.

Maya: Now let's do our vocabulary spotlight. I'll say a word, tell you what it means, and use it in a sentence. Word one is spectrum, which means a wide range of how something can appear, from mild to severe. Autism is called a spectrum because its symptoms can be very mild or very severe.

Leo: Word two is developmental disorder, which means a condition that affects how a child grows, learns, or develops new skills. Autism is a developmental disorder that first appears in early childhood.

Maya: Word three is social cue, which means a signal in someone's face, body, or voice that shows what they feel or mean. Children with autism may have trouble reading social cues like a smile or a frown.

Leo: Word four is diagnose, which means to identify an illness or condition by studying its symptoms. A psychologist can diagnose autism by carefully watching a child's behavior.

Maya: Word five is regress, which means to lose skills one had learned before and move back to an earlier stage. Some toddlers with autism regress and lose words they had already learned to say.

Leo: Word six is intervention, which means an action taken to improve a situation. Early intervention, like speech therapy, can make a real difference for young children with autism.

Maya: Word seven is stereotyped behavior, which means a repeated movement that does not fit the situation. Hand flapping is an example of stereotyped behavior.

Leo: Word eight is stimulus, which means something in the surroundings that the senses pick up, like a sound, a light, or a touch. A loud room full of conversations can be an overwhelming stimulus for a child with autism.

Maya: Word nine is refute, which means to prove that a claim or belief is wrong. Scientists have refuted the idea that vaccines cause autism.

Leo: And word ten is routine, which means a set of actions done in the same order every time. Treatment programs give children a predictable routine so each day feels safe and clear.

Maya: Great work on those words.

Maya: Now a quick quiz. Question one. According to what we learned today, autism symptoms usually become visible when a child is between twelve and eighteen months old, or between two and three years old?

Leo: Listeners, think for a moment. The answer is between twelve and eighteen months old.

Maya: Question two. True or false. Scientists have proven that vaccines cause autism.

Leo: Think it through. False. The medical community has refuted that claim, and no study has found such a connection.

Maya: Last one. Who described early infantile autism in nineteen forty-three, Doctor Leo Kanner or Eugen Bleuler?

Leo: Take a guess. It was Doctor Leo Kanner. Bleuler used the word autism much earlier, in nineteen eleven, but for a different condition.

Maya: You did great. So remember, autism spectrum disorder is a developmental disorder that affects communication and behavior, its symptoms form a wide spectrum and usually appear between twelve and eighteen months, vaccines do not cause it, and early intervention helps the most. See you next time on The World, Word by Word.

ScienceEpisode 497:23

Vaccines: How a Tiny Shot Trains Your Immune System

Vaccines have saved millions of lives, from wiping out smallpox to fighting COVID-19. A tiny shot, training your body’s defenders — that’s the story of vaccines.

Full episode

7:23 · Story, knowledge, and vocabulary

Download full episode

Words in This Episode

1:15 · Slow vocabulary review

Download words review
Words in this episode
Vaccine
a medicine that teaches your body how to fight off a disease before you ever get sick“Many children get a flu vaccine every fall to stay healthy.”
Pathogen
anything that can cause disease, like a virus, a bacteria, or a toxin“Washing your hands is a simple way to avoid harmful pathogens.”
Immune system
your body's defense team — the cells that find and destroy harmful germs“Your immune system works every day to protect you from germs.”
Immunity
your body can fight off a disease without getting sick“After the shot, her body had immunity to the disease.”
Antigen
a marker on the surface of a virus or bacteria that triggers your immune system to react“The antigen on the virus told her immune system to attack.”
Contract
to catch an illness or develop it“If you do not wash your hands, you might contract a cold.”
Inactivated
a vaccine made from dead germs that cannot make you sick“An inactivated vaccine uses dead germs, so it cannot make you sick.”
Attenuated
a vaccine made from live germs that have been weakened“The attenuated germs in the vaccine are too weak to make you sick.”
Subunit
A subunit vaccine uses only small pieces of a germ, not the whole germ“A subunit vaccine shows the body only a small piece of the germ.”
Herd immunity
when so many people are immune that a disease cannot spread easily“When most of the class is vaccinated, herd immunity protects everyone.”
Transcript

Maya: Welcome to The World, Word by Word! I'm Maya.

Leo: And I'm Leo. Maya, do you remember the last time you got a shot at the doctor's office?

Maya: Oh, I definitely remember. My arm was sore for a whole day. But that shot was a vaccine, and vaccines might be one of the greatest inventions in human history.

Leo: That is a big claim. Let's start with the basics. What is actually in that little shot?

Maya: A vaccine is a treatment that helps your body defend itself against disease. It doesn't wait until you're sick. It trains your immune system ahead of time, so it already knows how to fight the germ if the real one ever shows up.

Leo: The immune system is the body's defense team, right? The cells that hunt down harmful germs?

Maya: Exactly. And those germs are called pathogens. Here's the cool part: immune cells can remember. If they beat a certain germ once, they remember exactly how to fight it, and they get faster and better at it with every exposure.

Leo: So a vaccine is like a practice round for the immune system!

Maya: Perfect way to put it. A vaccine contains a very small dose of a disease-causing agent. It's too weak to make you sick, but it's just enough to teach your body what the enemy looks like. That is how you get immunity — your body can fight off the real disease without getting sick at all.

Leo: And some vaccines protect you for life, while others, like the flu vaccine, you need again every year.

Maya: Right. This idea goes back hundreds of years. The first vaccine was developed in seventeen ninety-six by an English doctor named Edward Jenner. Back then, a deadly disease called smallpox was killing thousands of people every year.

Leo: And Jenner noticed something about cowpox, right?

Maya: He noticed that people who caught a milder cow disease, cowpox, never seemed to get smallpox. So he tested his theory. He purposely gave a young boy cowpox, and after the boy recovered, Jenner exposed him to smallpox. The boy did not get sick!

Leo: That boy was the first person ever to be vaccinated.

Maya: And here's a fun fact: the word vaccine comes from the Latin word meaning from cows, all because of that cowpox story. Over the next two hundred years, this idea spread across the world, and smallpox was wiped out completely.

Leo: Vaccines for diseases like cholera, typhoid, and polio went on to prevent millions of deaths. And this story is still being written. In twenty twenty, when COVID nineteen swept the world, scientists rushed to create new vaccines.

Maya: By twenty twenty-one, several effective vaccines were available, and they helped prevent severe illness and slowed the spread everywhere. Vaccines are still being studied and created today.

Leo: Not all vaccines are the same, though. There are different types. Inactivated vaccines use dead viruses or bacteria. Even though they're dead, your immune system still learns to recognize them. These usually need several doses over your lifetime.

Maya: Attenuated vaccines use live germs that have been weakened. They reproduce very slowly, so your immune system has time to learn. One shot usually gives lifelong protection. But these can't be given to people with weakened immune systems.

Leo: Then there are subunit vaccines. They don't use whole germs at all. They just use antigens, the little markers on a germ's surface, to trigger the immune response.

Maya: And there's a newer kind: mRNA vaccines. Some COVID vaccines are mRNA vaccines. They give your body a tiny message that tells your cells to build one small piece of the virus. Your immune system learns to recognize that piece, and attacks the real virus later.

Leo: So many clever ways to do one job: train the defense team. Most vaccines are injected into the arm, but some come as drops you swallow, and others as a spray into the nose.

Maya: Now here's one of my favorite ideas: herd immunity. The more people who are immune to a disease, the less it spreads. And that protects everyone, even people who can't get vaccinated because they are sick or have weak immune systems.

Leo: Because people who are immune cannot spread the disease to others. So vaccination protects people with immunity, and people without it. It's a team effort.

Maya: Exactly. All right, now let's slow down and learn the words that make this story make sense. Here is our vocabulary segment!

Leo: Word one is vaccine, which means a medicine that teaches your body how to fight off a disease before you ever get sick. For example: The nurse gave me a vaccine in my arm to protect me from the flu.

Maya: Word two is pathogen, which means anything that can cause disease, like a virus, a bacteria, or a toxin. For example: Washing your hands helps keep pathogens away.

Leo: Word three is immune system, which means your body's defense team — the cells that find and destroy harmful germs. For example: A strong immune system helps you recover faster when you are sick.

Maya: Word four is immunity, which means your body can fight off a disease without getting sick. For example: After the vaccine, she had immunity against the measles.

Leo: Word five is antigen, which means a marker on the surface of a virus or bacteria that triggers your immune system to react. For example: The vaccine uses antigens to show the immune system what the virus looks like.

Maya: Word six is contract, which means to catch an illness or develop it. For example: He contracted the flu after sitting next to a sick classmate.

Leo: Word seven is inactivated, which describes a vaccine made from dead germs that cannot make you sick, but your body still learns to fight them. For example: The inactivated flu shot cannot give you the flu.

Maya: Word eight is attenuated, which describes a vaccine made from live germs that have been weakened. For example: The attenuated vaccine uses weakened live germs to build lifelong protection.

Leo: Word nine is subunit, which means a vaccine type that uses only small pieces of a germ, not the whole germ. For example: The subunit vaccine contains only parts of the virus.

Maya: Word ten is herd immunity, which means so many people are immune that a disease cannot spread easily, protecting even those who are not vaccinated. For example: Herd immunity protects babies who are too young to be vaccinated.

Leo: Wonderful! Now it's quiz time. Question one: What did Edward Jenner notice about people who caught cowpox?

Maya: He noticed they never seemed to get smallpox. That observation led him to test the first vaccine on a young boy, and it worked.

Leo: Question two: How does a vaccine train your immune system?

Maya: A vaccine gives your body a very small, harmless dose of a germ, so your immune system learns to recognize and destroy it. When the real germ shows up later, your immune cells remember how to fight it and act fast.

Leo: Question three: What is herd immunity, and who does it protect?

Maya: Herd immunity happens when enough people are immune that a disease can't spread easily. It protects everyone — including people who can't get vaccinated, like those who are sick or have weakened immune systems.

Leo: Nice work, listeners! If you got all three, give yourself a round of applause.

Maya: Vaccines have saved millions of lives, from wiping out smallpox to fighting COVID nineteen. A tiny shot, training your body's defenders — that's the story of vaccines.

Leo: Thanks for listening to The World, Word by Word! I'm Leo.

Maya: And I'm Maya. See you in the next episode!

THE WORLD, WORD BY WORD — Episode 48

Title: "Viruses: Tiny Invaders, Big Trouble" (Science)

Hosts: Maya (avocado_v2:MAI_01, Warm), Leo (avocado_v2:MAI_03, Smooth)

Date: 2026-09-10

--- PART 1: intro + main content (normal speed) ---

Leo: Welcome to The World, Word by Word! I'm Leo.

Maya: And I'm Maya. Leo, quick question. What's the smallest thing you can think of?

Leo: Hmm. An ant? A grain of sand?

Maya: Smaller. So small you can't see it at all. Today we're talking about something invisible that has shaken the whole world: viruses.

Leo: Like the flu and the common cold? Everyone gets those.

Maya: Exactly. A virus is a microorganism, too small to see without a microscope. And yet this tiny thing can make a whole person sick.

Leo: How? How does something invisible beat a whole human?

Maya: It's sneaky. For example, when a sick person sneezes, tiny droplets float through the air, and you might breathe them in.

Leo: Like invisible boats sailing straight at me. Yikes.

Maya: Good picture. Once inside you, the virus finds your cells, the tiny building blocks of your body. It slips into a cell, injects its instruction book, its genetic material, and takes over.

Leo: So the virus turns the cell into a virus factory?

Maya: Exactly. The hijacked cell makes more viruses, which invade even more cells. If your defenses can't stop them, you get a viral disease.

Leo: So a viral disease is a virus infection that spreads through the whole body?

Maya: Right. Unlike a cut that gets infected in one spot, a viral disease can hit many areas. Scientists know more than two hundred viruses that can affect humans.

Leo: I only knew about colds and the flu.

Maya: Chickenpox, measles, and mumps are viral diseases too.

Leo: Fast fact. The word virus comes from a Latin word meaning slimy liquid, or poison. Gross, but perfect.

Maya: And coronaviruses, the family that includes covid nineteen, are actually fragile. Plain soap and detergent can destroy them. That is why handwashing works.

Leo: Soap, the everyday superhero. Love it.

Maya: But not every virus is mild. Ebola kills about fifty percent of the people who catch it. And rabies, passed through animal bites, is fatal without treatment.

Leo: Scary. And covid nineteen became a global pandemic in March of twenty twenty, right?

Maya: Yes. It appeared in Wuhan, China, in late twenty nineteen. Countries closed schools and businesses, but by early twenty twenty one, more than one point eight million people had died.

Leo: Heartbreaking. But then vaccines arrived.

Maya: They did. By the end of twenty twenty, covid vaccines were approved, and life slowly returned to normal. Vaccines also defeated one of the scariest viral diseases ever: smallpox.

Leo: That sounds like history class.

Maya: It is history now. Smallpox came from the variola virus, and about thirty percent of patients died. The last United States case was in nineteen forty nine.

Leo: And today it is just gone?

Maya: Completely gone. Thanks to an effective vaccine, smallpox was eradicated, wiped out everywhere on Earth. One of the greatest victories in medicine.

Leo: So how do viruses travel from person to person?

Maya: Most often through the air, in droplets from coughs and sneezes. Some spread by touching dirty surfaces, or through animal and insect bites.

Leo: Can medicine kill a virus?

Maya: Antiviral drugs can make the flu shorter and less severe. But antibiotics do not work on viruses. They only fight bacteria, which are completely different germs.

Leo: Good to know. So antibiotics won't help my cold.

Maya: Nope. Most colds fade on their own. But here is the silver lining: after your body beats a virus, you usually develop immunity. It remembers the virus, so you are protected if it ever comes back.

Leo: Like your body keeps a mugshot of the bad guy. Nice.

Maya: Now let's slow down and learn our ten words for today. Listen carefully, and try repeating each word after me.

ScienceEpisode 477:18

Bacteria and Fungi: Nature's Tiniest Recyclers

Bacteria and fungi are some of the tiniest living things on Earth — and nature’s recyclers. Maya and Leo explore how these decomposers break down dead plants and animals, return nutrients to the soil, and keep ecosystems working.

Full episode

7:18 · Story, knowledge, and vocabulary

Download full episode

Words in This Episode

2:05 · Slow vocabulary review

Download words review
Words in this episode
organism
a living thing“a mushroom is an organism.”
microscopic
so small that it is only visible under a microscope“a single bacterium is microscopic.”
nucleus
the center part of a cell that holds its DNA“bacteria have no nucleus, but fungal cells do.”
multicellular
made up of many cells“mold is a multicellular fungus.”
decomposer
an organism that breaks down dead plants and animals and returns nutrients to the soil“fungi are important decomposers in the forest.”
pathogen
an organism that causes illness“the bacterium that causes strep throat is a pathogen.”
antibiotic
a medicine that fights bacterial infections“the doctor gave me an antibiotic for my ear infection.”
spore
a tiny reproductive cell that some fungi and plants use to make new organisms“mold spreads by releasing spores into the air.”
mycelium
the main hidden body of a mushroom that lives in the soil“the mycelium under the tree was as wide as a football field.”
chitin
the tough material that fungi use to build their cells“insects also use chitin to make their hard outer shells.”
Transcript

Maya: Welcome to The World, Word by Word! I'm Maya.

Leo: And I'm Leo. And today, Maya, I have a question for you. Have you ever left an apple out too long, and it got all brown and mushy?

Maya: Oh yes, definitely. It looks gross, but it made me wonder — what actually does that? Who breaks the apple down?

Leo: Great question! The answer is some of the tiniest living things on Earth. Today's episode is called "Bacteria and Fungi: Nature's Tiniest Recyclers."

Maya: I love that. So let's start at the beginning. What are bacteria?

Leo: Bacteria are single-celled organisms. That means each one is just one tiny cell. They do not have a nucleus inside. Cells without a nucleus are called prokaryotic cells.

Maya: And they are SO small that you need a microscope to see them. Each single bacterium is called a bacterium, by the way.

Leo: Right. And they live nearly everywhere — in soil, in water, on your skin, and even inside your body.

Maya: Now, some people hear "bacteria" and think, yuck, germs. But most bacteria are harmless. Some are even helpful!

Leo: Exactly. Your stomach has helpful bacteria that make it too acidic for harmful bacteria to live there. They're like tiny bodyguards.

Maya: But some bacteria are pathogens — organisms that cause illness, like strep throat, pneumonia, and food poisoning. The good news is doctors can fight them with medicines called antibiotics.

Leo: Now, scientists sort bacteria by shape. Most are spheres, rods, or spirals. Sphere-shaped ones are called cocci, and rod-shaped ones are called bacilli.

Maya: And shape actually matters. It affects how they move, how they get food, and how they fight off threats.

Leo: Here's my favorite weird fact. Even though each bacterium is just one cell, they can communicate. They send chemical signals to each other in a process called sensing.

Maya: Like they're texting each other! When they communicate, they can coordinate almost like one big team instead of tiny individuals.

Leo: Okay, now let's talk about fungi. Fungi are more complicated than bacteria. They can be made of one cell or many cells.

Maya: And here's a fun fact — people used to think mushrooms were plants. But they're not! Fungi are actually more closely related to animals than to plants.

Leo: That's wild. One reason is that fungi don't make their own food like plants do. They absorb nutrients from the surfaces around them.

Maya: Also, fungi build their cells with a special material called chitin. Some animals use chitin too, but plants do not. Scientists have found nearly one hundred thousand types of fungi — and they keep discovering more.

Leo: Let's meet the three main environmental fungi: yeast, mold, and mushrooms. Yeast are microscopic, single-celled fungi. Mold is made of many cells. And mushrooms are the most famous of all.

Maya: Here's the cool part about mushrooms. The mushroom you see above the ground is just the fruit of the organism — like an apple on a tree. The main living part hides underground in the soil. That hidden body is called the mycelium.

Leo: So a mushroom is like an iceberg — most of it is out of sight!

Maya: Some fungi, called commensals, live on and in our bodies. Usually they are harmless or even helpful, but if they grow too fast, they can cause problems.

Leo: Now the big job both bacteria and fungi do — they're decomposers. When a plant dies and falls to the ground, bacteria and fungi break it down. The nutrients go back into the soil, where new plants can use them.

Maya: So they recycle! Without decomposers, dead things would just pile up forever, and plants would run out of nutrients.

Leo: But some fungi and bacteria are also pathogens. Fungal infections can kill plants — one fungus wiped out most of the American chestnut trees in the eastern United States.

Maya: And in people, fungi can cause athlete's foot and ringworm. People with weak immune systems have to be extra careful.

Leo: And this whole tiny world — bacteria, fungi, and other microbes — is studied by scientists called microbiologists. They help make medicines, keep our food and water safe, and understand how microbes affect our health.

Maya: Small things, huge jobs. Now, before we finish, let's slow down and learn some key words from today's story.

Maya: Okay, time to test those brand-new words! Quiz question number one. What do we call the main, hidden body of a mushroom that lives in the soil?

Leo: Think about it. I'll give you three seconds. One, two, three. The answer is mycelium! The mushroom you see is just the fruit. The mycelium is the real living body underground.

Maya: Question two. Fungi build their cells with a special material that plants never use. What's it called?

Leo: One, two, three. It's chitin! Fungi and some animals use chitin, but plants do not. Nice work if you got that one.

Maya: Last question. Bacteria and fungi that break down dead plants and animals and return nutrients to the soil are called what?

Leo: One, two, three. Decomposers! They are nature's recyclers, and ecosystems couldn't work without them.

Maya: How did you do? Three for three? Even if you missed one, you're smarter than you were six minutes ago.

Leo: That's it for today's episode of The World, Word by Word. Keep looking closely at the world around you — sometimes the smallest things do the biggest jobs.

Maya: See you next time. Bye!

Leo: Bye!

ScienceEpisode 467:25

Brain: The Three-Pound Boss Inside Your Skull

Your brain is a three-pound, eighty-six-billion-neuron command center: the cerebrum thinks, the cerebellum balances, the brain stem keeps you alive, and every time you learn something new, your brain builds fresh connections.

Full episode

Transcript

Maya: Leo, quick question. What in your body weighs about three pounds, is about seventy-five percent water, and runs absolutely everything you do?

Leo: Three pounds of water that runs everything? That sounds like a sponge, not a body part.

Maya: It is your brain. Today we are going on a tour of the command center inside your skull, and trust me, it is stranger and cooler than a sponge.

Leo: Okay, where do we start?

Maya: Let us start with the fast facts. Your brain weighs about three pounds, it is about seventy-five percent water, and it holds around eighty-six billion nerve cells called neurons. That is eighty-six billion tiny messengers sending signals through your body, every second of every day.

Leo: Eighty-six billion? That is more than the number of stars you can see on a clear night.

Maya: Probably. And here is the best part. Your brain never stops building. Even when you learn something new, like a word on this show, your brain is literally making fresh connections between neurons. That is how you get smarter.

Leo: What does the brain actually do with all those neurons?

Maya: Everything. It processes sensory information, like what you see and hear. It controls your muscles. It releases hormones, which are chemicals that keep your body running. It is divided into different parts, and each part has its own job.

Leo: Okay, break it down for me. What are the parts?

Maya: The biggest part is the cerebrum, and it is split into two halves called hemispheres. The left hemisphere controls the right side of your body, and the right hemisphere controls the left side. They are connected by a bundle of nerve fibers called the corpus callosum, so the two halves can talk to each other.

Maya: Now the outer layer of the cerebrum is called the cerebral cortex, and it has four regions called lobes. The frontal lobe sits at the front and handles the heavy thinking: judgment, planning, problem solving, attention, and controlling your emotions and impulses.

Leo: So when I decide not to eat a third cookie, that is my frontal lobe being responsible?

Maya: Exactly. Right behind it is the parietal lobe, which handles senses like touch and sight. It also helps you process language and find your way around, so you do not get lost.

Leo: And the other two lobes?

Maya: Near your ears sits the temporal lobe. It is in charge of memory and understanding language. It helps you recognize faces, and it has two special areas inside, the hippocampus and the amygdala, that manage memory and emotion. At the very back is the occipital lobe, the visual processing center, which helps you read words, recognize colors, and see motion.

Leo: So the front thinks, the sides remember, the top senses, and the back sees. What about balance? I can stand on one foot without falling, mostly.

Maya: That is the cerebellum, the little brain at the back, below the occipital lobe. It coordinates fine motor skills, which are the small movements of your hands and feet. It works with your eyes to keep your balance, your posture, and your coordination.

Leo: Is there anything in the middle?

Maya: Yes, a region called the diencephalon, between the cerebrum and the brain stem. It holds the thalamus, a relay station that sends signals up to the cortex and helps control alertness and sleep. It also holds the hypothalamus, which keeps your body in homeostasis, meaning the balance of all your body functions, like temperature, hunger, and your sleep-wake cycle.

Leo: So the hypothalamus is basically the body's thermostat and clock. What is the brain stem for?

Maya: The brain stem connects the brain to the spinal cord, and it runs the things you do without thinking. The midbrain helps with eye movement. The pons carries nerves that control your face and pass along sensory information. And the medulla oblongata controls your heart and lungs, so it handles breathing, swallowing, and even sneezing.

Leo: So if I did not have a brain stem, I would forget to breathe. That is terrifying and amazing at the same time.

Maya: It is. Now, one more cool thing. Human brains are not just bigger than most animal brains. They have many folds in the cortex, which packs in far more neurons. Dolphins have folds too, and they are smart. Mice have smooth brains, and they are, well, less smart. Plus, humans have the largest frontal lobe of any animal, which is why we can plan, reason, and think abstractly.

Leo: Folds make us smart, and a giant frontal lobe makes us human. I love that.

Maya: Now let's do our vocabulary spotlight. Listen carefully, because these words describe the most complicated object in the known universe.

Leo: Word one is neuron, which means a nerve cell that sends messages through the body. Your brain contains about eighty-six billion neurons.

Maya: Word two is nervous system, which means the network of nerves that sends signals from the brain to the rest of the body. Your nervous system tells your hand to wave hello.

Leo: Word three is sensory, which means relating to the physical senses, like sight, hearing, and touch. Your eyes send sensory information to your brain every second.

Maya: Word four is hormone, which means a chemical the body produces to control certain functions. A hormone called adrenaline gives you a burst of energy when you are nervous.

Leo: Word five is hemisphere, which means one of the two halves of the cerebrum. The left hemisphere of your brain controls the right side of your body.

Maya: Word six is cerebrum, which means the largest part of the brain, where thinking and planning happen. The cerebrum is divided into four lobes, each with a different job.

Leo: Word seven is cerebellum, which means the small brain at the back that controls balance and fine movements. Your cerebellum keeps you steady when you ride a bicycle.

Maya: Word eight is motor skills, which means the learned abilities to move your muscles on purpose. Tying your shoes and catching a ball both use motor skills.

Leo: Word nine is equilibrium, which means the ability to keep your body's balance. The cerebellum helps you keep your equilibrium when you stand on one foot.

Maya: And word ten is homeostasis, which means the balance of all the body's functions, kept steady on purpose. Your hypothalamus keeps homeostasis by holding your body temperature steady.

Leo: That is all ten words. Let us make sure they stuck.

Maya: You have earned a quiz. Question one. The largest part of the brain is the cerebellum, the cerebrum, the medulla, or the pons?

Leo: Think it through, listeners. The answer is the cerebrum. The cerebrum is the largest part, and it handles thinking, planning, and judgment.

Maya: Question two. True or false. The left hemisphere of the brain controls the left side of the body.

Leo: Hmm, be careful with this one. The answer is false. The left hemisphere controls the right side of the body, and the right hemisphere controls the left side.

Maya: Last one. Which part of the brain keeps your heart beating and your lungs breathing without you thinking about it: the frontal lobe, the temporal lobe, the occipital lobe, or the brain stem?

Leo: Take a guess. It is the brain stem. The brain stem runs the automatic jobs, like breathing, swallowing, and your heartbeat.

Maya: You did great. So remember, your brain is a three-pound, eighty-six-billion-neuron command center. The cerebrum thinks, the cerebellum balances, the brain stem keeps you alive, and every time you learn something new, your brain builds fresh connections. Keep feeding it good questions.

Leo: See you next time on The World, Word by Word.

Cancer: When Cells Stop Listening

Maya and Leo explain what cancer is and how tumors form, the risk factors you can avoid, and how doctors fight back with surgery, radiation therapy, and chemotherapy — plus what it means when symptoms fade into remission.

Transcript

Maya: Welcome back to The World, Word by Word, the show where we explore big ideas one word at a time. I'm Maya.

Leo: And I'm Leo. Today's topic is a serious one, but really worth understanding: cancer.

Maya: Cancer is actually the second leading cause of death around the world. But what is it, exactly?

Leo: Let's start with your body. Your body is made of trillions of tiny building blocks called cells. Normally, cells grow, divide to make new cells, and die when they're old or damaged. That's the healthy cycle.

Maya: But every cell has genes, tiny instruction manuals that tell it how to grow and work. Sometimes those genes get changed or damaged. And when that happens, the cell can stop following the rules.

Leo: Right. These broken cells start growing out of control and refuse to die. They pile up and clump together into a lump called a tumor.

Maya: And a tumor is dangerous because as it grows, it destroys the healthy cells and tissue around it. Even worse, some cancer cells can break away, travel through the blood to other parts of the body, and start brand-new tumors there.

Leo: Here's something surprising: cancer isn't just one disease. There are more than one hundred types of cancer, and each type has different symptoms.

Maya: So why do these gene changes happen? Sometimes it's just bad luck, tiny mistakes when the body copies its genes. But scientists have also found risk factors, things that raise a person's chance of getting cancer.

Leo: And some risk factors can be avoided. Tobacco is a big one. Smoking causes about twenty-two percent of cancer deaths worldwide. Substances that can cause cancer are called carcinogens, and tobacco smoke is full of them.

Maya: Too much sunlight is another avoidable risk. The ultraviolet rays from the sun can damage skin cells and cause skin cancer. That's one reason sunscreen matters.

Leo: Polluted air, water, and soil can also expose people to cancer-causing substances. And alcohol plays a role too. Drinking raises the risk of cancers of the mouth, throat, breast, and liver, and the more a person drinks, the higher the risk.

Maya: Staying at a healthy weight, eating well, and staying active can lower the risk as well. But experts say no single food has ever been proven to cause or prevent cancer.

Leo: Then there are risk factors nobody can control. Age is the biggest one. The average age of a cancer diagnosis is sixty-six. But cancer can appear at any age. Bone cancer and leukemia, a cancer of the blood, are most common in kids and teenagers under twenty.

Maya: Heredity can matter too. Some people inherit gene changes from their parents that raise their risk. That's why doctors ask about family history.

Leo: And a weak immune system, your body's defense team, makes it harder to find and destroy cancer cells. Certain medicines, diseases, and even long-lasting inflammation, that swollen state when the body keeps fighting an injury, can raise the risk.

Maya: Now for some hope. Doctors have three main treatments. The first is surgery: doctors cut out the tumor, and sometimes a little healthy tissue around it, to be sure they got every bad cell.

Leo: The second is radiation therapy, which uses high-energy waves, like X-rays, to kill cancer cells and shrink tumors.

Maya: The third is chemotherapy, special anticancer medicines, some in pill form, some given through a vein. The medicine travels through the blood, attacking cancer cells.

Leo: These treatments have saved millions of lives, but they have side effects. They can harm healthy cells too, which is why patients sometimes feel tired, lose their appetite, or lose their hair.

Maya: When the symptoms of cancer fade or disappear after treatment, doctors call it remission. It's the goal of every treatment.

Leo: Great. We've covered what cancer is, what raises the risk, and how doctors fight it. Now let's slow down and build our vocabulary, because knowing the words makes the science so much easier. Here are our words for this episode.

Maya: Word one is tumor, which means a lump formed by abnormal cells clumping together. For example, the surgeon removed the tumor from the patient's arm.

Leo: Word two is gene, which means a tiny instruction inside your cells that controls how they grow and work. For example, you inherit half of your genes from each parent.

Maya: Word three is carcinogen, which means a substance that can cause cancer. For example, cigarette smoke contains many harmful carcinogens.

Leo: Word four is radiation, which means energy that can also be used to kill cancer cells and shrink tumors. For example, the patient received radiation therapy to shrink the tumor.

Maya: Word five is chemotherapy, which means cancer treatment using special medicines that attack cancer cells. For example, chemotherapy helped put her cancer into remission.

Leo: Word six is remission, which means the slowing or disappearance of disease symptoms after treatment. For example, the doctor was happy to say the cancer was in remission.

Maya: Word seven is leukemia, which means cancer of the blood or blood-forming tissue like bone marrow. For example, leukemia is one of the most common cancers in children.

Leo: Word eight is inflammation, which means a swollen state that appears when the body reacts to an injury or infection. For example, the inflammation around the cut helped it heal.

Maya: Word nine is symptom, which means a sign that something is wrong in the body, like pain or tiredness. For example, a lasting cough can be a symptom of an illness.

Leo: Word ten is heredity, which means the passing of traits or gene changes from parents to children. For example, her eye color is a result of heredity.

Maya: That was our slow vocabulary round. Now it's quiz time. Three questions to test what you learned. Question one: what is a tumor?

Leo: A tumor is a lump formed by abnormal cells that grow out of control and clump together.

Maya: Question two: name one risk factor for cancer that a person can avoid.

Leo: Tobacco use, too much sun exposure, or drinking alcohol. Any of those works.

Maya: And question three: what are the three main treatments for cancer?

Leo: Surgery, radiation therapy, and chemotherapy. And here's a bonus: what do doctors call it when the symptoms fade after treatment? That's right, remission.

Maya: Nicely done, listeners. That wraps up our episode on cancer. Keep asking questions about the world, and we'll see you next time on The World, Word by Word.

Leo: Bye everyone.

Asthma: The Airways That Tighten

Maya and Leo explain what happens inside the airways during an asthma attack, why about one in thirteen Americans has asthma, what causes it, the three common types, and how inhalers and treatment plans help people live full, active lives.

Transcript

Maya: Welcome to The World, Word by Word. I'm Maya.

Leo: And I'm Leo. Today's topic is something almost everyone knows a little about, but few people truly understand.

Maya: Let me start with a number. Think of your whole grade, plus the one above it. If every thirteen kids formed a group, at least one kid in each group would have today's condition.

Leo: One in thirteen. That's a lot. What condition are we talking about?

Maya: Asthma. About one out of every thirteen Americans has some form of asthma. That's roughly seven or eight percent of the country.

Leo: So in a school of four hundred kids, about thirty of them would have asthma. Wow.

Maya: And many of them developed it during childhood. Asthma is one of the most common reasons kids miss school days.

Leo: Okay, but what actually is asthma? What happens inside the body?

Maya: Asthma is a chronic disease of the airways. The airways are the tubes that carry air in and out of your lungs, like hallways air travels through.

Leo: And asthma messes with those hallways?

Maya: Exactly. Asthma causes inflammation and tightening of the airways. Inflammation is swelling, and tightening means the tubes squeeze narrow. Imagine trying to breathe through a straw that keeps getting thinner.

Leo: That sounds scary. Is it always like that, or does it come and go?

Maya: It comes in episodes. A severe episode is called an asthma attack. During an attack, a person can feel shortness of breath, like the air just won't come in.

Leo: What does that feel like for the person? Are there signs other people can notice?

Maya: Yes. One is wheezing, a whistling sound that happens when air squeezes through a blocked airway. Another is tightness in the chest, like a belt pulled too tight around your ribs. And it often causes regular coughing.

Leo: Can an asthma attack be dangerous?

Maya: It can. These symptoms can usually be managed with a treatment plan made together with a doctor. But if an asthma attack is not controlled, it can become a medical emergency.

Leo: How long does someone have asthma? Is it like a cold that goes away?

Maya: No, and that's an important point. Asthma is a lifelong condition that cannot be cured. Once a person has asthma, they have it for a lifetime.

Leo: Forever? That's heavy.

Maya: It's true, but there's hope. Some children who have asthma feel much better as they get older, with fewer and fewer symptoms. And modern medicine can manage it very well.

Leo: You said earlier that doctors still don't know the single cause. But they must have some clues.

Maya: They do. First, there seems to be a genetic piece. Asthma tends to run in families. If one parent has asthma, the children are more likely to have it too.

Leo: So part of it is in your genes. What else?

Maya: The environment. People who live in cities where smog and other air pollution are common might be at greater risk. The air inside your home matters too.

Leo: What inside a home could cause trouble?

Maya: Allergens, things that can cause an allergic reaction. Dust, pollen, mold, smoke, and pet dander, which are the tiny skin cells that animals shed. People with other allergic conditions, like eczema, might also be at greater risk.

Leo: So your family history plus the air you breathe. I heard there are different types of asthma?

Maya: Three common ones. Allergy-induced asthma happens when something in the air, like mold, smoke, pollen, or pet dander, sets off a reaction. Sometimes it only shows up in certain seasons when a particular pollen is in the air.

Leo: What about the second type?

Maya: Exercise-induced asthma. It happens when a person does hard physical activity, and it is even more likely when the air is cold or dry.

Leo: That must be tough for kids who love sports. And the third?

Maya: Occupational asthma. That one comes from something in the workplace, like fumes from a chemical, smoke, or a strong smell. Once a person's body has reacted to that trigger, they are likely to react to it again.

Leo: So triggers are everywhere. How do people with asthma deal with all of this?

Maya: There is no cure, but there are good treatment options. The most famous one is the inhaler. An inhaler is a small device that delivers medicine straight to the lungs, usually as a quick puff of spray.

Leo: I've seen those in movies. A kid takes a puff before a game. How do they work?

Maya: There are two main types. One is called a bronchodilator. It is a fast-acting inhaler, sometimes called a rescue inhaler, because it is used at the start of an asthma attack to open up the airways.

Leo: A rescue inhaler. That name tells you exactly what it does.

Maya: The other type is a steroid inhaler. It reduces the inflammation in the airways over time, which makes an asthma attack less likely to happen in the first place.

Leo: So one stops an attack, and the other tries to prevent the next one. Smart.

Maya: Doctors can also prescribe medicine in pill form to reduce swelling of the airways, or give injections that calm down reactions to triggers like pollen or pet dander. And people with asthma learn their personal triggers and try to avoid them.

Leo: What are some common triggers?

Maya: They differ from person to person, but the list includes irritants in the air, having a cold, air pollution, cold air, stress, exercise, and even reflux.

Leo: Let's talk numbers again. How serious is asthma in the United States?

Maya: In twenty twenty-four, the Centers for Disease Control reported that about seven point seven percent of Americans had asthma as of twenty twenty-one. And the Asthma and Allergy Foundation reported that three thousand five hundred sixteen people died from asthma in twenty twenty-one.

Leo: Over three thousand deaths. That's sobering.

Maya: Here's the part that sticks with me. The foundation says the majority of those deaths could have been prevented with proper care and management. So understanding asthma really matters.

Leo: Which brings us to our vocabulary spotlight. Maya, take it away.

Maya: Let's do our vocabulary spotlight. Word one is chronic, which means a condition that lasts a long time, often for the rest of a person's life. Asthma is a chronic disease, so people with asthma have it for a lifetime.

Leo: Word two is inflammation, which means swelling of a part of the body, usually with redness and heat. Asthma causes inflammation and tightening of the airways that lead to and from the lungs.

Maya: Word three is wheezing, which means making a whistling sound when an airway is blocked. A child having an asthma attack might start wheezing as air squeezes through narrowed airways.

Leo: Word four is irritant, which means something that causes discomfort or pain to the body. Smoke, strong smells, and dust can act as irritants that trigger asthma symptoms.

Maya: Word five is trigger, which means something that sets off a reaction or an event. Cold air, exercise, and stress can be triggers that bring on an asthma attack.

Leo: Word six is dander, which means the tiny skin cells that animals shed. Pet dander floating in the air can cause an allergic reaction in some people with asthma.

Maya: Word seven is mucus, which means the slippery protective liquid that lines parts of the body. During an asthma attack, extra mucus can make the airways even narrower and harder to breathe through.

Leo: Word eight is smog, which means smoke or other pollutants combined with fog. People who live in cities where smog and air pollution are common might be at greater risk of asthma.

Maya: Word nine is bronchodilator, which means a fast-acting medicine that opens the passageways to the lungs. A bronchodilator inhaler, sometimes called a rescue inhaler, is used at the onset of an asthma attack.

Leo: And word ten is steroid, which here means a type of medicine that reduces inflammation in the body. A steroid inhaler helps reduce swelling in the airways and makes an asthma attack less likely.

Maya: Well done, listeners. Ten new words, all connected to how our airways work.

Leo: Now a quick quiz. Question one. About how many Americans have asthma: one in fifty, one in thirteen, or one in a hundred?

Leo: Question two. True or false. Asthma can be completely cured with medicine, so people only have it for a short time.

Maya: Think it through. False. Asthma is a lifelong condition that cannot be cured, though it can be managed very well with a treatment plan.

Leo: Last one. The word bronchodilator most nearly means a medicine that opens airways, a kind of cough, a type of allergy, or a medical emergency?

Maya: Take a guess. It means a medicine that opens the airways. It is the fast-acting rescue inhaler used at the start of an asthma attack.

Leo: You did great. So remember, asthma affects about one in thirteen Americans. It is a chronic disease that swells and tightens the airways, but with the right treatment plan, people with asthma can live full, active lives.

Maya: And most importantly, the Asthma and Allergy Foundation says the majority of asthma deaths could have been prevented with proper care. Understanding the condition is the first step.

ScienceEpisode 435:24

Compounds and Mixtures: Joined for Good, or Just Mixed Together

Maya and Leo explain how atoms lock together with chemical bonds to form compounds with fixed recipes, while mixtures are just blended and easy to change, using water, salt, coffee, and chicken noodle soup as examples.

Full episode

5:24 · Story, knowledge, and vocabulary

Download full episode

Words in This Episode

2:08 · Slow vocabulary review

Download words review
Words in this episode
compound
a substance where atoms are chemically bonded together“water is a compound made of hydrogen and oxygen.”
mixture
two or more substances blended together without chemical bonds“tea is a mixture of water, tea leaves, and sugar.”
molecule
a group of atoms that are chemically bonded together“one molecule of water has two hydrogen atoms and one oxygen atom.”
bond
the link that joins atoms together when they share or trade electrons“salt is held together by an ionic bond.”
homogeneous
spread out evenly through a mixture“coffee with stirred-in milk is a homogeneous mixture.”
heterogeneous
not spread out evenly“chicken noodle soup is a heterogeneous mixture because the noodles float on the broth.”
ratio
the relationship in the numbers between two or more things“water always has a hydrogen to oxygen ratio of two to one.”
ionic
It describes a bond where one atom gives electrons to another“table salt is an ionic compound made of sodium and chlorine.”
covalent
It describes a bond where atoms share electrons“in a water molecule, hydrogen and oxygen share electrons.”
components
the parts that make up something larger“the components of coffee include water, coffee flavoring, sugar, and cream.”
Transcript

Maya: Hi there! Welcome back to The World, Word by Word. I'm Maya.

Leo: And I'm Leo. Today we're talking about something that's inside almost everything around you, from your breakfast to the air you breathe.

Maya: Our topic is compounds and mixtures: two ways that different materials join together.

Leo: Let's start simple. Everything in the universe is made of tiny building blocks called atoms. An atom is the smallest unit of matter. It has a center called the nucleus, with tiny particles called electrons circling around it.

Maya: Atoms love to team up. When atoms link together at the tiniest level, they form a compound. They join by sharing or trading electrons. That link is called a chemical bond.

Leo: So a compound is a substance where atoms are chemically bonded together. And here is the cool part: a compound has a fixed recipe. It always has the same ratio of atoms, no matter what.

Maya: Exactly. Take water. Every water molecule is made of two hydrogen atoms and one oxygen atom, written as H two O. Add one more oxygen atom, and it is no longer water. It becomes hydrogen peroxide, the liquid people use to clean small cuts.

Leo: And compounds can surprise you. Hydrogen and oxygen are both gases, but together they make liquid water. A compound has its own brand-new properties, different from its atoms.

Maya: There are different kinds of chemical bonds. When atoms share electrons, it is called a covalent bond. When one atom gives an electron to another, it is called an ionic bond. Table salt is made this way: one sodium atom gives an electron to one chlorine atom.

Leo: Now what about mixtures? A mixture is when two or more substances are blended but not chemically bonded. Like tea. Water, tea leaves, sugar, and flavorings are all in your cup, but none of them are locked together by chemical bonds.

Maya: Mixtures come in two kinds. A homogeneous mixture is evenly spread out. Coffee with milk and sugar, stirred well, is homogeneous. Every sip tastes the same.

Leo: A heterogeneous mixture is not evenly spread. Chicken noodle soup is a perfect example. The noodles and chicken often float on the broth. A bowl of cereal is another one.

Maya: The big difference? You can change a mixture easily. Add more sugar to coffee and it gets sweeter. Take some away and it is more bitter. But you cannot change a compound's recipe. Water is always H two O.

Leo: Mixtures have no fixed boiling or freezing points either. Pure water freezes at thirty-two degrees Fahrenheit, but salty water, which is a mixture, freezes much colder.

Maya: So to sum up: compounds are joined at the atomic level with chemical bonds and fixed recipes. Mixtures are just blended, and you can always change them or pull them apart.

Maya: It is word time! Let's review ten important words from today, nice and slow.

Maya: Word one is compound. A compound is a substance where atoms are chemically bonded together. Water is a compound made of hydrogen and oxygen.

Leo: Word two is mixture. A mixture is two or more substances blended together without chemical bonds. Tea is a mixture of water, tea leaves, and sugar.

Maya: Word three is molecule. A molecule is a group of atoms that are chemically bonded together. One molecule of water has two hydrogen atoms and one oxygen atom.

Leo: Word four is bond. A chemical bond is the link that joins atoms together when they share or trade electrons. Salt is held together by an ionic bond.

Maya: Word five is homogeneous. Homogeneous means spread out evenly through a mixture. Coffee with stirred-in milk is a homogeneous mixture.

Leo: Word six is heterogeneous. Heterogeneous means not spread out evenly. Chicken noodle soup is a heterogeneous mixture because the noodles float on the broth.

Maya: Word seven is ratio. A ratio is the relationship in the numbers between two or more things. Water always has a hydrogen to oxygen ratio of two to one.

Leo: Word eight is ionic. Ionic describes a bond where one atom gives electrons to another. Table salt is an ionic compound made of sodium and chlorine.

Maya: Word nine is covalent. Covalent describes a bond where atoms share electrons. In a water molecule, hydrogen and oxygen share electrons.

Leo: Word ten is components. Components are the parts that make up something larger. The components of coffee include water, coffee flavoring, sugar, and cream.

Maya: That is all ten words. Now let's see what you remember.

Leo: Quiz time! Question one: is salt water a compound or a mixture?

Maya: It is a mixture! The salt and water are blended, but not chemically bonded. You can separate them and change the recipe.

Leo: Question two: what makes a homogeneous mixture different from a heterogeneous one?

Maya: In a homogeneous mixture, everything is spread evenly, like coffee. In a heterogeneous mixture, the parts are not evenly spread, like cereal or soup.

Leo: Question three: what happens if you add an extra oxygen atom to water?

Maya: It is no longer water! It becomes hydrogen peroxide. That is because a compound always has the same fixed ratio of atoms.

Leo: Great job today. You now know the difference between compounds and mixtures, and ten brand-new words.

Maya: Thanks for listening to The World, Word by Word. See you next time!

ScienceEpisode 428:23

Atoms: The Tiny Building Blocks of Everything

Atoms are the building blocks of everything: protons decide the element, neutrons decide the isotope, and you are made of stardust.

Words in this episode
proton
one of the positively charged particles inside an atom's nucleus“A hydrogen atom has exactly one proton.”
neutron
one of the particles in an atom's nucleus that carries no electrical charge“The number of neutrons can vary without changing the element.”

a tiny negatively charged particle that forms a cloud around an atom's nucleus“Electrons are more than eighteen hundred times smaller than protons.”

the dense center of an atom that holds the protons and neutrons“Almost all of an atom's mass sits in the nucleus.”

the number of protons in an atom, and it defines the element“Oxygen's atomic number is eight because oxygen atoms have eight protons.”
