Transcript

Maya: Here's a riddle for you, Leo. What is the smallest building block of everything, too tiny to see, and so numerous that the universe holds a one followed by eighty zeros of them?

Leo: Too tiny to see, and there are that many of them? Is it some kind of alien dust?

Maya: Not aliens. Atoms. The atom is the most fundamental part of all the matter in the universe, which means it is the most basic building block everything is made of.

Leo: Everything? Like me, this table, the air?

Maya: Everything. You, me, the table, the air, the stars. They are all built out of atoms.

Leo: Okay, but if atoms are so tiny, how do we even know what they look like?

Maya: It took scientists thousands of years to figure it out. Way back in the fifth century B C, a Greek philosopher named Democritus had an idea. He thought that if you kept cutting something into smaller and smaller pieces, you would finally reach a piece that could not be divided any further.

Leo: And he called that piece an atom?

Maya: Exactly. He called them atomos, which is Greek for indivisible, meaning cannot be divided. But he was only guessing. For over two thousand years, nobody could prove atoms were real.

Leo: So when did we actually learn what is inside one?

Maya: Mostly in the last one hundred and thirty years. In eighteen ninety seven, J J Thomson discovered the electron, and proved that atoms can actually be divided. That surprised everyone, because Democritus thought atoms were indivisible.

Leo: So atoms are not indivisible after all. What is inside them?

Maya: An atom has three main particles. In the center there is a dense ball called the nucleus, and the nucleus is made of two kinds of particles, protons and neutrons. Around the nucleus floats a cloud of electrons.

Leo: Three particles. How are they different?

Maya: Protons carry a positive electrical charge. Neutrons carry no charge at all, they are neutral. And electrons carry a negative charge, and they are more than eighteen hundred times smaller than protons and neutrons.

Leo: If protons are positive and electrons are negative, do they balance each other out?

Maya: They do. In a normal atom, the number of protons always equals the number of electrons, so the charges cancel out. But sometimes an atom gains or loses electrons, and then it becomes an ion, an atom with an electrical charge.

Leo: An ion. Like a charged-up atom. Got it. So what decides which element an atom is?

Maya: The number of protons. Every element is defined by its atomic number, which is simply the number of protons inside the nucleus. Hydrogen has one proton. Oxygen has eight protons. Add or remove a proton, and you change one element into a different element.

Leo: Wait, that is amazing. So the whole difference between hydrogen and oxygen is seven protons?

Maya: That is right. Scientists organized all the known elements by their atomic number into the periodic table. Dmitri Mendeleev published the first widely recognized periodic table back in eighteen sixty nine.

Leo: I remember the periodic table from school. But do all atoms of the same element stay exactly the same?

Maya: Not quite. The number of protons never changes, but the number of neutrons can vary. Atoms of the same element with different numbers of neutrons are called isotopes. Isotopes are just different forms of the same element.

Leo: What about the electrons? They just float around?

Maya: Their arrangement matters a lot. Scientists call the arrangement of electrons around a nucleus the electron configuration. The electrons furthest from the nucleus can link up with nearby atoms and bond with them, forming molecules and chemical compounds.

Leo: Like water?

Maya: Exactly like water. One water molecule is two hydrogen atoms and one oxygen atom bonded together. H two O.

Leo: And what holds all of this together? What holds protons together inside the nucleus if they are all positively charged and should push each other away?

Maya: Great thinking. Tiny particles called gluons hold the protons and neutrons together. And the protons and neutrons themselves are made of even smaller units called quarks. Two of the three quarks inside a proton are positively charged, and one is negatively charged.

Leo: How far down does this go? Is there anything smaller than a quark?

Maya: As far as scientists know today, quarks and electrons are as small as it gets. But remember, most of an atom is actually empty space. The nucleus holds almost all of the atom's mass, but the atom itself is mostly nothing.

Leo: That is wild. If I am mostly empty space, why can't I walk through walls?

Maya: Because the electron clouds of your atoms push against the electron clouds of the wall's atoms. Their negative charges repel each other, so you bounce off instead of passing through.

Leo: Where did all these atoms come from in the first place?

Maya: Scientists believe the building blocks of atoms were created thirteen point seven billion years ago in the big bang. Once the universe cooled, quarks and electrons formed, then protons and neutrons, and finally the first full atoms, which were hydrogen and helium, the lightest elements.

Leo: And the heavier elements?

Maya: They were cooked inside stars. Clouds of hydrogen and helium formed stars, and inside the stars heavier atoms were built up. When giant stars exploded, those atoms spread across the universe and eventually became planets, and us.

Leo: So I am literally made of stardust?

Maya: You really are. The carbon in your body, the iron in your blood, all of it was forged inside a star billions of years ago. Now, let us slow down and learn the key words from this episode.

Maya: Let us slow down and learn our words together, nice and steady, and repeat each one after us.

Maya: Word one is proton, which means one of the positively charged particles inside an atom's nucleus. A hydrogen atom has exactly one proton.

Leo: Word two is neutron, which means one of the particles in an atom's nucleus that carries no electrical charge. The number of neutrons can vary without changing the element.

Maya: Word three is electron, which means a tiny negatively charged particle that forms a cloud around an atom's nucleus. Electrons are more than eighteen hundred times smaller than protons.

Leo: Word four is nucleus, which means the dense center of an atom that holds the protons and neutrons. Almost all of an atom's mass sits in the nucleus.

Maya: Word five is atomic number, which means the number of protons in an atom, and it defines the element. Oxygen's atomic number is eight because oxygen atoms have eight protons.

Leo: Word six is isotope, which means a different form of an element that has the same number of protons but a different number of neutrons. Most elements have more than one naturally occurring isotope.

Maya: Word seven is ion, which means an atom that has gained or lost electrons and now carries an electrical charge. When an atom loses electrons, it becomes a positively charged ion.

Leo: Word eight is quark, which means an extremely tiny particle that makes up protons and neutrons. A proton is built from two positively charged quarks and one negatively charged quark.

Maya: Word nine is fundamental, which means basic, essential, and forming the foundation of something. The atom is the most fundamental building block of all matter in the universe.

Leo: And word ten is configuration, which means the way the parts of something are arranged. An atom's electron configuration decides how it will bond with other atoms.

Maya: Wonderful listening. Now let us see how much you remember with a quick quiz.

Maya: Quiz time. Question one. What is an atom's atomic number?

Leo: Think it through. The atomic number is the number of protons in the atom's nucleus, and it defines the element.

Maya: Question two. An isotope has a different number of protons, neutrons, or electrons?

Leo: Take a guess. Neutrons. An isotope is the same element with a different number of neutrons.

Maya: Last one. Where did most of the atoms in your body come from?

Leo: Think for a moment. From inside ancient stars. The heavy elements were forged in stars and spread across the universe when the stars exploded.

Maya: You did great. So remember, atoms are the building blocks of everything, protons decide the element, neutrons decide the isotope, and you are made of stardust. See you next time on The World, Word by Word.

ScienceEpisode 417:33

Simple Machines: Tools That Make Work Easier

Maya and Leo explore the six simple machines — wheel and axle, lever, inclined plane, pulley, wedge, and screw — from Archimedes to pyramid ramps to the scissors on your desk, and learn ten words along the way.

Full episode

7:33 · Story, knowledge, and vocabulary

Download full episode

Words in This Episode

2:11 · Slow vocabulary review

Download words review
Words in this episode
work
the amount of energy it takes to move an object“pushing a heavy box across the room takes a lot of work.”
force
a push or a pull that can move something or change its direction“the wind's force pushed the sailboat across the lake.”
lever
a long stiff bar that rests on a support and is used to lift heavy objects“a seesaw is a lever that children use to lift each other up and down.”
fulcrum
the fixed support that a lever pushes down against“on a seesaw, the bar in the middle that holds up the board is the fulcrum.”
inclined plane
a sloping surface with one end higher than the other, like a ramp“movers use an inclined plane to roll heavy boxes up into a truck.”
wedge
a tool with a sharp edge that is made for splitting objects apart“an axe is a wedge that splits firewood into pieces.”
pulley
a wheel with a groove around its edge that holds a rope and is used to lift things“a flag is raised to the top of the flagpole with a pulley.”
axle
the rod in the center of a wheel that the wheel turns around“the wheels of a bicycle spin smoothly on their axles.”
perpendicular
meeting at a right angle, like the corner of a square“the two edges of a book cover are perpendicular to each other.”
compound
made by joining two or more parts together“a pair of scissors is a compound machine built from wedges and a lever.”
Transcript

Maya: Leo, quick challenge. Can you lift the front of a car with your bare hands?

Leo: No way. A car weighs like a ton.

Maya: What if I hand you a long metal bar and a wooden block?

Leo: A crowbar! I could slide one end under the car, rest the bar on the block, and push down on the other end. The car would lift right up.

Maya: Exactly. And that simple trick is thousands of years old. Today we're talking about simple machines, the most important inventions humans ever made.

Leo: Six of them, right? I remember from science class. Wheel and axle, lever, inclined plane, pulley, wedge, and screw.

Maya: That's the whole team. A simple machine is a basic tool with few or no moving parts, and its job is to make work easier.

Leo: When scientists say work, do they mean homework?

Maya: Ha, no. In science, work is the amount of energy it takes to move an object. Simple machines don't remove the work. They let you trade a big force for a smaller one, or push in a more comfortable direction.

Leo: So my crowbar didn't lift the car for free. I just pushed down with a smaller force over a longer distance.

Maya: Precisely. Some simple machines move a force from one place to another. Others change the direction of a force. And some increase the size of a force.

Leo: Let's start with the most famous one, the wheel and axle. It's probably the best known of all six.

Maya: A wheel is a round frame that turns on a rod in the middle called the axle. Push the wheel, and the axle turns with it. Or push the axle, and the wheel turns.

Leo: Cars, bicycles, rolling pins, Ferris wheels. They're everywhere.

Maya: And they go way back. The wheel and axle was first used by the Sumerians about five thousand years ago.

Leo: What about the lever? That's my crowbar.

Maya: A lever is a long stiff bar that rests on a support called the fulcrum. Push down on one end, and the other end lifts a heavy object up.

Leo: Why does the crowbar make me feel so strong?

Maya: Because a small force on your end becomes a bigger force on the car. And here's the trick. The closer the fulcrum sits to the heavy object, the easier the object is to lift.

Leo: So I want the wooden block close to the car, not close to me.

Maya: Right. Ancient peoples used levers to dig and to move the giant stones for buildings. Today we use them in door handles and bottle openers.

Leo: Number three is the inclined plane. That's just a fancy name for a ramp, isn't it?

Maya: It is a sloping surface with one end higher than the other. A ramp makes it easier to move something heavy from a low point to a higher point.

Leo: Because pushing a box up a ramp takes less force than lifting it straight up.

Maya: Exactly. Ancient Egyptians probably used inclined planes to move the huge stones for the pyramids. Today we see them as wheelchair ramps, escalators, and truck ramps.

Leo: Now the wedge. Knives, axes, chisels.

Maya: A wedge is like an inclined plane with both sides slanted. It's made for splitting things. It pushes force out along its two sides, and the object splits apart.

Leo: What about a doorstop? That's a wedge too.

Maya: It is, but it works the opposite way. A doorstop wedge stays still and uses friction to hold the door in place.

Leo: Next, the pulley. A flag going up a flagpole.

Maya: A pulley is a wheel with a groove around its edge, and a rope sits in the groove. It can lift or lower things, and using more than one pulley together makes the job even easier.

Leo: The trick is that you pull down over a long rope, and the object rises with less force.

Maya: Right. Pulleys raise flags, open and close window blinds, lift sails on ships, and move elevator cars between floors.

Leo: Last one, the screw. A jar lid is a screw?

Maya: Yes. A screw is an inclined plane wrapped around a center post. Turn it in a circle, and it climbs up or down. That turning motion becomes a straight-line push.

Leo: So wood screws, jar lids, light bulbs, faucets. All screws.

Maya: And there's a giant one called the screw pump. It's a huge screw lowered into water. When it turns, water climbs up the spiral and gets lifted to a new location. Two Archimedes screws on the River Thames in England generate electricity this way today.

Leo: The ancient Greek thinker Archimedes was actually the first to describe simple machines. They've been in use for thousands of years.

Maya: And they team up. Scissors are two wedges plus a lever. A lawnmower spins wedge-shaped blades on a wheel and axle.

Leo: A hand-cranked pencil sharpener combines a wheel and axle with a screw.

Maya: When simple machines join forces, we call the result a compound machine.

Leo: Okay, I'm sold. Simple machines really are simple, and really are everywhere. Let's slow down and learn the words that explain them.

Maya: Welcome to our vocabulary spotlight. I'll say a word, tell you what it means, and use it in a sentence. Word one is work, which means the amount of energy it takes to move an object. Pushing a heavy box across the room takes a lot of work.

Leo: Word two is force, which means a push or a pull that can move something or change its direction. The wind's force pushed the sailboat across the lake.

Maya: Word three is lever, which means a long stiff bar that rests on a support and is used to lift heavy objects. A seesaw is a lever that children use to lift each other up and down.

Leo: Word four is fulcrum, which means the fixed support that a lever pushes down against. On a seesaw, the bar in the middle that holds up the board is the fulcrum.

Maya: Word five is inclined plane, which means a sloping surface with one end higher than the other, like a ramp. Movers use an inclined plane to roll heavy boxes up into a truck.

Leo: Word six is wedge, which means a tool with a sharp edge that is made for splitting objects apart. An axe is a wedge that splits firewood into pieces.

Maya: Word seven is pulley, which means a wheel with a groove around its edge that holds a rope and is used to lift things. A flag is raised to the top of the flagpole with a pulley.

Leo: Word eight is axle, which means the rod in the center of a wheel that the wheel turns around. The wheels of a bicycle spin smoothly on their axles.

Maya: Word nine is perpendicular, which means meeting at a right angle, like the corner of a square. The two edges of a book cover are perpendicular to each other.

Leo: Word ten is compound, which means made by joining two or more parts together. A pair of scissors is a compound machine built from wedges and a lever.

Maya: Those are our ten words. Keep them in mind for the quiz.

Maya: Great job on the words. Now a quick quiz. Question one. What are the six simple machines?

Leo: Think for a moment. The answer is the wheel and axle, the lever, the inclined plane, the pulley, the wedge, and the screw.

Maya: Question two. True or false. A simple machine lets you lift something heavy without doing any work.

Leo: False. The work stays the same. A simple machine lets you use a smaller force, but you apply it over a longer distance, or it changes the direction of your push.

Maya: Last one. You want to lift a huge rock with a lever. Where should you put the fulcrum?

Leo: The answer is as close to the rock as possible. The closer the fulcrum is to the load, the easier it is to lift.

Maya: Perfect. Simple machines may have few moving parts, but they've shaped everything from the pyramids to the bicycle you ride to school.

Leo: So next time you open a door, turn a faucet, or ride an escalator, you'll know exactly which simple machine is helping you.

Maya: And that's a wrap. See you next time on The World, Word by Word.

Black Death: The Plague That Shook Europe

Maya and Leo trace the Black Death, the bubonic plague caused by Yersinia pestis bacteria that sailed into Sicily in 1347 and wiped out 30 to 60 percent of Europe's population — from its symptoms and spread to quarantine, medieval cures, and how antibiotics and vaccines treat it today.

a widespread outbreak of disease“The Black Death was one of the worst epidemics in history.”

a deadly disease that spreads quickly“The bubonic plague reached Europe on merchant ships.”
bacteria
tiny living things that can cause disease“Yersinia pestis is the bacteria that caused the Black Death.”

keeping sick people separated from healthy people“Quarantine helped slow the spread of the plague.”

Original Text

SOURCE: Gale In Context: Middle School — "Black Death" (Document Number GALE|QIBGRG638936443)
Source link used: https://link.gale.com/apps/doc/QIBGRG638936443/MSIC?u=sunnyvale15&sid=bookmark-MSIC&xid=f2f7de23
NOTE: The task's primary link (doc UHJZID603332926) hit the Gale institutional login wall
("Stratford Prepatory Blackford / Please enter your password"), so this backup Black Death
entry from the same Gale source (listed in mom's material document) was used instead.
Content matches the requested topic ("Black Death"). Transcribed below.

---

Black Death Victims in Florence, Italy [Image: The city of Florence, Italy, was ravaged
by the Black Death in 1348. Records from the time indicate 80 percent of the city's
residents died of the disease in that single year.]

In 1347, a deadly wave of disease began to sweep across Europe at a very fast rate.
The resulting epidemic, which lasted until the early 1350s, killed tens of millions
of people and is widely known as the Black Death. It was the first of several plagues
that struck Europe in the years and centuries that followed.

Fast Facts
- The main form of the disease that resulted in the Black Death is now known as
bubonic plague. It is caused by a type of bacteria known as Yersinia pestis.
- The Black Death began when these bacteria came to Europe aboard merchant ships.
- Fleas that lived on rats have often been blamed for the spread of the Black Death.
Some researchers now believe, however, that the disease was carried by the fleas
that lived on humans.
- Diseases similar to the Black Death still occur in some parts of the world. They
can be treated with antibiotics if they are detected early. There are also vaccines
available that can prevent the illness.

Origins
Historians and researchers are not entirely sure how the Black Death began. Most
believe the bacteria that caused the plague originally came from Asia. For many years,
researchers thought the disease came from China, but it now appears more likely that
it began in the grassland plains of central Asia. Historical accounts show that the
Black Death was almost certainly brought to Europe aboard merchant ships returning
from Asia. The sailors on board these ships were dead or sick when the ships arrived.

Symptoms
Records dating to the time of the Black Death describe tumors as one of the main
early signs of the disease. These usually appeared in the armpits or lower stomach.
As the disease advanced, it caused the skin around these tumors to turn black, which
is why the illness was called the Black Death. Modern researchers now know that these
tumors were actually swollen lymph nodes filled with blood and pus. The sores would
sometimes burst open and bleed. While this was very painful, people whose sores burst
open actually stood a better chance of surviving.

Beyond these black sores, people with the plague also developed chills, fever,
headaches, vomiting, and muscle aches. People suffering from the sickness became very
weak and tired, and were practically unable to move. It is thought that about half of
those who caught this form of the plague died. Death usually occurred within two to
seven days after symptoms first appeared. In some cases, however, people died within
a matter of hours.

The plague germs sometimes moved into the lungs or the bloodstream, which made people
vomit uncontrollably or cough up blood. About 90 percent of those whose lungs were
affected died of their illness. Practically no one who developed the blood-borne form
of the plague survived.

Spread and Death Toll
The Black Death began in October 1347 in what is now Italy. It began after several
merchant ships returning from Asia docked in the port city of Messina on the island
of Sicily. Many members of the ships' crews were already dead and the rest were sick
with the plague. The ships were ordered to be set adrift back into the water, but by
then it was too late. The disease had already begun to spread among the population.

From Sicily, the Black Death followed two paths as it quickly moved onto the European
mainland. One path led into France, Spain, and Portugal, and moved north from there,
reaching England by the summer of 1348. From England, it went into Scotland, Wales,
and Ireland. The second path moved northeast, through central and northern Europe. By
the time the plague ended in 1353, just about every country in Europe was affected.

The Black Death is often thought of as a disease that mainly affected peasants and the
poor. While this is true, many wealthy people and members of the nobility also died
from it. There is also a misconception that the Black Death only struck larger towns
and cities. In reality, it also reached many areas of the countryside. However, some
parts of Europe were lucky enough not to suffer outbreaks. The plague is known to
have devastated the population of one town while passing over nearby cities and towns.
Researchers are not sure why some areas were hit very hard while others escaped almost
entirely.

In total, the Black Death is thought to have resulted in between twenty million and
seventy-five million deaths, which represented between 30 and 60 percent of Europe's
population at the time. The epidemic was the deadliest event in European history up
to that point. It took several centuries for population levels to make a full recovery.

How People Reacted
Medieval people did not understand the real causes of the Black Death. At the time,
many believed it was a punishment from God and desperately prayed for the disease to
stop. Others thought that if they punished themselves, God would show mercy and halt
the plague. Some people traveled from town to town whipping themselves in public to
show God how much they were suffering. These efforts failed, as did every other
attempt to cure the disease.

Doctors of the era tried many different treatments, including using raw chickens,
cooked onions, sugary syrups, and herbs. Some people burned large fires in the hope
the flames would burn the plague from the air. Church bells were rung at all hours,
as people believed that loud sounds would also remove the disease.

Some Christians thought Europe's Jewish population was responsible for the Black Death.
They believed it was part of a Jewish plot to destroy Christianity. As word of this
idea spread, large numbers of Jews were rounded up, punished, and even killed. These
events mainly took place in France, Spain, and the Holy Roman Empire of central Europe.

Animals such as rats, cats, and dogs were also blamed. In fact, fleas were largely
responsible for carrying the disease from person to person. During the winter, the
rate at which the plague spread seemed to slow down because fleas are inactive during
the winter season.

Connections: Medieval Understanding of the Causes of the Black Death
In the fourteenth century, Europeans had no idea what really caused the Black Death.
The people of the era proposed many possible explanations, including the following:
- A punishment from God
- Unusual movements of the planets
- Air or water pollution
- A Jewish plot to destroy Christianity
- Animals including rats, cats, and dogs

The End of the Black Death
The Black Death finally began to slow down in 1352, although modern researchers are
unsure why. One of the most likely suggestions is that sick people began to be
quarantined, which helped prevent the plague from spreading. The Black Death
eventually stopped, but reoccurrences of the disease appeared again and again for
centuries. Some of these later epidemics were severe, but became less deadly as time
went on. Later outbreaks of the plague also tended to affect smaller geographic areas.
The last European plague outbreak is believed to have ended in the eighteenth century.

Words to Know
- blood-borne: A disease carried in the blood.
- epidemic: A widespread outbreak of disease.
- Medieval: Relating to the period of European history from about 500 to 1500 AD.
- misconception: A mistaken belief.
- nobility: A wealthy social class that ranked just below royalty in Medieval society.
- quarantine: The practice of isolating sick people and keeping them separated from
healthy people.
- tumor: An unusual growth on the body that often signals the presence of disease.

Source text supplied with episode 40

ScienceEpisode 397:21

Electricity and Magnetism

Electricity is moving charges, magnetism is the force they create, and together they power our modern world.

Full episode

7:21 · Story, knowledge, and vocabulary

Download full episode

Words in This Episode

2:00 · Slow vocabulary review

Download words review
Words in this episode
current
which is a noun meaning a flow of electric charge moving through something“the current flows through the wire and lights up the lamp.”
electron
which is a noun meaning a tiny particle inside an atom that has a negative electrical charge“electrons move through wires to make electric current.”
proton
which is a noun meaning a tiny particle inside an atom that has a positive electrical charge“protons and electrons have opposite charges, so they attract each other.”
particle
which is a noun meaning a very small piece of matter, like an atom, a proton, or an electron“dust floating in the air is made of tiny particles.”
static
which is an adjective meaning not moving, staying in one place“static electricity builds up when you rub a balloon on your hair.”
repel
which is a verb meaning to push something away with a force“two magnets with the same pole will repel each other.”
pole
which is a noun meaning one of the two ends of a magnet“the north pole of a magnet attracts the south pole of another magnet.”
magnetic field
which is a noun phrase meaning the invisible area around a magnet where its force can be felt“Earth is surrounded by a magnetic field that makes compasses point north.”
voltage
which is a noun meaning the electrical push that makes current flow through a wire“batteries with higher voltage can push a stronger current.”
electromagnet
which is a noun meaning a magnet made by running electric current around a piece of metal“an electromagnet in a scrapyard can lift heavy cars.”
Transcript

Maya: Hey everyone, welcome back to The World, Word by Word. I'm Maya.

Leo: And I'm Leo. Today we're talking about two forces you use every single day, probably without even thinking about them. Electricity and magnetism.

Maya: Right. You flip a light switch, your phone charges, you stick a magnet on the fridge. But what is electricity, really? It all starts very, very small.

Leo: Super small. Everything around you, including you, is made of atoms. And inside atoms there are tiny charged particles. Two of them matter here. Protons, which have a positive charge, and electrons, which have a negative charge.

Maya: And there's a simple rule for charges. Opposite charges attract. Same charges push each other away. It's a bit like magnets on a fridge, which we'll get to in a minute.

Leo: Electricity is energy from those moving or resting charges. And it comes in two forms. Static electricity, where the charges are standing still and piling up, and current electricity, where the charges are moving in the same direction.

Maya: Static electricity is the one that gives you a little shock when you touch a doorknob in winter. Here's what happens. When you rub a balloon on your hair, you move electrons onto the balloon. The charges pile up on the surface with nowhere to go.

Leo: And they repel each other. That's why your hair stands up after you take off a hat. The charges on your hairs are all the same, and same charges push each other away.

Maya: Current electricity is the kind that flows through wires to power your house. The moving charges need a path, like a wire, and they need a push. That push is called voltage. Think of voltage as the push that gets the electric river flowing.

Leo: There are two kinds of current. Direct current, or D C, where electrons flow in one direction, like from a battery in a flashlight. And alternating current, or A C, where the electrons keep switching direction. Your home outlets and power lines use A C.

Maya: Now, one of the most famous stories in science is about electricity. Back in seventeen fifty two, Benjamin Franklin wanted to prove that lightning is actually electricity. So he flew a kite in a thunderstorm, with a metal key tied to the string.

Leo: And when the storm clouds sent electricity down the wet string, the key gave him a shock. Proof. Lightning is electricity. One lightning bolt is so powerful it could power two hundred thousand homes.

Maya: But here's the really cool part of our story today. Electricity and magnetism are secretly connected. When electric charges move, they create a magnetic field. When a magnetic field moves, it can create electricity. They're two sides of the same coin.

Leo: Let's talk about magnets. A magnet has two ends, called poles. One pole pulls things in, the other pushes things away. And around the magnet there is an invisible area of force called the magnetic field.

Maya: Some materials are naturally magnetic, like iron ore. These are called ferromagnets. But here's the wild thing. You can also make a magnet with electricity. Run an electric current through a wire wrapped around a piece of iron, and boom, you get an electromagnet. The study of this connection is called electromagnetism.

Leo: And the biggest magnet of all might be right under your feet. Earth itself is surrounded by a magnetic field. Deep inside Earth, the core is made mostly of iron. The electrons in that iron line up, and that makes the whole planet magnetic, with a North Pole and a South Pole.

Maya: That's why a compass needle always points north. It's responding to Earth's magnetic field.

Leo: Electricity and magnetism are everywhere in your daily life. Electricity powers your microwave, your computer, your lights. Electricity plus magnetism together run radios and hospital scanners like the M R I machine. And electromagnets help store data on hard drives.

Maya: So remember this one sentence. Electricity is moving charges, magnetism is the force they create, and together they power our modern world. Simple as that.

Leo: And now, Maya, I think it's time for our favorite part.

Maya: The vocabulary. Slow down, listen carefully, and repeat after us if you can.

Maya: Welcome to the words section. We'll teach you ten important words from today's episode. Listen carefully, and say each word with us.

Leo: Word one is current, which is a noun meaning a flow of electric charge moving through something. For example, the current flows through the wire and lights up the lamp.

Maya: Word two is electron, which is a noun meaning a tiny particle inside an atom that has a negative electrical charge. For example, electrons move through wires to make electric current.

Leo: Word three is proton, which is a noun meaning a tiny particle inside an atom that has a positive electrical charge. For example, protons and electrons have opposite charges, so they attract each other.

Maya: Word four is particle, which is a noun meaning a very small piece of matter, like an atom, a proton, or an electron. For example, dust floating in the air is made of tiny particles.

Leo: Word five is static, which is an adjective meaning not moving, staying in one place. For example, static electricity builds up when you rub a balloon on your hair.

Maya: Word six is repel, which is a verb meaning to push something away with a force. For example, two magnets with the same pole will repel each other.

Leo: Word seven is pole, which is a noun meaning one of the two ends of a magnet. For example, the north pole of a magnet attracts the south pole of another magnet.

Maya: Word eight is magnetic field, which is a noun phrase meaning the invisible area around a magnet where its force can be felt. For example, Earth is surrounded by a magnetic field that makes compasses point north.

Leo: Word nine is voltage, which is a noun meaning the electrical push that makes current flow through a wire. For example, batteries with higher voltage can push a stronger current.

Maya: Word ten is electromagnet, which is a noun meaning a magnet made by running electric current around a piece of metal. For example, an electromagnet in a scrapyard can lift heavy cars.

Leo: Let's review the ten words one more time. Current, electron, proton, particle, static, repel, pole, magnetic field, voltage, and electromagnet. Great job. You're one word richer, ten times over.

Leo: Three, two, one. Quiz time.

Maya: Question one. What are the two forms of electricity, and how are they different?

Leo: Take a moment to think. The answer: static electricity, where charges pile up at rest, and current electricity, where charges move in the same direction.

Maya: Question two. What did Benjamin Franklin's kite experiment prove?

Leo: It proved that lightning is a form of electricity. The metal key on his kite string gave him an electric shock.

Maya: Question three. Why does a compass needle point north?

Leo: Because Earth is surrounded by a magnetic field, created by the iron in Earth's core. The compass needle follows that field to the North Pole.

Maya: How did you do? Three out of three? Awesome. Thanks for listening to The World, Word by Word.

Leo: See you next time. Bye.

TITLE: Artificial Intelligence
Source: Gale In Context: Middle School

Artificial intelligence refers to machines that are capable of intelligent functions in the same way as humans. Artificial intelligence is studied in a number of academic fields such as computer science, philosophy, mathematics, psychology, and engineering. The main goal of artificial intelligence research is to create machines that can learn and problem-solve the way that people can. Early models of artificial intelligence performed straightforward human tasks such as calculating math problems. Modern artificial intelligence technologies have advanced to include automated customer service, autonomous vehicles, and intelligent robotics. Some scientists hope to create a conscious machine capable of free thought and emotion. Others believe such a creation would be unethical.

Fast Facts
- IBM created an artificially intelligent question-answering system named Watson that won the quiz show Jeopardy in 2011.
- In 2014, a Russian-created robot named Eugene Goostman tricked people into thinking it was a real thirteen-year-old boy.
- The first multi-layered neural network, named Cognitron, was created in 1975 and gave machines the power to self-organize.

Background
Ideas about machines with the potential for intelligence have existed for thousands of years. Ancient Greek mythology featured stories about robotic creatures. Modern notions of artificial intelligence are rooted in the writings of classical philosophers. These thinkers tried to formulate a symbolic system to describe human thought. Such philosophical ideas spilled over into other academic spheres, such as math, physics, and computer science. Scholars began to theorize about the possibility of creating intelligence. Researchers from a number of scientific fields undertook multiple experiments between the 1940s and 1960s to test this hypothesis.

Early research in neural networks provided some of the first breakthroughs in the science of artificial intelligence. Neural networks were electrical systems that used interconnected nodes to send and receive signals. They were modeled to mimic the biological process of neurons in the brain. Artificial neural networks operated through algorithms, which helped them recognize patterns and correlations in the data provided. Through continuous processing of this data, the system was constantly learning and could improve its function, much like the brain. These early experiments led to the development of computer technology.

One of the earliest pioneers of artificial intelligence was British mathematician and computer scientist Alan Turing. Turing believed it was possible for machines to learn how to solve problems the way that people do. He created a machine called the Turing Machine, which is considered the predecessor of the modern computer. Turing came up with a number of experiments to test the intelligence of his machines. He also developed the Turing test, a test designed to gauge a machine's intelligence. A machine was deemed intelligent if it was able to exhibit intelligence that was indistinguishable from that of a person.

The field of artificial intelligence was formally established at a conference hosted by cognitive scientists John McCarthy and Marvin Minsky at Dartmouth College in 1956. This gathering saw the first use of the term artificial intelligence, which was coined by McCarthy. The very first artificial intelligence program was introduced at the conference. Known as the Logic Theorist, the program was capable of mimicking the problem-solving skills of a human. The events and discussions held at this conference shaped the future of artificial intelligence for years to come.

Early Advances
Artificial intelligence saw many advances over the next few decades. Scientists improved algorithms and opened the possibility of artificial intelligence performing functions such as spoken language interpretation. The success of these projects convinced the federal government to begin funding artificial intelligence research in natural language processing, abstract thinking, and self-recognition.

The main obstacle to further advancements in artificial intelligence was computer data storage. Early computers did not have the power to store enough information to process complex algorithms. It was not until the late 1980s that computer scientists began to make breakthroughs in computer processing power. Scientists developed a type of machine learning called deep learning. It trained computers to perform human tasks such as identifying images and recognizing speech. Deep learning differed from earlier machine learning methods. Instead of using predefined processing paths to organize and execute data, deep learning taught computers to learn on their own. All that was required was a set of basic parameters regarding the data. The computer would then process the patterns of data in layers and learn from each layer of processing.

Artificial intelligence research funding became more and more privatized in the ensuing decades. Private technology corporations funded their own research projects, eliminating limits set by government funding. As a result, artificial intelligence research boomed. Between the 1990s and 2000s, advances in computer science allowed for great leaps in artificial intelligence development.

Connections: Artificial Intelligence in Fiction
Artificial intelligence has appeared in many works of fiction throughout history. These depictions have greatly informed society's perception of artificial intelligence throughout the years. Intelligent robots, computers, cars, operating systems, and other machines have driven the plots of many science fiction books, films, and television shows. Popular culture often takes one of two approaches to stories featuring artificial intelligence. In dystopian works, all possible things have gone wrong with artificial intelligence, pitting man against machine. In utopian works, humans and artificially intelligent machines live in harmony.

Deep Blue Wins
In 1997, IBM created a chess playing program named Deep Blue. The program was able to defeat world chess champion and grand master Gary Kasparov. This victory showed that artificial intelligence engineers were making major progress toward machines with the capacity for decision making. Further, scientists were also developing artificially intelligent machines capable of understanding and exhibiting emotions. The MIT artificial intelligence lab created a robot named Kismet that was able to recognize and mimic emotions.

By the twenty-first century, research in artificial intelligence was closer than ever to reaching its goals. Advances in computer storage limits rapidly expanded the memory and speed of computers. As computer technologies began to saturate everyday life through mobile technology and smartphones, huge amounts of data became available for use in artificial intelligence development.

Modern Artificial Technology
Modern artificial technology takes many forms. Artificially intelligent machines can be programmed by companies to answer customer questions online and by phone. Artificial intelligence has also given rise to the possibility of driverless vehicles, which are capable of self-navigating roads. The primary goal of many artificial intelligence developers was to simplify many aspects of society. Some artificial intelligence researchers also hoped to create a machine that possessed consciousness. This would mean the machine could think freely and critically and come to decisions without human input. Some scientists worried about the ethics of such a creation, however, and such a machine was many decades away from coming to fruition.

Words to Know
- algorithms: A step-by-step procedure for solving a problem.
- ethics: A system of moral principles.
- neuron: A nerve cell that transmits signals throughout the body.
- node: A point of communication.

RETRIEVED: 2026-09-09 (PT)
SUBJECT: Science
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

Self-Driving Cars

Cockpit of Self-Driving Vehicle [Image: The empty cockpit of a self-driving vehicle.]

Self-driving cars may seem like an idea out of a science fiction novel. However, they are quickly becoming a reality. Self-driving cars are sometimes called autonomous vehicles. The word autonomous means "having the power to govern itself."

Many people are interested in the idea of self-driving cars. Some experts believe that these vehicles could help reduce the number of car accidents that occur every year. Others worry that self-driving cars might be dangerous. Some people also worry about how self-driving cars would affect the economy.

Fast Facts
- Self-driving cars developed by Waymo had already driven twenty million miles on public roads as of 2020.
- Cities across the United States are already testing self-driving cars. Some of these cities include Boston, Massachusetts; Denver, Colorado; and Washington, DC. Self-driving cars are also being tested in Singapore and Paris, France.
- Escape artist Harry Houdini did not like that the Houdina Radio Control Company had a similar name. After Francis P. Houdina began receiving Houdini's mail, Houdini broke into the company's offices and accused them of unlawfully using his name to conduct their business. However, Houdina was the founder's real last name.

History of Self-Driving Cars
Self-driving cars might seem like a hi-tech idea. Yet, people have been interested in self-driving cars since the first automobiles became popular. In 1925, Francis P. Houdina built a radio-controlled car. Houdina had been an electrical engineer in the army. After leaving the service, he founded the Houdina Radio Control Company. Houdina's vehicle was a Chandler sedan that he modified to include an electrical engine with an antenna. This allowed someone to control the car using radio waves.

Houdina demonstrated the car on Broadway in New York City. A person riding in a car behind the Chandler controlled the vehicle's movements. The operator could even honk the car's horn. But the car's demonstration ended when it crashed into another vehicle.

For years, driverless cars were seen as a gimmick. They were used by companies to grab people's attention and promote other products. Still, the idea of self-driving vehicles interested many people. In the 1960s, computer scientist John McCarthy wrote an essay called "Computer-Controlled Cars." He explained how computers might one day be used to "see" the road around a vehicle. In the 1990s, computers were more common and more advanced. This led more scientists to think about how they could use computers to create self-driving cars.

Scientists were not the only people interested in self-driving cars. The military also believed they would be useful. Driverless vehicles could save lives in dangerous military situations. The Defense Advanced Research Projects Agency (DARPA) is part of the Department of Defense. DARPA began researching self-driving cars in the early 2000s. But the agency soon went to outside companies to see if they could develop cars that could drive themselves. DARPA even held a contest in 2004 to see which design could make it through the Mojave Desert. The winning car only made it about eight miles before it caught on fire.

Some car companies were able to make aspects of driving autonomous. For instance, several companies introduced features that allowed cars to parallel park themselves. Others had sensors that told the driver when there was a car in another lane of traffic. However, these were not true self-driving cars. This is because a driver still had to be in control of the car at all times.

Levels of Autonomous Cars
- Level 0: A driver must control all major systems of the car.
- Level 1: Certain aspects of the car, such as automatic braking, are controlled by the car. However, they can only be controlled one at a time. A human driver controls most aspects of the car.
- Level 2: Level-2 vehicles can control at least two systems, such as braking and steering, at the same time. Human supervision is still needed to operate the car safely.
- Level 3: The car can control all the critical functions to operate the vehicle safely in most conditions. A driver must be ready to take over when alerted, though.
- Level 4: A level-4 car can operate autonomously in some situations but not all.
- Level 5: The car can operate itself in all conditions without the need of a human driver.

Around 2009, Google began working on fully autonomous cars. Google is owned by a company called Alphabet Inc. Alphabet eventually created another company called Waymo to handle the self-driving car designs. Waymo began testing self-driving cars in various locations. By the 2010s, many major car companies, including Ford and BMW, started developing self-driving cars, too. Technology companies like Tesla and Apple also became interested in the idea. Rollouts of new semi-autonomous vehicles and related services continued into the early 2020s. General Motors subsidiary Cruise created its first driverless car not equipped with a steering wheel or pedals in 2020. That same year, Waymo launched a driverless taxi service for public use in Phoenix, Arizona. The amount of interest in and development of self-driving cars means it is only a matter of time before people everywhere are using them.

How Self-Driving Cars Work
It is difficult to explain how self-driving cars work. There are different levels of autonomous vehicles. However, a truly autonomous car relies on many sensors, computers, and cameras to work. Most of these vehicles use cameras to view the area around the car. Some also use lasers and radio waves to determine distances between objects. This information makes an internal map. This map is used by the car's software to help make decisions. Driverless cars also need a global positioning system (GPS) to move the car from one place to another.

Other details about driverless cars largely depend on the company that produces them. Some companies are focusing on electric self-driving cars. Others believe that hybrid electric cars that run on electricity stored in batteries and some gasoline will work best for daily use.

Pros and Cons to Self-Driving Cars
Many people believe that self-driving cars could be beneficial. They think these cars could reduce the number of car accidents. About 1.35 million people die in car accidents worldwide each year, according to the Association for Safe International Road Travel. Many car accidents are caused by human error. Driverless cars would reduce such errors. Many people also believe that self-driving cars could help the environment. Electric self-driving cars would not need to use any gasoline. Burning gasoline in regular cars creates harmful air pollution.

Still, not everyone is convinced. Some people worry that driverless cars will not be able to respond to changing situations fast enough to prevent an accident. In fact, an Arizona woman was killed in early 2018 after she was hit by a self-driving car. The car was being tested by the company Uber.

Other people worry about what will happen to the economy. Some experts believe that there are trillions of dollars to be made from self-driving cars. Yet, these vehicles could also cost people their jobs. Driverless cars would mean that taxi drivers, truck drivers, and car insurance salespeople could be out of work.

Words to Know
- antenna: A device used to send and receive radio signals.
- gimmick: A way of getting people's attention.
- global positioning system (GPS): A system that uses radio waves from satellites to identify a person's location and provide directions.
- sensor: A device that detects light, movement, heat, or another variable and then reacts to the change in the variable in a certain way.
- software: A computer program that performs certain functions.

the act of finding something nobody knew before“The discovery of radium changed medicine forever.”
Nobel Prize
one of the world’s most famous international awards“Marie Curie is the only woman to win Nobel Prizes in two different sciences.”

# Columbia Electronic Encyclopedia 对应条目（via FactMonster）作为来源：
# https://www.factmonster.com/encyclopedia/science/bios/physics/curie
# 内容核对：条目确为 Marie Curie（居里夫人）及 Curie 家族，符合主题，未发生链接贴错。
# 抓取日期：2026-09-09（PT）

---
Curie, family of French scientists. Pierre Curie, 1859–1906, scientist, and his wife, Marie Sklodowska Curie, 1867–1934, chemist and physicist, b. Warsaw, are known for their work on radioactivity and on radium. The Curies' daughter Irène (see under Joliot-Curie, family) was also a scientist.

Pierre Curie's early work dealt with crystallography and with the effects of temperature on magnetism; he discovered (1883) and, with his brother Jacques Curie, investigated piezoelectricity (a form of electric polarity) in crystals. Marie Sklodowska's interest in science was stimulated by her father, a professor of physics in Warsaw. In 1891 she went to Paris to continue her studies at the Sorbonne. In 1895 she married Pierre Curie and engaged in independent research in his laboratory at the municipal school of physics and chemistry where Pierre was director of laboratories (from 1882) and professor (from 1895).

Following A. H. Becquerel's discovery of radioactivity, Mme Curie began to investigate uranium, a radioactive element found in pitchblende. In 1898 she reported a probable new element in pitchblende, and Pierre Curie joined in her research. They discovered (1898) both polonium and radium, laboriously isolated one gram of radium salts from about eight tons of pitchblende, and determined the atomic weights and properties of radium and polonium. The Curies refused to patent their processes or otherwise to profit from the commercial exploitation of radium. For their work on radioactivity they shared with Becquerel the 1903 Nobel Prize in Physics.

The Sorbonne created (1904) a special chair of physics for Pierre Curie; Marie Curie was appointed his successor after his death in a street accident. She also retained her professorship (assumed in 1900) at the normal school at Sèvres and continued her research. In 1910 she isolated (with André Debierne) metallic radium. As the recipient of the 1911 Nobel Prize in Chemistry she was the first person to be awarded a second Nobel Prize. She was made director of the laboratory of radioactivity at the Curie Institute of Radium, established jointly by the Univ. of Paris and the Pasteur Institute, for research on radioactivity and for radium therapy.

During World War I, Mme Curie devoted her energies to providing radiological services for hospitals. In 1921 a gram of radium, a gift from American women, was presented to her by President Harding; this she accepted in behalf of the Curie Institute. A second gram, presented in 1929, was given by Mme Curie to the newly founded Curie Institute in Warsaw. Five years later she died from the effects of radioactivity. In 1995 Marie and Pierre Curie's ashes were enshrined in the Panthéon, Paris; she was the first woman to be honored so in her own right.

Among the numerous and valuable writings of the Curies are Marie Curie's doctoral dissertation, Radioactive Substances (2 vol., 1902; tr. 1961); Traité de radioactivité (1910); Radioactivité (1935); and her biography of Pierre Curie (1923, tr. 1923). Pierre Curie's collected works appeared in 1908. A biography of Marie Curie was written by her daughter Ève Curie (tr. 1937). See also biographies by R. W. Reid (1974), F. Giroud (tr. 1986), S. Quinn (1995), and B. Goldsmith (2004); S. Emling, Marie Curie and Her Daughters (2012).

The Columbia Electronic Encyclopedia, 6th ed. Copyright © 2025, Columbia University Press. All rights reserved.

Periodic Table - Document - Gale In Context: Middle School

Reading Levels: 860L, 1100L

Full Text:

[Image: The periodic table of the elements including new elements nihonium, moscovium, tennessine, and oganesson with atomic number, element symbol, element name, and atomic weight.]

The periodic table is a list of the known chemical elements on Earth. The table is arranged into columns and rows. The columns are called groups. The table's rows are called periods. The list of elements is ordered by increasing atomic number and atomic mass. Elements with similar chemical behaviors are placed in the same column. The rows are ordered so that the metal elements are placed on the left, while the nonmetal elements are placed on the right. This arrangement helps scientists predict the behavior of unfamiliar or unknown elements. An element's placement in the periodic table helps determine what chemical reactions it takes part in and whether it can form chemical bonds with other elements. As of 2018, the periodic table had 118 confirmed elements.

## Fast Facts

- Although Dmitri Mendeleev is called the father of the periodic table, he did not create the first periodic table. His creation was simply the first to earn scientific credibility.
- Ninety-two of the elements in the periodic table occur naturally, the others are man-made.
- Technetium (Tc) was the first artificially produced element.
- Most of the elements in the periodic table are metals.

## History of the Periodic Table

Russian chemist Dmitri Mendeleev is considered by many to be the father of the periodic table. He gave order to the elements known during his lifetime. Before his work, scientists grouped the elements into gases, metals, nonmetals, and earths. A few chemists had discovered some relationships between various groups of chemical elements, but no one had come up with a way to organize this information effectively.

While employed as a university professor in Russia in the 1860s, Mendeleev began developing a Russian-language organic chemistry textbook. As he worked on this textbook, he decided to come up with an orderly arrangement for the known elements. At the time, only about half of the chemical elements were known to scientists and some of the information about the known elements was wrong.

Mendeleev completed his two-volume textbook, Principles of Chemistry, between 1868 and 1870. While compiling the textbook, he wrote the individual properties of each element on cards to keep track of the information. He ordered the cards by increasing atomic weight. While putting the cards in order, he noticed a pattern in the physical and chemical properties of each element. He decided to plot out a table of elements arranged by atomic weight and valence. Valence describes the combining ability of an atom and is determined by how many electrons it loses, gains, or shares when reacting with another atom. Mendeleev published his first complete table in 1869.

Mendeleev's table was unique because it left spaces for elements that the chemist believed existed but had not yet been discovered. It also assembled the elements into groups, or chemical families, by column, ignoring some of the accepted atomic weights of elements. Mendeleev's table correctly guessed that the atomic weight of several elements was incorrect. He was able to determine this by the chemical properties of each element, and he grouped elements with similar properties into the same columns. Mendeleev's prediction about undiscovered elements was proven true in the years to follow, and he revised his table several times to reflect new information.

As time went on, scientists rearranged the periodic table to be organized by increasing atomic number, which is the number of an atom's protons, instead of atomic weight. Further scientific discoveries eventually led American chemist Glenn Seaborg to rearrange the periodic table of elements in the 1940s. Seaborg's revisions were the last major changes made to the table for many decades. This version became the standard model for teaching the properties of the elements.

## Connections: New Elements Added

In June 2016, scientists added four newly discovered elements to the periodic table. Nihonium (Nh), moscovium (Mc), tennessine (Ts), and oganesson (Og) were given the respective atomic numbers of 113, 115, 117, and 118. The elements' addition completed the seventh row of the periodic table. The four elements were not found in nature but were created in labs. They are difficult to reproduce because they decay very quickly. Nihonium was named for the Japanese phrase nihon, which means "land of the rising sun." Moscovium and tennessine were named in honor of their places of discovery: the Russian city of Moscow for moscovium and the state of Tennessee for tennessine. Oganesson was named for Russian professor Yuri Oganessian, who helped discover superheavy elements.

## Structure of the Periodic Table

The periodic table contains a great deal of information and requires careful study to understand completely. Most periodic tables are color coded to help viewers identify elements with similar properties. The elements are arranged into individual squares and given a one- or two-letter abbreviation, referred to as its atomic symbol. In each square, the number directly above the atomic symbol is its atomic number, or proton number. The atomic number defines the element. The number below the symbol is the element's atomic mass, also known as atomic weight.

The standard version of the periodic table arranges the elements by their recurring properties. The table is organized into groups (columns) and periods (rows) and has eighteen groups and seven periods. Elements in the same group share similar electron shell structures and have the same valence. The elements in each group behave similarly in chemical reactions, which is why they are grouped together. The groups have been given varying official group names over the years based on their collective properties. For example, group one is referred to as the alkali metals, group two is the alkaline earth metals, group seventeen is the halogens, and group eighteen is the noble gases.

The atoms of the elements in the same period share the same ground state electron energy level, which is the lowest energy level of electrons before they enter an excited state. Alongside groups and periods, the periodic table can be divided into four standard blocks: s-block, p-block, d-block, and f-block. An element's block is determined by its highest-energy electrons, which decide the atomic orbital to which the element belongs.

The periodic table also organizes the elements by other chemical trends, including ionic radius, atomic radius, ionization energy, electronegativity, and electron affinity. Atomic radius refers to half the distance between the nuclei of two atoms that are barely touching. Ionic radius is calculated similarly and measures half the distance between two ions barely making contact. The atomic and ionic radiuses increase from top to bottom of an element group. These measurements decrease from left to right in each period.

Electronegativity is an atom's power to attract electrons and form chemical bonds. Atoms with higher electronegativity form electron bonds more easily. Electronegativity gets smaller moving down the groups and gets bigger moving left to right across the periods.

Removing electrons from atoms or ions requires energy. This energy is called the ionization energy, and it is used to remove an electron from a gaseous atom or ion. Ionization energy decreases from top to bottom of the groups and increases from left to right across periods.

Finally, electron affinity refers to an atom's ability to receive an electron. The six noble gases—helium (He), neon (Ne), argon (Ar), krypton (Kr), xenon (Xe), and radon (Rn)—have very little electron affinity. For the rest of the table, electron affinity decreases moving down a group and increases moving across a period.

## Words to Know

- atomic orbital: A mathematical function describing the state of an electron in an atom.
- atomic weight: Also known as atomic mass; the combined mass of protons and neutrons in an atom.
- electron: A negatively charged particle that orbits the nucleus of an atom.
- electron shell: A group of electrons with similar nuclear orbits.
- neutron: A neutrally charged particle found within an atom's nucleus.
- proton: A positively charged particle found within an atom's nucleus.

# Big Bang Theory

Full Text:

Expansion and Evolution of the Universe. A cosmic timeline shows the evolution of stars, the galaxy, and the universe.

The big bang theory is the most likely scientific explanation about how the universe was born. Scientists believe that approximately 13.8 billion years ago all the matter in the universe was squeezed into a very small and very hot point. Suddenly, the point began to quickly expand outward in all directions. The early universe was so hot that atoms could not form and light could not shine. It took many millions and billions of years for the universe to expand and cool enough for galaxies, stars, and planets to form.

## Fast Facts

- The astronomers who discovered the cosmic microwave background in 1964 at first thought the sound was caused by birds nesting in the equipment.
- Famous scientist Albert Einstein did not believe in the big bang theory when he first heard of it, but he later came to accept it.
- Georges Lemaître also referred to the idea of a primordial atom as a "cosmic egg."

In the early years of the twentieth century, astronomers noticed that all of the other galaxies in the sky seemed to be moving away from our own galaxy. They determined that the galaxies must have been much closer together at one time. Something very powerful must have happened long ago to make them separate. Astronomers eventually gave this event a nickname: the "big bang." Although some scientists were skeptical of the theory at first, many had come to accept it by the 1960s. It was then that astronomers noticed dim radiation spread throughout the most distant parts of the sky. Astronomers considered the discovery of this background radiation as strong evidence in favor of the big bang theory.

## The Idea Is Formed

By the 1920s, astronomers knew the universe was a very big place, but they had no idea how incredibly large it really was. When they looked at the night sky through telescopes, astronomers would often see small, fuzzy patches of light. They thought these patches were giant clouds of gas and dust in our own galaxy, the Milky Way. In 1923, an American astronomer named Edwin Hubble discovered that these "clouds" were actually other galaxies. Even more surprising, he found that they all seemed to be moving away from the Milky Way at very high speeds. The galaxies that were farthest away seemed to be moving the fastest.

Around the same time that Hubble was making his discovery, a Belgian Catholic priest named Georges Lemaître had come up with his own idea about the origin of the universe. Lemaître, who was also an astronomer, believed that the universe began as a single, primordial atom that exploded outward. This small atom expanded very quickly and eventually became the modern universe. Hubble's observations seemed to support Lemaître's theory and, as a result, many scientists began to believe in the idea of an expanding universe.

## Connections: The Big Bang in Ancient Myth

Most ancient people had myths and legends about how the universe and their world were created. Many of these stories involved a god or spiritual being who played a role in creating the earth and the sky. However, one myth found in one of the world's oldest religions has some similarities to the big bang theory. The Hindu religion originated in ancient India. A Hindu religious book written about 1,400 years ago tells the story that the universe was born from a cosmic egg. This egg grew from a very small point called a bindu. The universe began expanding and eventually grew to become the modern universe. The Hindu myth goes on to say that one day the process will reverse. The universe will begin to contract and return to the original cosmic egg. From there, it will be reborn and continue the cycle of death and rebirth forever.

## Some Proof Found

Some scientists did not believe the universe was born from a primordial atom. A British astronomer named Fred Hoyle was very outspoken against the idea. Hoyle believed the universe was not born but had always existed in a "steady state." He said the reason it seemed to be expanding was because of the creation of new stars and galaxies. Hoyle mocked the primordial atom theory by referring to it as a "big bang." The term was meant as an insult, but instead, it became the official name of the theory.

Although many scientists had begun to believe in the big bang theory, there was a problem they could not seem to solve. If the universe had been born from a big bang, there should be a large amount of distant radiation in the sky. However, scientists could not find any sign of such radiation. Then, in 1964, astronomers using a type of telescope that detects radio waves noticed something strange. They discovered an unusual buzzing sound that seemed to come from all parts of the sky. They checked their data and realized that they had found the echo of radiation left behind by the big bang. They called this radiation the cosmic microwave background. This discovery was a large step toward proving the big bang theory. Modern scientists still have many questions, but the big bang seems the most likely explanation of how the universe began.

## The Theory

According to the big bang theory, all the matter and energy in the universe was squeezed into a very small and very hot point around 13.8 billion years ago. This point was known as a singularity. Astronomers do not know what triggered the event, but they believe that the singularity suddenly began to expand outward. The name "big bang" is a little misleading because what happened was not an explosion. It was more like a very fast and violent expansion. The temperature of the new universe reached about ten billion degrees Fahrenheit. This was so hot that subatomic particles such as protons, neutrons, and electrons could not join together to form atoms. Subatomic particles are the building blocks that make up atoms. Atoms have a core of protons and neutrons that is surrounded by a shell of electrons.

Because the immense heat would not allow atoms to form, the early universe was filled with roaming electrons. These electrons scattered the light, causing the universe to seem like it was covered in a thick fog. Something similar happens on Earth when a cloud of water droplets scatters sunlight. Then, around 380,000 years after the big bang, the universe cooled enough to allow electrons to form atoms. With no roaming electrons to act as an obstacle, the first light of the universe began to shine. This was the light detected in 1964 as the cosmic microwave background.

The universe kept expanding and cooling, but it remained very hot for millions of years. It was approximately 100 million years after the big bang before the first stars and galaxies were able to form. These first stars and galaxies are long since dead; they burned out a long time ago. The most distant objects modern astronomers can see today formed when the universe was about 600 million years old. The sun, Earth, and all the planets in our solar system did not begin to form until about 9 billion years after the big bang.

## Words to Know

- primordial: Something so old it is believed to have existed from the beginning of time.
- radiation: Powerful energy released by very bright or very hot objects.
- radio waves: A form of radiation with a very low frequency that is invisible to the naked eye.
- theory: A scientifically possible explanation to answer a question or describe how something works.

Original Text

Columbia Electronic Encyclopedia, 6th ed. — "galaxy" (via FactMonster)
"galaxy, large aggregation of stars, gas, dust, and usually dark matter, typically
containing billions of stars. Recognition that galaxies are independent star systems
outside the Milky Way came from a study of the Andromeda Galaxy (1926-29) by Edwin P.
Hubble that indicated the great distances at which this and other galaxies are located.
Previously, the galaxies had been classified with the luminous gas clouds, or bright
nebulae, within the Milky Way. The sun and its solar system, as well as the visible
stars, are all in the Milky Way galaxy. Harlow Shapley encouraged the exclusive use of
the term 'galaxies.' Billions of galaxies are within the optical range of the largest
telescopes. In 1996, analysis of photographs taken from the Hubble Space Telescope
increased the estimated number of galaxies from 10 billion to 50 billion, and
subsequent observations after improvements to Hubble led astronomers to revise this
upward to at least 100 billion. A subsequent estimate (2016) of 2 trillion includes
many relatively small galaxies (some with as few as 1 million stars), most of which
have merged during the life of the universe to form larger galaxies. Research with the
Hubble Space Telescope has also indicated that supermassive black holes are found at
the center of large galaxies. A galaxy is held together by the gravitational
attraction between its constituent parts (see gravitation), while its rotational
motion prevents it from collapsing on itself. Just as gravitation binds individual
stars into galaxies, it also acts to coalesce galaxies together in clusters and
clusters into superclusters, which are among the large structures of the universe and
may span 150 million light-years or more."

"Galaxies that do not correspond to these classes are called irregular."

"The earliest galaxies imaged by modern astronomers include several that were formed
less than 600 million years after that."

"A typical spiral galaxy is shaped like a flat disk, about 100,000 light-years in
diameter, with a central bulge, or nucleus, containing old stars; winding through the
disk are the characteristic spiral arms of dust, gas, and young stars (see stellar
populations). This type of galaxy is further classified as being either a normal or a
barred spiral. In the normal spiral, the arms, at least two in number, join smoothly
with the nucleus; in the barred spiral, such as the Milky Way, the arms project from
a bank of stars that runs through the nucleus. The elliptical galaxies, lacking
spiral arms entirely and containing little or no gas and dust, resemble the nuclei of
spiral galaxies. Their shapes vary from nearly spherical to highly flattened
ellipsoids. Elliptical galaxies have a much greater variation in size, mass, and
luminosity than do spiral galaxies; their sizes range from the largest known
galaxies of all, with luminosities about 10 times that of the Andromeda Galaxy, to
the small dwarf ellipticals, which can contain as few as a million stars. Irregular
galaxies appear structureless and without any nucleus or rotational symmetry; their
light comes mostly from young stars. Ultra diffuse galaxies can be as large as the
Milky Way in extent but with only 1% of the stars."

"Other galaxies radiate strongly in the infrared, ultraviolet, or X-ray parts of the
spectrum."

Bibliography (from the entry): R. J. Tayler, Galaxies, Structure and Evolution
(1993); N. Henbest and H. Couper, The Guide to the Galaxy (1994); M. S. Longair,
Galaxy Formation (1998); M. Merrifield and J. Binney, Galactic Astronomy (1998);
L. S. Sparke and S. Gallagher 3d, Galaxies in the Universe (2d ed. 2007); H. Mo,
F. van den Bosch and S. White, Galaxy Formation and Evolution (2010).

University Press. All rights reserved.

Columbia Electronic Encyclopedia — "Milky Way" (via FactMonster, excerpts)
"The Milky Way is a large barred spiral galaxy comprising an estimated 200 billion
stars (some estimates range as high as 400 billion) arrayed in the form of a disk,
with a central elliptical bulge (some 12,000 light-years in diameter) of closely
packed stars lying in the direction of Sagittarius. A compact radio source found
there, Sagittarius A*, is believed to be a supermassive black hole; such black holes
are believed to lie at the center of most spiral and elliptical galaxies... Our sun
is situated in one of the smaller arms, called the Local or Orion Arm... The sun lies
roughly 27,000 light-years from the center of the galaxy... The diameter of the disk
is c. 120,000 light-years..."

Columbia Electronic Encyclopedia — "Andromeda Galaxy" (via Infoplease, excerpts)
"Andromeda Galaxy, cataloged as M31 and NGC 224, the closest large galaxy to the
Milky Way and the only one visible to the naked eye in the Northern Hemisphere...
It is 2.2 million light-years away and is part of the Local Group of several
galaxies that includes the Milky Way... It has a diameter of about 165,000
light-years and contains at least 200 billion stars..."

DK Science: Galaxies (via FactMonster, full text)
"Stars are not scattered evenly throughout the Universe. Instead, they are grouped
together in great star islands, called galaxies. All the stars we see in the sky
belong to our home galaxy, the MILKY WAY. Some galaxies are tiny and contain only a
few million stars, but many contain hundreds of billions of stars. Galaxies are
classed into three broad groups, according to their shape: elliptical (oval), spiral
(if they have spiral arms), and irregular.

SPIRAL GALAXY ESO 510-G13 — A spiral galaxy is roughly disc-shaped and has a bulge
in the middle. The disc is formed by arms that curve out from the central bulge. The
stars in the central bulge are relatively old. Most star formation takes place on the
spiral arms, which are full of gas and dust...

IRREGULAR GALAXY — The Large Magellanic Cloud is one of our nearest galactic
neighbours in space. It is an example of an irregular galaxy, which means it has
little definite structure. It is some 160,000 light years away, and is less than a
third as wide as our own Galaxy.

ELLIPTICAL GALAXY — M87, found in the Virgo cluster of galaxies, is an example of an
elliptical galaxy. These galaxies lack the curved arms of spirals and can be round
or oval in shape. Some of the largest galaxies are elliptical. M87 may be as big as
500,000 light years across."

Encyclopaedia Britannica — "galaxy" (search snippets, 06 June 2026)
"Galaxies differ from one another in shape, with variations resulting from the way
in which the systems were formed and subsequently evolved. Galaxies are extremely
varied not only in structure but also in the amount of activity observed. Some are
the sites of vigorous star formation, with its attendant glowing gas and clouds of
dust and molecular complexes. Others, by contrast, are quiescent, having long ago
ceased to form new stars. Perhaps the most conspicuous activity in galaxies occurs
in their nuclei, where evidence suggests that in many cases supermassive objects
— probably black holes — lurk... The existence of galaxies was not recognized until
the early 20th century."

ScienceEpisode 317:16

Bill Gates

From a thirteen-year-old programmer to Microsoft's founder to one of history's most generous philanthropists — the story of Bill Gates.
