Full Text:

Bill Gates

## Early Life

William Henry Gates III was born on October 28, 1955, in Seattle, Washington. He was the only son of William Henry Gates Jr., an attorney, and Mary Maxwell Gates, a schoolteacher. Although his parents wanted him to go into law, Gates had an interest in computer science. He began studying computers in the seventh grade, when he met Paul Allen. Allen would eventually become Gates's business partner.

## Fast Facts

- Bill Gates is one of the richest men in the world.
- He co-founded Microsoft, one of the world's most successful tech companies.
- Gates read the entire World Book Encyclopedia series when he was a teen.
- Gates is interested in artificial intelligence, giving machines human capabilities.
- Gates received a near-perfect score on his SATs.

Gates's early work with computers included debugging programs for the Computer Center Corporation's PDP-10. Gates and Allen also spent time hacking and learning programming of software. Gates began creating game programs. The first was a simple tic-tac-toe game.

By his mid-teens, Gates formed a business with Allen, which helped Seattle-area cities control traffic by using a computer to look at traffic patterns.

While working with PDP-10, Gates was responsible for what was probably the first computer virus. Discovering that the machine was hooked up to a national network of computers known as Cybernet, Gates invaded the network and installed a program on the main computer that sent itself to the rest of the computers on the network. Cybernet crashed. Gates was punished for the virus and had to keep away from computers for an entire year.

## Invented BASIC language

Gates entered Harvard University. Allen showed Gates the latest issue of a computer magazine. The cover featured the Altair, a small metal box with several switches and lights on it; it was the first personal computer. Allen and Gates believed it was the beginning of a revolution—one that might someday end with computers in nearly every American home.

Altair inventor Ed Roberts needed a "language" that would enable his computer to run programs. Gates and Allen contacted Roberts and lied that they had written a BASIC interpreter for the Altair. (BASIC was a language designed in the 1960s for programming computers). The duo had not even started designing the operating-system language when they offered to sell it to Roberts.

When Roberts accepted, Gates and Allen promised delivery within a few weeks. They did not even have an Altair. Allen had to figure out how to make the PDP-10 at Harvard work like an Altair. Gates's job was to write the language to make a BASIC that would fit in the Altair's small memory. It took eight weeks to complete the language. Gates and Allen received money for each Altair sold with their BASIC.

## Created Microsoft

After Gates dropped out of Harvard, Gates and Allen created Micro-Soft. Soon they made it just Microsoft. Writing programs for the early Apple and Commodore machines, they expanded their BASIC to run on microcomputers other than the Altair.

Gates was asked by IBM to help design the operating system for its personal computer project. Gates developed the Microsoft Disk Operating System, or MS-DOS. Gates also convinced IBM to shed the secrecy surrounding its PC so others could write software for the machine. The result was that licenses for MS-DOS quickly multiplied as software developers made their products work with IBM.

MS-DOS and its related applications were able to run on almost any IBM-compatible PC (or IBM clones). In the early 1990s Microsoft sold more than 100 million copies of MS-DOS, making the operating system the all-time leader in software sales.

## Timeline: Bill Gates

- 1955: Born on October 28 in Seattle, Washington.
- 1968: Begins programming at age thirteen.
- 1975: Drops out of Harvard and co-founds Microsoft.
- 1985: Develops MS-DOS for IBM.
- 1987: Becomes the youngest US billionaire.
- 1995: Introduces Windows 95.
- 1998: Faces lawsuits for anti-competitive practices.
- 2008: Gives up role at Microsoft to concentrate on philanthropy.
- 2016: Along with wife Melinda, receives the Medal of Freedom from President Barack Obama.
- 2019: Ranks number two on Forbes' list of billionaires.
- 2021: Divorces wife Melinda.

## Found Success with Windows

Gates next focused on the area dominated by the Apple Corporation's Macintosh computers which took a different approach to computer operations. Users had to type in special coded commands and icons could be selected to give the commands. Apple computers quickly became known for being "user friendly." When Gates introduced Windows, his own version of a "user-friendly" software for the PC, it also became popular.

Gates then entered the world of computer-driven multimedia when he began promoting CD-ROM technology. A CD-ROM disc had lots of storage and was easy to connect to a PC. It was able to hold information such as encyclopedias, movies, and games. Gates wanted to expand his business by combining PCs with CD-ROM and offering numerous multimedia products.

In 1995, Gates introduced Windows 95, a new operating-system software for personal computers. The same year, he published The Road Ahead. In it, he discusses the information superhighway, the future of the computer industry, and the impact of technology on our lives.

## Antitrust Lawsuits

Gates then worked on developing software for the Internet. He introduced Internet Explorer, a tool for browsing the Internet. In most cases, computers with preinstalled Windows software also came with Explorer. Some observers thought the "bundling" of Microsoft software gave Explorer an unfair advantage over other browsers, which were not preinstalled on new computer systems. The US Department of Justice and twenty states filed lawsuits accusing Microsoft of using illegal, anticompetitive practices to dominate its rivals and avoid competition.

In 1998, Microsoft released its next generation of Windows software, Windows 98. This release also created controversy because it more closely tied together the operating system and the Explorer software. During this time, Gates published the book Business @ the Speed of Light, which discusses ways he believes companies must begin to use technology to survive the ever-increasing speed of business in the future.

A US district judge found Microsoft to be a monopoly that used its market share and profits to harm competing companies. The US Court of Appeals for the District of Columbia upheld the lower court's conclusion that Microsoft had violated antitrust laws. A settlement was later reached.

In 2000, Gates handed day-to-day management of Microsoft to his friend, Steve Ballmer, adding chief executive officer to his existing title as president. Gates retained his position as chairman and added the title of chief software architect. By 2008, Gates stopped his full-time work at Microsoft to concentrate on philanthropy. He remained chairman until 2014, and worked on special projects for the company. Gates also remained on the board and served as technology advisor.

## Philanthropy

Gates is a very giving philanthropist. He and his ex-wife created the Bill & Melinda Gates Foundation to give funds to causes around the world. The foundation donated billions of dollars to improve health care and reduce poverty worldwide and to expand educational opportunities and access to information technology in the United States. The Gates also gave billions of dollars for numerous studies, including developing AIDS prevention programs, and eradicating diseases such as polio and tuberculosis worldwide.

## Words to Know

anticompetitive practices: Activities to reduce the competition.
bundling: Selling different kinds of software together as a package.
debugging: Finding and removing errors.
monopoly: When a company and its products are the only available option in a particular industry.

Henry Ford: The Man Who Put the World on Wheels

Maya and Leo tell the story of Henry Ford: the farm boy who didn't invent the car but used the moving assembly line and mass production to make the Model T cheap enough for ordinary families, and put the whole world on wheels.

Transcript

Maya: Here's a riddle for you, Leo. What machine changed the way the whole world lives, works, and travels, and it all started as one man's dream in a small shed?

Leo: A machine that changed the whole world. Is it the airplane?

Maya: Good guess, but think smaller and much more everyday. It is the car. And the man who put the world on wheels was Henry Ford.

Leo: Henry Ford! I know he invented the car. Wait, did he actually invent it?

Maya: Actually, no. Cars existed before Ford. But back then, they were toys for rich people, built by hand one at a time, and each one cost a fortune.

Leo: So what did Ford do that was so special?

Maya: He made cars cheap enough for ordinary families to buy. That is the story we are telling today.

Leo: Okay, start at the beginning. Who was Henry Ford?

Maya: He was born on a farm in Michigan in eighteen sixty-three. As a boy, he loved taking machines apart to see how they worked. At sixteen, he left the farm for the city of Detroit, where he became an apprentice in a machine shop.

Leo: An apprentice, a beginner who learns by working with an expert?

Maya: Exactly. Later he got a job at the Edison Company, working as an engineer. By day he fixed machines. Nights and weekends, he built his first car in a shed behind his house.

Leo: Did it work?

Maya: It did. In eighteen ninety-six, he finished it, and it actually ran. So he quit his job and started building cars full time. His first two car companies failed, but in nineteen oh three, he tried again and founded the Ford Motor Company.

Leo: Third time is the charm. What was his big idea?

Maya: His dream was a car for the great multitude, a simple, sturdy car so cheap that almost any working family could afford one. In nineteen oh eight, his team designed the Model T.

Leo: The famous Model T! How much did it cost?

Maya: Eight hundred fifty dollars. That sounds like a lot, but back then it was far cheaper than any other car. And it was tough enough for bumpy country roads.

Leo: People must have wanted it badly.

Maya: They wanted it so badly the factory could not keep up. So Ford had to find a way to build cars much faster.

Leo: And that is where the assembly line comes in?

Maya: Right. In nineteen thirteen, Ford set up the first moving assembly line for building a whole car. The car body moved slowly down a conveyor belt, and each worker stood in one place and did just one small job, over and over.

Leo: Like one worker puts on the wheels, and the next one puts on the doors?

Maya: Exactly. Before the line, building one car took more than twelve hours. With the moving line, it took about one hour and thirty-three minutes.

Leo: Whoa. So the cars got cheaper too?

Maya: Much cheaper. By nineteen sixteen, a Model T cost only three hundred sixty dollars. And by nineteen eighteen, half of all the cars in America were Model Ts.

Leo: But doing the same tiny job all day sounds really boring. Did the workers hate it?

Maya: Many did, and they kept quitting. So in nineteen fourteen, Ford did something shocking. He nearly doubled the pay. Workers would get five dollars for an eight-hour day.

Leo: Was that a lot back then?

Maya: It was huge. News spread everywhere, and suddenly thousands of people wanted to work for Ford. He also started a profit-sharing plan that gave up to thirty million dollars a year to his workers.

Leo: How many Model Ts were built in the end?

Maya: More than fifteen million, before production stopped in nineteen twenty-seven. Ford came to be known as the apostle of mass production.

Leo: The apostle of mass production? That sounds religious.

Maya: It just means he was the great champion of it. Mass production means making huge numbers of the same thing quickly and cheaply.

Leo: So his real invention was not the car, but the way of making cars.

Maya: Exactly. And that way of making things changed everything, from toasters to hamburgers. His factories later built war planes during World War Two. Ford retired in nineteen forty-five and died in nineteen forty-seven.

Leo: A farm boy who put the world on wheels. I love that. Time for our vocabulary spotlight?

Maya: Now it is time for our vocabulary spotlight. Listen carefully, and say each word with us.

Maya: Word one is automobile, which means a car, a road vehicle with an engine. Henry Ford built his first automobile in a shed behind his house.

Leo: Word two is engineer, a person who designs or builds machines. Ford worked as an engineer at the Edison Company.

Maya: Word three is apprentice, a beginner who learns a trade by working with an expert. At sixteen, Ford became a machine shop apprentice.

Leo: Word four is mass production, which means making huge numbers of the same product quickly and cheaply. The Model T was a triumph of mass production.

Maya: Word five is assembly line, a factory line where workers each add one part as the product moves past them. Ford's moving assembly line built a car in about ninety minutes.

Leo: Word six is inexpensive, which means not costing much money. Ford wanted an inexpensive car for ordinary families.

Maya: Word seven is standardized, which means made the same way every time. Every Model T was standardized, even painted the same black color.

Leo: Word eight is wage, the money a worker is paid. Ford shocked the world by raising the daily wage to five dollars.

Maya: Word nine is profit, the money a business makes after paying its costs. Ford shared up to thirty million dollars of profit with his workers every year.

Leo: And word ten is retire, which means to stop working, usually because of age. Henry Ford retired in nineteen forty-five.

Maya: That is all ten words. Play this track again anytime you want to review.

Maya: Quiz time. Question one. Who did Henry Ford work for before he started building cars full time?

Leo: Think for a moment. The answer is the Edison Company, Thomas Edison's electric company.

Maya: Question two. True or false. The moving assembly line made building a car take longer.

Leo: Think it through. False. It cut the time from more than twelve hours to about one hour and thirty-three minutes.

Maya: Last one. The word multitude most nearly means: a crowd, a machine, a price, or a factory?

Leo: Take a guess. It means a crowd, a great number of people. Ford wanted a car for the great multitude.

Maya: You did great. So remember, Henry Ford did not invent the car, but his assembly line and mass production made cars cheap enough for ordinary families, and that put the whole world on wheels. See you next time on The World, Word by Word.

Temple Grandin

Maya and Leo explore Temple Grandin: the autistic scientist who thinks in pictures, invented the squeeze machine, and redesigned how farms treat cattle.

Transcript

Maya: Here's a riddle for you, Leo. She's a scientist who thinks in pictures instead of words. She calms frightened cattle, and she taught the world that being different can be a superpower. Who is she?

Leo: Thinking in pictures instead of words, that's a wild way to think. Is it Temple Grandin?

Maya: You got it. Temple Grandin was born in Boston in nineteen forty seven. As a small child, she didn't speak until she was three and a half years old. Loud sounds upset her, and when she grew frustrated, she threw big tantrums.

Leo: That sounds like a hard childhood. What was going on with her?

Maya: As a child she was diagnosed with autism. Autism is a brain difference that affects how a person communicates and experiences the world. At that time, many children with autism were sent to live in institutions. But Temple's mother refused, and instead sent her to specialized schools that helped her learn to talk and use her strengths.

Leo: She made it to college, right?

Maya: She did much more than that. She earned a bachelor's degree in psychology, a master's degree in animal science, and even a doctorate in animal science. Today she is a professor at Colorado State University.

Leo: But she's famous for working with cattle, not just teaching. How did that start?

Maya: It started with a visit to her aunt's ranch in Arizona. There she saw a squeeze chute, a narrow pen with sides that press together to hold cattle still while they get shots or checkups. Temple noticed that the cattle looked calm inside it, so she asked her aunt to close her inside the chute too. And guess what she felt.

Leo: Let me guess. Calm?

Maya: Exactly. She felt safe and calm, like someone was protecting her. So back at school she built her own squeeze machine and studied whether it could calm other people too. That little machine made her want to become a scientist.

Leo: So she started designing things for animals because she understood how they feel?

Maya: That's the heart of her story. Temple thinks in pictures, not words. She calls this visual thinking. Because animals also see the world in pictures, she could imagine what frightened them. She noticed that cattle were scared of small details most people ignored, like a hose lying on the floor, or a dark passageway.

Leo: And what did she do about it?

Maya: She fixed those small things. She picked up the hose, she lit up the dark chute, and suddenly cattle would walk right through places they had refused to enter. It amazed her that she could take away their fear with something so simple.

Leo: Did she change how farms handle animals?

Maya: She redesigned whole facilities. Instead of straight paths, her designs lead cattle along curving runways, which feel natural and safe to the animals. Today more than half of the cattle in the United States are processed in systems she designed.

Leo: What about the people side? Is she known for autism too?

Maya: Very much. She is an advocate for people with autism, which means she speaks up for them and their rights. She gives talks about what she calls specialized minds. People with autism, dyslexia, or attention difficulties often think in unusual ways, and she says that when different kinds of minds work together, you get better results.

Leo: So her difference became her greatest strength.

Maya: That's her message. Now let's do our vocabulary spotlight. I'll say a word, tell you what it means, and use it in a sentence. Word one is autism, which means a brain difference that affects how a person communicates and experiences the world. Temple Grandin was diagnosed with autism as a small child.

Leo: Word two is diagnose, which means to find out what disease or condition a person has. Doctors diagnosed her condition when she was only three years old.

Maya: Word three is sensory, which means connected to the senses, like sight, sound, or touch. Autistic people often have very strong sensory reactions to loud sounds.

Leo: Word four is livestock, which means farm animals like cattle, sheep, and pigs. Temple Grandin became one of the world's top experts on livestock.

Maya: Word five is facility, which means a building or place made for a special purpose. She designed new facilities where cattle could move through calmly.

Leo: Word six is squeeze, which means to press from both sides. The squeeze chute holds cattle gently between its two sides.

Maya: Word seven is visual, which means using pictures and images. Temple is a visual thinker who thinks in pictures instead of words.

Leo: Word eight is humane, which means kind to people or animals. She works to make farming more humane.

Maya: Word nine is advocate, which means a person who speaks up for a cause. Temple Grandin is an advocate for people with autism.

Leo: And word ten is mentor, which means an experienced person who guides a younger one. Her science teacher was a mentor who encouraged her to study her squeeze machine.

Maya: Nice work. Now a quick quiz. Question one. What is a squeeze chute?

Leo: Listeners, think for a moment. It is a narrow pen with sides that press together to hold cattle still and calm while they are checked.

Maya: Question two. True or false. Temple Grandin thinks in words, just like most people.

Leo: Think it through. False. She thinks in pictures, which she calls visual thinking.

Maya: Last one. The word advocate most nearly means enemy, critic, supporter, or stranger?

Leo: Take a guess. It means supporter, a person who speaks up for a cause.

Maya: You did great. So remember, Temple Grandin turned autism into an advantage. Her visual thinking lets her see the world the way animals do, her designs made farming more humane, and she shows that different minds can change the world. See you next time on The World, Word by Word.

Animal Husbandry - Source Material Source: Source link (The Columbia Electronic Encyclopedia, 6th ed., via FactMonster) Original Gale link (Source link) was unreachable (institutional login wall / empty response); the Google Doc materials list carried no separate backup link for this topic, so the Columbia Electronic Encyclopedia entry was used, supplemented by the Wikipedia "Animal husbandry" article (Source link) for background detail.

The Columbia Electronic Encyclopedia entry (transcribed)

animal husbandry, aspect of agriculture concerned with the care and breeding of domestic animals such as cattle, goats, sheep, hogs, and horses. Domestication of wild animal species was a crucial achievement in the prehistoric transition of human civilization from hunting-and-gathering to agriculture. The first domesticated livestock animal may have been the sheep, which was tamed around 9000 b.c. in N Iraq. By about 7000 b.c. (and perhaps much earlier) the pig was domesticated in Anatolia; around 6500 b.c. domestic goats were kept in Mesopotamia; by 5900 b.c. (and perhaps 3,000 years earlier) there were domesticated cattle in Chad, while independently about 5500 b.c. there were domesticated cattle in SW Iran; and around 3500 b.c. the horse was domesticated on the Eurasian steppes. Nothing is known of the early development of husbandry; selective breeding for the improvement of livestock was already practiced in Roman times. Continuing systematic development and improvement of domestic livestock breeds, established in England following 1760 by Robert Bakewell and others, has been paralleled by advances in animal nutrition and veterinary medicine.

Supplementary detail (Wikipedia: Animal husbandry)

Animal husbandry is the branch of agriculture concerned with raising domesticated animals for harvesting valuable animal products (meat, milk, eggs, fibre, hide) or to train animals to provide labour or services. It involves day-to-day management, feeding, nutrition and health, selective breeding, assisted birth, grooming, marketing, and harvest of products. It covers livestock - mainly cattle, sheep, goats, horses, pigs, and poultry (chickens, ducks, geese) - and extends to aquaculture (fish, shrimp, oysters) and insects (beekeeping, sericulture).

History: The first wild animal domesticated was the dog (more than 17,500 years ago), used as guard animal and hunting companion. Pigs were domesticated in the Near East between 8,500 and 8000 BC; sheep and goats in or near the Fertile Crescent about 8,500 BC; cattle from wild aurochs in modern Turkey and Pakistan around 8,500 BC. Draught animals were first used about 4,000 BC. In ancient Egypt cattle were the most important livestock; sheep, goats, pigs were kept, and ducks, geese, and pigeons were captured in nets and bred on farms, force-fed with dough to fatten them. Honey bees were domesticated from at least the Old Kingdom. In ancient Rome all the livestock known in ancient Egypt were available, and rabbits were domesticated for food by the first century BC. In the Columbian exchange, Old World livestock (cattle, horses, sheep, goats) were introduced into the New World for the first time.

Robert Bakewell established selective breeding as a scientific practice during the British Agricultural Revolution in the 18th century. Using native stock he quickly selected for large, fine-boned sheep with long, lustrous wool: the Lincoln Longwool was improved by Bakewell and used to develop the New (or Dishley) Leicester. Long-horned heifers were crossed with the Westmoreland bull to create the Dishley Longhorn cattle, and English farmers began to breed cattle primarily for beef.

Modern systems: In developed countries intensive animal farming dominates (beef cattle in high-density feedlots; thousands of chickens in broiler houses), while on poorer soil animals are kept extensively and forage for themselves. Most livestock are herbivores; ruminants like cattle and sheep are adapted to feed on grass but may be fed fodders richer in energy and protein such as pelleted cereals. Pigs and chickens are omnivores and need high-protein foods. Bees give honey and wax; horses historically provided transport and military service until motor vehicles replaced them; oxen pulled wagons and ploughs and are still used in regions without motorization.

Transcribed 2026-09-09 from the publicly available Columbia Electronic Encyclopedia text.

cell, in biology

Introduction

cell, in biology, the unit of structure and function of which all plants and animals are composed. The cell is the smallest unit in the living organism that is capable of integrating the essential life processes. There are many unicellular organisms, e.g., bacteria and protozoans, in which the single cell performs all life functions. In higher organisms, a division of labor has evolved in which groups of cells have differentiated into specialized tissues, which in turn are grouped into organs and organ systems.

Cells can be separated into two major groups—prokaryotes, cells whose DNA is not segregated within a well-defined nucleus surrounded by a membranous nuclear envelope, and eukaryotes, those with a membrane-enveloped nucleus. The cyanobacteria and bacteria (kingdom Monera) are prokaryotes. They are smaller in size and simpler in internal structure than eukaryotes and are believed to have evolved much earlier. All organisms other than cyanobacteria and bacteria consist of one or more eukaryotic cells.

All cells share a number of common properties; they store information in genes made of DNA; they use proteins as their main structural material; they synthesize proteins in the cell's ribosomes using the information encoded in the DNA and mobilized by means of RNA; they use adenosine triphosphate as the means of transferring energy for the cell's internal processes; and they are enclosed by a cell membrane, composed of proteins and a double layer of lipid molecules, that controls the flow of materials into and out of the cell.

The Study of Cells

Because almost all cells are microscopic, knowledge of the component cell parts increased proportionately to the development of the microscope and other specialized instruments and of allied experimental techniques. Among those who contributed to early knowledge of cells through their use of the microscope were Antony van Leeuwenhoek, Robert Hooke, and Marcello Malpighi. In the 19th cent. Matthias J. Schleiden and Theodor Schwann developed what is now known as the cell theory. The theory was widely promoted after the pronouncement by Rudolf Virchow in 1855 that "omnis cellulae e cellula" [All cells arise from cells]. The study of cell structure came to be called cytology and that of tissues histology. In the 20th cent. appreciation of the biochemistry of the cell flourished, along with a better understanding of its structure; cell biology now integrates both chemical and structural information.

Cell Structure

In a eukaryotic cell's nucleus the DNA, along with certain proteins, is arranged in long, thin threads called chromatin fibers that coil into bodies called chromosomes during meiosis. The nucleus also contains one or more nucleoli that participate in the production on the RNA of ribosomes. The portion of the cell outside the nucleus, called the cytoplasm, contains several additional cell structures (often called organelles). Among the important organelles that may be present are the ribosomes; the endoplasmic reticulum, a highly convoluted system of membranes believed to be continuous with the nuclear envelope and responsible for transporting certain newly made proteins; the mitochondria, which are present in nearly all eukaryotic cells and extract energy by breaking down the chemical bonds in molecules of complex nutrients during respiration and perform other functions; the chloroplasts, which are present only in green plants and convert energy from sunlight by the process of photosynthesis; lysosomes, which contain digestive enzymes; peroxisomes, which contain a number of specialized enzymes; the centrosomes, which function during cell division; the Golgi apparatus, which functions in the synthesis, storage, and secretion of various cellular products; filaments and microtubules that form a sort of skeletal system known as a cytoskeleton and also participate in movement of cells and organelles; vacuoles containing food in various stages of digestion; and inert granules and crystals. In plant cells there is, in addition to the cell membrane, a thickened cell wall, usually composed chiefly of cellulose secreted by the cell.

(Note: the episode's assigned link MVKVIA731731443 showed a Gale institution login wall; the alternate Cloning link from the mom's reading list document opened the full text below.)

# Cloning

## Gale In Context: Middle School

Full Text:

Living things that are identical in every way are known as clones. Some clones occur naturally. For example, identical twins are clones of each other. Scientists have recently learned how to create clones in laboratories, and this practice is known as cloning.

Cloning offers many potential benefits. Plants and animals can be cloned to take advantage of desirable characteristics, and human cloning might be the solution to serious diseases that cannot currently be cured. However, cloning also creates many ethical issues. These issues are especially complicated with regard to human cloning.

## Fast Facts

- The first cloned mammal in history was a sheep named Dolly, who was born in 1997 and died in 2003.
- Researchers have since cloned many other animals, including monkeys, horses, and dogs.
- In 2009, scientists cloned a champion racehorse, which was then used to breed more horses.
- In 2011, seven cloned golden retrievers went on security duty at an airport in South Korea. They were cloned from a dog named Chase, who had a remarkable ability to detect illegal drugs in luggage.

## The History and Development of Cloning

The modern history of cloning dates back to 1891, when a German scientist named Hans Driesch used a sea urchin in a cloning experiment. Driesch experimented with a sea urchin embryo made up of exactly two cells and separated it into two separate embryos, each made up of one cell. The separate embryos then grew into adult sea urchins. Driesch's experiment proved a very important point; it showed that each cell in a developing embryo is capable of growing into an adult organism on its own.

During the twentieth century, researchers performed additional cloning experiments. Many early efforts attempted to clone frogs and salamanders using part of a cell known as the nucleus. Over time, scientists learned that a cell nucleus could be taken from a fully grown organism to create clones of that organism.

In 1975, scientists applied their new knowledge to mammals for the first time. First, they removed the nucleus from a rabbit embryo cell, and then they took the nucleus out of another rabbit embryo cell and put it in the first cell. The first cell then began to grow into a baby rabbit. This process came to be known as nuclear transfer.

In 1996, nuclear transfer research took a major step forward. For the first time, scientists used the nucleus from a sheep cell that had been kept alive only in the laboratory. These types of cells are known as cultured cells. The scientists managed to grow a live baby sheep from these cultured cells.

Researchers then succeeded in taking a cell from an adult sheep and using it to grow a baby sheep. This was the first time such a procedure had ever resulted in the birth of a live animal. The baby sheep was named Dolly, and she became famous around the world. Since Dolly's birth, scientists have cloned other animals, including monkeys, pigs, cows, chickens, cats, dogs, horses, and wolves.

## Cloning Methods

Scientists use three different methods to create artificial clones. The first method is known as gene cloning, and it is used to find and copy a particular gene. It enables researchers to reproduce very specific characteristics of an organism.

The second method is called reproductive cloning, which is a technique that creates an exact copy of a living adult organism. Dolly the sheep is an example of an animal that was made using reproductive cloning.

Therapeutic cloning is the third technique, and this method is used to harvest special types of cells known as stem cells, which are young cells that are capable of maturing into any other type of cell. Human beings, for example, are made up of about two hundred different types of cells. Stem cells are capable of growing into any one of these.

## The Uses of Cloning

Cloning can be used to create organisms with favorable characteristics. For example, consider an apple tree that yields more fruit than most other apple trees. Cloning that tree would create new trees that would also be able to produce more fruit than usual. This technique could help solve food shortages.

One of cloning's most promising uses is in medicine. Stem cells could be the key to curing many serious diseases and medical conditions. For instance, if a person has a disease that leads to unhealthy blood cells, stem cells could be used to grow new, healthy blood cells to cure the disease. Cloning could also be used to grow new body parts to replace old or injured ones. For example, stem cells could produce organs that could be used in transplant surgery. These organs would also be safer for the patient because they would be made with the patient's own genes. This would ensure that the patient's body would not reject them. Human cloning also has other possible uses. However, many ethical questions surround the issue of cloning.

## Bio Blurb: Hans Driesch

Hans Driesch was born in 1867 in what is now Germany. As a young man, he attended university, where he studied chemistry, physics, and zoology. Following his schooling, he traveled around Europe. In Naples, Italy, he began experimenting with marine animals. It was during this time that Driesch experimented with sea urchin embryos. He discovered that each cell in an embryo could grow into an adult organism. He eventually returned to Germany and continued his experiments. Driesch died in Germany in 1941.

## Ethical Issues

Most of the ethical issues regarding cloning apply to human cloning. Still, some critics argue that cloning is unethical in every situation. One argument against cloning is that it is not known if cloning is completely safe. There are concerns that cloned organisms might have a higher risk for certain conditions or health problems.

Stem cell research leads to some very difficult questions as well. To create stem cells, it is first necessary to produce a human embryo. That embryo would exist for the sole purpose of being destroyed so its stem cells could be harvested. Many people, including some researchers, think this is wrong. In places where human stem cell research is allowed, it is regulated by strict laws.

The cloning of human beings also raises many other issues. Gene cloning could be used to allow parents to choose the characteristics of their children. For example, they could decide on the color of the baby's eyes or hair. One day, it might also be possible to create people with unnatural abilities, such as a person with enormous strength or incredible intelligence. Again, these examples raise the question of whether it is right for people to interfere in the creation of life in such a manner.

## Words to Know

embryo: The earliest stage of development for a human or animal.
ethical: Related to questions of right and wrong.
gene: The basic physical unit that determines the characteristics of a living thing.
nucleus: The part of a cell that contains the genes.
organism: A living thing.
transplant: Surgery that involves taking a body part from a donor and putting it in another patient.
zoology: The study of animals.

Original Text

Source: Source link (The Columbia Electronic Encyclopedia, 6th ed., "genetics" article — used as the fallback for the assigned Gale link Source link, which could not be opened; Gale's Middle School in Context articles are based on the Columbia Electronic Encyclopedia)

genetics | The Columbia Electronic Encyclopedia, 6th ed.
Copyright © 2026, Columbia University Press. All rights reserved.

Introduction

genetics, scientific study of the mechanism of heredity. While Gregor Mendel first presented his findings on the statistical laws governing the transmission of certain traits from generation to generation in 1856, it was not until the discovery and detailed study of the chromosome and the gene in the 20th cent. that scientists found the physical basis of hereditary characteristics. A brief summary of the basic laws of heredity and the terms used follows.

Sections in this article: Introduction; Evolutionary Mechanisms; Modifications of Mendel's Principles; Basic Laws and Terminology; Bibliography.

Evolutionary Mechanisms

The study of mutations, together with the analyses of population genetics, has been used to explain the mechanism of evolution. The elementary process of evolution is considered to be the changes in the frequency of occurrence of alleles in a population. Mutation, which causes the appearance of new alleles or changes the relative frequency of already existing alleles, is one important mechanism by which evolution occurs. Natural selection, by affecting reproductive success, influences the frequencies of alleles and other genetic variants in successive generations. For example, if the presence of a particular allele makes a homozygous individual unable to mate, the allele may be eliminated from the population.

Genetic drift — the random fluctuation in the frequency of an allele, resulting mainly from the vagaries of chance mating — is also an evolutionary mechanism. Although in large populations drift varies only a little above and below a statistical mean, in small breeding populations an entire generation might, by chance alone, be born with the same genotype with respect to a particular allelic pair of genes, thus leading to either the elimination or dominance of a particular gene. Because fluctuations in the proportions of alleles are more significant in the gene pools of small, isolated breeding populations, genetic drift is a mechanism of species diversity and evolution in such groups.

Modifications of Mendel's Principles

Modification of Mendel's principles developed as knowledge of the chromosomes increased; many discoveries have helped to account for apparent deviations from Mendelian ratios. For example, Mendel's studies emphasized genes that behave independently from one another during transmission to offspring. But we now know that genes are transmitted as constituents of chromosomes, each of which carries many different genes, which sheds light on the tendency of certain characteristics to appear in combination with one another (linkage). It also has been found that some characteristics are sex-linked, i.e., are transmitted by genes carried by the sex chromosomes; and that a non-sex-linked gene inherited from the father may differ in its expression from the same gene inherited from the mother, a phenomenon called "imprinting." Other research has shown that there may be multiple alleles (more than two alternative genes) for a given characteristic: the human blood groups are determined by a combination of several possible alleles. It is apparent that homologous portions of paired chromosomes may be interchanged during meiosis (crossing over) and that the interaction of many genes is responsible for determining many of the traits of individuals. Since the discovery (1953) of the structure of DNA, work on nucleic acids has begun to explain how genes determine life processes by directing the synthesis of proteins. It has also explained mutations as alterations in gene or chromosome structure. It has been found, for example, that mutations in the form of repeated sequences of otherwise normal chemical bases, can grow in length with succeeding generations, in some cases causing diseases (e.g., myotonic muscular dystrophy) that increase in severity each time they are inherited.

Most of the knowledge of chromosome structure and the behavior of genes has come from studies of the vinegar, or fruit, fly (Drosophila melanogaster), which reproduces so rapidly that many generations can be studied over a short time. The work of T. H. Morgan and his associates on Drosophila was the basis of much of the early progress of genetics in the United States. Certain other small laboratory animals, plants, and microorganisms such as the E. coli bacteria are now used, also largely because of their ability to reproduce rapidly. For obvious reasons human beings are poor subjects for experimental genetic studies; however, much that aids understanding heredity in humans has been learned from the "lower" forms of life. Also, by tracing the appearance of certain abnormal characteristics (e.g., hemophilia, color blindness, and certain mental disorders and anatomical defects) and blood groups through a number of generations the hereditary pattern of these conditions has been established. The increasing ability of scientists to decode genetic information (see Human Genome Project) has led to a considerable expansion of knowledge about the nature and role of genes in humans and other organisms. Application of this knowledge has played an important role in the fields of gene therapy, genetic engineering, and evolutionary studies, and has resulted in a better understanding of the genetic components of disease, physical characteristics, mental illness, and even personality.

Basic Laws and Terminology

The gene is defined as the unit of inheritance. A gene is actually a sequence of DNA contained by and arranged linearly along a chromosome. Each gene transmits chemical information that is expressed as a trait, e.g., tall or dwarf size in the garden pea plant. Each species has a genome, or characteristic set of genes, that contains the total genetic information for an individual organism. In many familiar organisms two genes for each trait are present in each individual, and these paired genes, both governing the same trait, are called alleles. The two allelic genes in any one individual may be alike (homozygous) or different (heterozygous). The chromosomes of animals and plants that reproduce sexually usually exist in pairs; the members of a chromosome pair are termed homologous. In humans there are 46 chromosomes, or 23 homologous pairs. Pairs of genes are borne on homologous chromosomes.

In the process of meiosis, by which ova and sperm are produced, the chromosomes are so divided that each mature sex cell contains half the original number of chromosomes, or one chromosome of each pair, and therefore one gene of each pair. Thus, when the ovum and the sperm fuse on fertilization, the fertilized egg (zygote) receives one allele from each parent. With many pairs of alleles that have contrasting effects (e.g., certain alleles produce different eye color), one is dominant and the other recessive: an individual heterozygous (carrying contrasting alleles) for a given characteristic invariably displays one aspect of that characteristic and not its alternative, although the gene for the aspect that does not appear (i.e., that is recessive) is present. This individual is called a hybrid.

In Mendelian law the offspring — or first filial (called F1) generation — of parents each homozygous for different alleles of a given gene are all hybrids heterozygous for the characteristic governed by that gene and are said to be of the same phenotype, i.e., they are all similar in appearance to the homozygous dominant parent because the recessive characteristic is masked, although their gene composition, or genotype, is different from either parent. A cross of members of the F1 generation produces a second filial (F2) generation of which approximately three fourths show the dominant characteristic and one fourth the recessive. Note however, that great numbers of characteristics are inherited simultaneously and the patterns of transmission of genes are such that offspring strongly resembling one parent in certain traits can resemble the other parent in other traits.

It has also become clear that an individual organism's heredity and environment interact in the manifestation of many traits: a pea plant with a genetic tendency toward tallness will not achieve its full size if deprived of adequate water and minerals for growth. However, true alterations in gene and chromosome structure are the product of mutation and are not produced by environmental conditions, as was postulated by the theory of acquired characteristics. The discovery by H. J. Muller in 1927 of methods for artificially inducing mutations by means of ionizing radiations and other mutagens opened the way for much new genetics research.

Bibliography

See T. Beebe and J. Burke, Gene Structure and Transcription (1988); R. McKie, The Genetic Jigsaw (1988); G. L. Stine, The New Human Genetics (1988); G. W. Burns and P. J. Bottino, The Science of Genetics (1989); C. Wills, The Wisdom of the Genes (1989); G. Edlin, Human Genetics (1990); B. Lewin, Genes IV (1990); S. Mukherjee, The Gene: An Intimate History (2016).

Down Syndrome: One Extra Chromosome

Maya and Leo explore Down syndrome: how an extra copy of chromosome 21 shapes the body and brain, the three types, how common it is, and how people with Down syndrome learn, compete, and live full lives.

Source: https://link.gale.com/apps/doc/RUQJJB017950216/MSIC?u=sunnyvale15&sid=bookmark-MSIC&xid=68a43959
(Full text transcribed from the Gale In Context: Middle School "Down Syndrome" document.)

World Down Syndrome Day [Image 0: Evelyn and Severino Gallo attend a parade on World Down Syndrome Day at the Down Syndrome Association of Greater St. Louis, in Brentwood, Missouri on March 21, 2021. The 21st day of March was selected to signify the uniqueness of the triplication of the 21st chromosome which causes Down syndrome.] Evelyn and Severino Gallo attend a parade on World Down Syndrome Day at the Down Syndrome Association of Greater St. Louis, in Brentwood, Missouri on March 21, 2021. The 21st day of March was selected to signify the uniqueness of the triplication of the 21st chromosome which causes Down syndrome. Bill Greenblatt/UPI

Down syndrome is a genetic disorder in which children are born with an extra chromosome. This causes physical and mental delays and disabilities. Most babies are born with forty-six chromosomes, twenty-three from each parent. Babies born with Down syndrome have three copies of chromosome 21 instead of two. According to the National Down Syndrome Society (NDSS), about one in seven hundred babies born in the United States each year has Down syndrome.

Fast Facts

- Zack Gottsagen is an actor with Down syndrome. He starred in the film The Peanut Butter Falcon (2019) about a young man with the condition who became a professional wrestler.

- World Down Syndrome Day is observed each year on March 21 to bring awareness to those with the condition. The day has been officially recognized by the United Nations (UN) since 2012.

- Since the 1970s, US public schools are legally required to provide an appropriate education to children with Down syndrome although many children are included in regular classes.

While some of the disabilities caused by Down syndrome are long-lasting and may shorten a person's lifespan, people with Down syndrome can lead full lives. Medical and technological advances have improved the lives of those with the condition.

In 1866, John Langdon Down, an English physician, became the first person to describe and classify Down syndrome. The condition was later named after him.

Causes and Symptoms

About six thousand children in the United States are born with Down syndrome each year. Most cases of Down syndrome are caused by a chromosomal disorder. Chromosomes contain genetic information and exist in every cell in the body. Twenty-three pairs of chromosomes are located in the nucleus of each cell.

A baby is conceived by combining one sperm cell with one egg cell. The baby receives twenty-three chromosomes from each parent. Chromosome 21 is one of the twenty-three pairs of chromosomes. Sometimes an accident occurs that causes a child to receive an extra copy of chromosome 21. Then the child has forty-seven chromosomes instead of forty-six. This is referred to as trisomy 21.

The exact cause of the extra chromosome that causes Down syndrome is unknown. The condition affects an equal number of girls and boys. As a woman's age increases, the chances of her having a baby with Down syndrome significantly increase. For example, in very young women, the risk is 1 in 4,000. In women age thirty-five, the risk is 1 in 350, and in women age forty, the risk is 1 in 100. By age forty-five, the risk of a woman having a child with Down syndrome is 1 in 30.

Physical Characteristics

This extra genetic material causes the physical characteristics associated with Down syndrome. These include a flattened face and nose; a small head; almond-shaped eyes that slant upward; a small mouth, which causes the tongue to stick out and look large; small ears; a short neck; small rounded hands and feet; a single line across the palm of the hand; extra folds of skin in the inside corner of each eye; poor muscle tone; and shortened height.

Children with Down syndrome may have serious physical disabilities. About 40 percent have heart defects. These are often abnormal openings in the walls that separate the heart's chambers. The condition is called atrial septal defect or ventricular septal defect, depending on which chambers are affected.

Children with Down syndrome are prone to infections such as pneumonia as well as kidney disease, chronic constipation, and thyroid disease. They are also likely to have hearing and vision impairments and are at a greater risk of developing leukemia, a type of cancer affecting blood cells.

Adults with Down syndrome have an increased risk of developing diabetes and seizure disorders. Later in life, they have a high risk of developing Alzheimer's disease, a brain disorder that affects memory.

Developmental Disabilities

Children with Down syndrome develop at a slower-than-normal rate. Because their muscles are weak, they learn to sit up, crawl, and walk later than children without the condition. They also usually talk at a later age. Children with Down syndrome learn at a slower rate and have developmental disabilities such as a short attention span and impulsivity.

Connections: Special Olympics

Special Olympics offers training and competitions to people with intellectual disabilities (IDs) throughout the world. More than five million athletes participate in the more than thirty Olympic-style team and individual sports. Children with Down syndrome, autism, fragile X, and other IDs may compete in the Special Olympics, which take place every two years and alternate between Summer and Winter Games.

Types of Down Syndrome

There are three types of Down syndrome, and all cause mostly the same characteristics, delays, and disabilities. Chromosomes must be analyzed to definitively determine the type of Down syndrome that a baby has.

- Trisomy 21: About 95 percent of babies born with Down syndrome have trisomy 21. Babies with this type have an extra copy of chromosome 21 in every cell.

- Translocation: About 3 percent of babies born with the condition have translocation Down syndrome. With this type, a whole or partial extra chromosome 21 is present but attached to a different chromosome instead of being separate.

- Mosaic: Only about 2 percent of babies born with Down syndrome have this type. Some of their cells have three copies of chromosome 21 but others do not. Because of this, children with this type may not have as many physical characteristics, delays, and disabilities as children with other types of Down syndrome.

Prognosis

No cure or specific treatment exists for Down syndrome. The treatment an individual with Down syndrome receives depends on his or her needs. For example, children with impaired hearing may benefit from wearing a hearing aid.

The prognosis for people with Down syndrome depends on the severity of their health problems. For example, the prognosis for children with severe heart defects or who develop leukemia may be poor. Those without serious health problems often live to be fifty-five or sixty years old. The most common cause of death is heart disease.

Many people with Down syndrome are able to live fulfilling lives. Children with Down syndrome typically live at home and go to school. They may be in a special education classroom or mainstreamed. In time, they are able to hold jobs and have meaningful relationships.

Words to Know

impulsivity - A tendency to act without thinking.

mainstreamed - Included in regular classrooms.

nucleus - The center of a cell.

Obesity: Why Healthy Habits Matter

Maya and Leo explore why obesity is rising worldwide — what causes it, who it affects most, and how healthier school lunches, exercise, and sleep can help prevent and reverse it.

Original Text

Source: Source link

FULL TEXT:

Students at Doherty Middle School in Andover, Massachusetts, get healthy lunch choices at the school cafeteria. The Andover public school district has been making an effort to give its students healthy lunches to help combat obesity. Food choices at the school include yogurt, healthy hot meals, and fresh vegetables and fruit from the salad bar.

Obesity is a health condition characterized by having too much body fat. It is defined as a body mass index (BMI) of 30 or higher in adults. Having obesity can increase the risk of diseases and health problems. Some of these are heart disease, high blood pressure, and Type 2 diabetes. Individuals with obesity can speak with a doctor about ways to lose weight. According to the World Health Organization (WHO), in 2022, about 16 percent of adults worldwide were obese. Additionally, the National Center for Health Statistics estimated in 2024 that 40 percent of American adults were obese.

FAST FACTS - According to the World Health Organization, in 2024, Pacific Island countries had the highest obesity rates. - According to the American Heart Association, teen boys need an average of 2,200 calories a day, while teen girls need about 1,800 calories a day. Very physically active teens may require more calories. - Many people gain weight when they quit smoking. This weight gain, however, is less damaging to health than smoking.

CAUSES Obesity is the result of consuming more calories than the body uses over time. According to the WHO, several factors influence whether a person consumes more calories than they need. These include environment, genetics, food choices, the availability of healthy foods, and places to get exercise. Some medications can cause weight gain.

Obesity is more likely to affect people with low incomes. Inexpensive food that is easy to prepare tends to contain more calories and sugar. Fast food is another low-cost option, but these items tend to be higher in fat and sodium than home-cooked meals. People with low incomes often face financial barriers to accessing exercise facilities and other health resources.

CONCERNS Obesity can lead to serious health problems. According to the WHO, obesity contributes to breathing problems, cancer, gallbladder disease, heart disease, high blood pressure, high triglycerides, low high-density lipoprotein (HDL) cholesterol, nonalcoholic fatty liver disease, osteoarthritis, and Type 2 diabetes. People with obesity may develop metabolic syndrome. That condition is diagnosed when high blood sugar, high blood pressure, high triglycerides, and low HDL cholesterol occur together.

Obesity among children is a major concern. Children who are overweight or obese are more likely to have health problems, including Type 2 diabetes, high blood pressure, high cholesterol, and breathing problems. According to the journal Childhood Obesity, teens with obesity are much more likely to experience bullying.

CONNECTIONS: THE HEALTHY, HUNGER-FREE KIDS ACT OF 2010 The Healthy, Hunger-Free Kids Act of 2010 was a law that made meals served in schools healthier. This law required that schools serve more fruits, vegetables, and whole grains and limit foods high in sugar, salt, and fat. As a result of this law, students received more nutritious foods at school. After the law was implemented, the rate of obesity in children began to go down in some groups—particularly in children from families with low incomes who eat most of their meals at school.

PREVENTION AND REVERSAL Individuals can take steps to limit weight gain or to lose weight in a healthy way with a doctor's approval. The National Heart, Lung, and Blood Institute (NHLBI) recommends several actions that a person can take to treat or prevent obesity. First, they recommend healthy food choices. These include fruit, vegetables, whole grains, healthy oils, and lean meats. Additionally, lean meats and other foods rich in protein, such as nuts and seeds, are recommended.

Second, the NHLBI recommends exercise. Exercise burns calories and has many other benefits for the body. The WHO recommends that adults get 150 to 300 minutes of moderate aerobic exercise or 75 minutes of vigorous aerobic exercise per week. People can get a combination of moderate and vigorous activity.

Third, the NHLBI recommends getting good-quality sleep. In adults, this is typically seven to eight hours per night. Children and teenagers need more sleep, depending on their age. Teenagers need eight to ten hours of sleep per night. Children aged six to twelve need nine to ten hours of sleep, while younger children need even more sleep.

WORDS TO KNOW - body mass index (BMI): A person's weight in divided by their height squared. - calorie: The energy needed to raise the temperature of 1 gram of water by 1 degree Celsius; used to measure the energy value of foods. - disorder: An abnormal condition. - genetics: Inherited characteristics. - metabolism: The processes of living cells making chemical changes to provide energy.

Wildfires: When Forests Catch Fire

How wildfires start, why most are caused by people, and the brave firefighters who battle them from the sky.

Transcript

Maya: Here's a number that might surprise you, Leo. Every year, about ten million acres of land in the United States burn in wildfires. That is an area bigger than the entire state of Maryland.

Leo: Ten million acres? So what exactly counts as a wildfire?

Maya: A wildfire is an uncontrolled fire in an unpopulated place, like a forest or a grassland. One fire can burn thousands of acres, along with homes in its path.

Leo: How do these fires start? Lightning?

Maya: Some start naturally, from lightning or lava. But about ninety percent are started by people, by accident or on purpose. A thrown-away cigarette, an unsupervised campfire, fireworks, or arson can all ignite one.

Leo: Ninety percent? So most wildfires are our own fault. What keeps a fire burning?

Maya: Three ingredients, called the fire triangle. Heat, oxygen, and fuel. The sun gives heat, the air gives oxygen, and plants and wood give fuel.

Leo: And if you take one away, the fire dies?

Maya: Exactly. The worst conditions are hot, dry weather with low humidity. Droughts and heat waves make things worse, drying out grass, leaves, and twigs. These dried bits are called flash fuels, and they burn fast and hot.

Leo: Does wind matter too?

Maya: A lot. Wind feeds the fire extra oxygen and pushes it faster. A big wildfire can even create its own wind, up to ten times faster than the natural wind. Wind can also carry flames into the tops of trees, causing crown fires.

Leo: What about the shape of the land?

Maya: The arrangement of land is called topography. Wildfires race uphill much faster than downhill, because heat rises and preheats everything above the flames. Shall we do the words now?

Maya: Let's do our vocabulary spotlight. Ten words today. Word one is catastrophic, which means causing terrible and sudden damage. The wildfire caused catastrophic damage across the region.

Leo: Word two is ignite, which means to set something on fire or catch fire. A single spark can ignite a whole forest.

Maya: Word three is arson, which means the crime of setting fire to something on purpose. Arson is one cause of human-made wildfires.

Leo: Word four is humidity, which means the amount of moisture in the air. Low humidity helps wildfires spread quickly.

Maya: Word five is exacerbate, which means to make a problem worse. A drought can exacerbate the spread of a wildfire.

Leo: Word six is topography, which means the shape and arrangement of the land. Steep topography makes wildfires race uphill.

Maya: Word seven is contain, which means to keep something inside a boundary so it cannot spread. Firefighters worked all night to contain the blaze.

Leo: Word eight is firebreak, which means a strip of land cleared of plants to stop a fire from spreading. The fire ran out of fuel when it reached the firebreak.

Maya: Word nine is smokejumper, which means a firefighter who parachutes from a plane to fight small fires in remote places. Smokejumpers are true heroes in the sky.

Leo: And word ten is backfiring, which means setting a small fire on purpose to burn up fuel ahead of a wildfire. Firefighters use backfiring as a last resort.

Maya: Great words. Now let me tell you about the worst wildfires in recent history. In Australia, wildfires are called bushfires. In twenty nineteen, extremely high temperatures and severe drought caused more than one hundred bushfires across the country.

Leo: More than a hundred at once? What happened?

Maya: They burned more than twenty-seven million acres, destroyed over three thousand homes, and killed more than one billion animals. About ten thousand koalas died. Heavy rain in February of twenty twenty finally put most of them out.

Leo: Over a billion animals. That breaks my heart. How do firefighters fight a fire that big?

Maya: Aircraft like air tankers and helicopters drop thousands of gallons of water and fire retardant. The goal may be to stop the fire, or at least to contain it so it cannot keep spreading.

Leo: And people on the ground?

Maya: Hotshots work in teams of twenty, clearing plants to make a strip of bare land called a firebreak. When the fire reaches it, there is nothing left to burn. Smokejumpers are paratroopers who sky dive from aircraft to attack small fires in remote places.

Leo: Such brave work. What if nothing works?

Maya: As a last resort, firefighters use backfiring. They light a small fire on purpose to burn away the fuel ahead of the wildfire. A fire keeps burning until one part of the fire triangle, heat, oxygen, or fuel, is gone.

Leo: Fighting fire with fire, literally.

Maya: Exactly. Now a quick quiz. Question one. What are the three ingredients of the fire triangle?

Leo: Listeners, think for a moment. Heat, oxygen, and fuel.

Maya: Question two. True or false. Wildfires spread faster downhill than uphill.

Leo: Think it through. False. They spread faster uphill, because heat rises and preheats the land above.

Maya: Last one. The word exacerbate most nearly means improve, worsen, ignite, or contain?

Leo: Take a guess. It means worsen, like a drought worsening a wildfire.

Maya: You did great. So remember, wildfires need heat, oxygen, and fuel, most are started by people, and the bravest firefighters drop from the sky to stop them. See you next time on The World, Word by Word.

Original Text

Turks - Document - Gale In Context: Middle School Source: Source link (assignment URL; also listed in mom's doc as ZNBVVT689246562 under [History] "Turks")

Full Text

Introduction

Turks, term applied in its wider meaning to the Turkic-speaking peoples of Turkey, Russia, Central Asia, Xinjiang in China (Chinese Turkistan), Azerbaijan and the Caucasus, Iran, and Afghanistan. They total about 125 million, and they are distributed from E Siberia to the Balkans. The wide differences in physical appearance and culture among the Uigurs of China, the Uzbeks of central Asia, and the Osmanlis of Turkey (to cite random instances) make it impossible to speak of Turks as an ethnic or racial group. Although Islam is the religion of the majority of Turks, its importance came relatively late. The most significant unifying link among the Turks is the very close relation of their languages, which are marked by great regularity of pattern and clarity of structure. It is probable that many peoples who were unrelated to the original Turks adopted either wholly or in part their speech and their social organization. The Avars were probably Turkic; they and the Magyars certainly had adopted the Turkic tribal organization when they appeared in Europe, and many Magyar words are of Turkic origin.

The Osmanlis

In Asia Minor the sultanate of Konya was taken over, after the Mongol wave had receded, by the emirate of Karamania, but the Osmanli Turks completed the overthrow of the Byzantine Empire. A minor tribe and the last of the Turkish invading peoples, the Osmanli had been assigned (13th cent.) to the border area of the Byzantine Empire by their Seljuk overlords. It was largely this position as guards of a constantly contested frontier that allowed them to develop their highly disciplined organization, which in turn enabled them in the 14th cent. to make themselves masters of the ruins of the Seljuk empire in Anatolia. Their first historic ruler Osman I, gave his name both to the nation and to the dynasty that ruled an empire extending, at one period, from Vienna to the Indian Ocean and from Tunis to the Caucasus. The people of modern Turkey, which was founded after the collapse of the Ottoman Empire in 1918, are called Osmanli Turks. The original Osmanlis had merged at an early stage with the Seljuks, and their descendants mixed extensively with Muslim converts from the many dozens of nationalities that made up their empire.

Successor States

Among the successor states were the Zangid sultanate of Syria, whose ruler Nur ad-Din was known for his victories over the Crusaders; the empire of Khwarazm, which at one time nearly attained the limits of the earlier Seljuk empire; and the sultanate of Rum or Iconium, which comprised a large part of Asia Minor. All the Seljuk states were overrun in the 13th cent. by Jenghiz Khan and his successors, whose hordes comprised both Mongols and Turks and became generally known as Tatars. The Turko-Tatars now living in the nations of the Commonwealth of Independent States are largely descended from the Golden Horde of Batu Khan, as are the Uzbeks, who ruled a vast empire in the 16th cent.

Seljuk Empire

At the beginning of the 11th cent. a great wave of Seljuk Turks, led by Tughril Beg, conquered Khwarazm and Iran. They entered Baghdad in 1055; Tughril Beg was proclaimed sultan. Under his successor, Alp Arslan, the Seljuks conquered Georgia, Armenia, and much of Asia Minor, overran Syria, and defeated (1071) the Byzantine emperor Romanus IV at Manzikert, opening Byzantium (except for a small area around Constantinople) to Seljuk and Turkmen occupation. This irruption was a major factor in bringing about the Crusades, during which a three-part struggle among Christians, Seljuks, and Egyptian Mamluks developed. Alp Arslan's son, Malikshah (reigned 1072-92), ably administered and developed his huge empire; he was a protector of Omar Khayyam, who reformed the calendar at his behest. At the start of the 12th cent. the Seljuk empire began to fragment, and various parts achieved virtual independence. The attacks of the Khwarazm shah led to the final downfall of the empire in 1157.

Early Migrations and Empires

The name Turk was first used by the Chinese in the 6th cent. to designate a nomadic people who had established a large empire stretching from Mongolia to the Black Sea. This empire, which was divided into two independent parts, was forced to accept Chinese sovereignty in the 7th cent. The northern empire regained its independence in 682, and the oldest known Turkic inscriptions are related to it. In succeeding centuries control of the area passed from the Oghuz Turks to the Uigurs and to the Kyrgyz, who were the last Turkic peoples to reside in Mongolia. They, like their predecessors, migrated to the south and west after they were expelled (924) by the Kitai. Other Turkic peoples, notably the Khazars, Cumans, and Pechenegs, played important roles in the medieval history of S Russia and SE Europe. The Turkish groups of the greatest import in the history of Europe and W Asia were, however, the Seljuks and the Osmanli or Ottoman Turks, both members of the Oghuz confederations. The Arab annexation of the area of ancient Sogdiana in the 7th cent. brought the Oghuz Turks into direct contact with the Abbasid caliphate and later with the Persian Empire. The Turks embraced the Sunni Muslim faith and began to migrate to the Middle East. At first they were used as mercenaries by the Abbasids, but soon the Turks became the actual rulers of the empire.

Original Text

# Refugees in the United States - Document - Gale In Context: Middle School
Source: https://link.gale.com/apps/doc/XQRMRT378837503/MSIC?u=sunnyvale15&sid=bookmark-MSIC&xid=587725ba
Reading Levels: 790L / 1010L

## Full Text

Refugees from Haiti [Image: Refugees from Haiti cross the Rio Grande natural border between Mexico and the United States to request political asylum in 2021.]

A refugee is a person from one country seeking refuge, or safety, in another country. Most refugees flee their home country to escape war or persecution based on race, religion, nationality, or political opinion.

## Fast Facts

- Since 1975, three million refugees have resettled in the United States.
- Refugee resettlement in the United States is traditionally offered to the most vulnerable, including women and children, the elderly, survivors of torture, and those in need of urgent medical care.

The legal basis for allowing refugees into the United States began with the Refugee Act of 1980. This act defined a refugee, established the Reception and Placement (R&P) program to resettle refugees, and created the Office of Refugee Resettlement.

The United States accepts only a limited number of refugees each year. This number is determined by the president in conjunction with Congress. In 2021, the United States planned to accept fifteen thousand refugees. This is a small fraction of the twenty-six million refugees in the world.

Refugees in the United States come from all over the world. To be allowed to resettle in the country, they must first be selected by the United Nations High Commissioner for Refugees (UNHCR). Then they are thoroughly vetted by US security agencies including the FBI (Federal Bureau of Investigation).

Once in the United States, refugees are allowed to work. After being granted a green card, they must reside in the United States for five years before applying for US citizenship.

For decades, the United States was the world leader in refugee resettlement. However, the number of refugees allowed to enter the country plummeted during Donald Trump's presidency.

## Refugee Limits

The United States is one of thirty-seven countries that resettles refugees. Only about 1 percent of the world's 26 million refugees are resettled in these countries.

In the United States, the president in consultation with Congress determines the number of refugees to be resettled in the country each year. This is called the Presidential Declaration. Each fall, the president sets a limit on the number of refugees that may enter the country in a fiscal year. Throughout history, the United States has resettled more refugees than any other country. However, this number was drastically reduced when Donald Trump became president. In 2016, the United States resettled nearly 85,000 refugees. For 2017, President Barack Obama sought to admit 110,000 refugees. However, after Trump took office, he reduced this number by more than half via executive order.

In 2018, Trump further reduced the refugee limit to 45,000 and in 2019 reduced it again to 30,000. For 2020, he set the limit at 18,000 and for 2021 reduced it to 15,000.

## Connections: Syrian Refugees

A civil war in Syria that began in 2011 killed hundreds of thousands of people. It also destroyed hospitals, schools, and water and sanitation systems. The war created about 5.6 million Syrian refugees with another 6.2 million trapped in the country and in need of assistance. About 60 percent of the refugees are children.

In 2016, the United States resettled about 15,500 Syrian refugees. In 2020, only 134 Syrian refugees were admitted to the United States even though about 600,000 were in need of resettlement. This was mainly because the Trump administration had sharply cut the number of refugees allowed in the country.

## Vetting

Refugees wishing to settle in the United States are thoroughly vetted. They are first referred to the country by the United Nations High Commissioner for Refugees (UNHCR). The UNHCR screens them to determine if they are in need of resettlement. The organization gives priority to families that have been targeted by violence.

After this, they are vetted by security agencies in the United States. They include the FBI (Federal Bureau of Investigation), the DHS (Department of Homeland Security), and the DOD (Department of Defense.) Refugees usually undergo several background checks, screenings, and interviews. While the vetting process usually takes twenty-four months, it can take up to thirty-six months.

## Resettlement

Before they resettle in the United States, refugees must sign a promissory note that they will reimburse the US government for travel expenses. It is also determined where in the country they will live and which refugee resettlement agency will assist them. A resettlement agency arranges for housing, appropriate clothing, and food. Refugees must also learn to speak and write in English. A resettlement agency also assists them in enrolling in English classes.

Refugees receive a stipend for their first three months in the country. However, they are encouraged to find work immediately. Agencies contact local employers on their behalf. Some of these employers are former refugees or immigrants. Most refugees find work within three months.

## Words to Know

**fiscal year**
A year for the purpose of budgeting that usually does not begin on January 1.

**resettle**
To move permanently to a new place.

**vetted**
Checked out carefully.

Franklin D. Roosevelt: America's Four-Term President

Maya and Leo tell the story of Franklin Delano Roosevelt — the only president elected four times, paralyzed by polio at 39, who faced the Great Depression with the New Deal and guided America through World War II after Pearl Harbor — plus ten vocabulary words and a three-question quiz.
