Original Text

Source: Gale In Context: Middle School — "Franklin D. Roosevelt" (Document Number GALE|JRMJWH435856237) URL: Source link Retrieved: 2026-09-10

---

Franklin D. Roosevelt was the thirty-second president of the United States. He was first elected in 1932 during a time of great suffering for the American people during the Great Depression, the period from 1929 to about 1939 when the American stock market crashed, economies around the world shrank, and millions of people were thrown into poverty. Roosevelt led the United States with his New Deal programs, a series of laws enacted throughout the 1930s to help strengthen the US economy, so it could recover from the disastrous effects of the Great Depression.

Roosevelt then had to guide the United States through World War II, which America entered near the end of 1941. This was a global war in which the United Kingdom, the United States, the Soviet Union, and other countries known as the Allies, fought against the Axis, which consisted of Germany, Japan, and Italy. The war finally ended with an Allied victory in 1945. Roosevelt’s success in leading America through the Great Depression and a world war made him a popular president throughout his time in office; in fact, he is the only US president to have been elected four times. He died while still holding the office in 1945.

Roosevelt often spoke to the American people over the radio during his presidency in addresses that came to be known as fireside chats. He did this to inform and calm the country during the severe conditions caused by the Great Depression and World War II. During his inaugural address as president in 1933, Roosevelt delivered a line that later became famous in American political history: “The only thing we have to fear is fear itself.” One of Roosevelt’s longest-lasting Depression-era programs was Social Security, which provided, and continues to provide, money to the elderly and disabled individuals among others.

Franklin Delano Roosevelt was born on January 30, 1882, in Hyde Park, New York, to parents James and Sara Roosevelt. James was a wealthy businessperson who later ensured his only child became well educated.

Roosevelt enrolled at Groton School in Massachusetts when he was fourteen. He later attended Harvard College where he developed an interest in politics, which was also inspired by his fifth cousin, Theodore Roosevelt, who became the US president in 1901. Roosevelt devoted himself to the Democratic Party while at Harvard.

He married Eleanor Roosevelt, Theodore Roosevelt’s niece, in March 1905. Roosevelt started studying law at Columbia University in New York City around this time, but he eventually grew bored with law school and accepted a job on New York’s Wall Street.

Roosevelt was elected to the New York State Senate in 1910 and reelected in 1912. From 1913 to 1920, he served as assistant secretary of the US Navy under President Woodrow Wilson, which included the country’s involvement in World War I. Democratic leaders saw Roosevelt as a major rising personality in the party, naming him the party’s vice-presidential candidate alongside James M. Cox in the 1920 presidential election. However, the Democrats lost the election to Republican Warren Harding.

In 1921, Roosevelt contracted polio, a disease that can cause paralysis, leaving him unable to move his legs. Over the next few years, he strengthened his legs through swimming and other forms of exercise. He was eventually able to stand and move about, but with some difficulty.

Roosevelt returned to politics in the late 1920s and was elected governor of New York in 1928. Beginning in 1929, he dedicated much of his time as governor to the New York economy. The US stock market crashed in October 1929, resulting in millions of Americans losing all the money they had invested. Americans across the country were suddenly poor and jobless. This was the start of the Great Depression. Roosevelt quickly passed laws to help the people of New York, including setting aside money for the elderly and unemployed. He was reelected as governor in 1930.

1882: Born on January 30 in Hyde Park, New York 1905: Marries Eleanor Roosevelt 1910: Elected to New York Senate 1920: Runs unsuccessfully for US vice president 1921: Contracts polio 1932: Elected president 1936: Reelected president 1940: Elected to a third term as president 1944: Elected to a fourth term as president 1945: Dies on April 12 in Warm Springs, Georgia

Roosevelt ran as the Democratic candidate for president of the United States in 1932, defeating Herbert Hoover, the incumbent Republican president. Roosevelt took office in March 1933 and immediately started working to help Americans most affected by the Great Depression.

Roosevelt’s Great Depression laws were known as the New Deal. They were aimed at providing money directly to the poor and helping farmers who otherwise would have had to give up their farms. Roosevelt also assisted the more than thirteen million Americans who were unemployed, creating organizations such as the Public Works Administration, which hired unemployed Americans to work on public building projects.

Roosevelt was easily reelected president in 1936. By this time, the US economy had started to recover but a new crisis was on the horizon. Adolf Hitler was preparing Nazi Germany for conquest of the European continent, and Japan and Italy, Germany’s allies, were also on the rise. World War II officially began in 1939 when Germany invaded Poland.

Roosevelt was elected to a third term as president in 1940, ending the tradition of presidents seeking only two terms in office. He wanted to keep the United States out of the war, but when Japan bombed the US naval base at Pearl Harbor, Hawaii, on December 7, 1941, he had no choice. Calling December 7 “A date which will live in infamy”, Roosevelt led the United States in declaring war on Japan, Germany, and their Axis allies.

Roosevelt worked closely with leaders of the United Kingdom and the Soviet Union in planning to defeat Germany and Japan, and their efforts finally turned the tide of the war against these two powers. During the war, Roosevelt also tried to prepare for the post-war world. His ideas contributed to the eventual creation of the United Nations, an alliance of countries that works to this day to promote world peace.

Roosevelt’s health worsened as the war was ending. Despite this, he was elected to a fourth term as president in 1944. He did not live to see the end of World War II. He died of brain bleeding in Warm Springs, Georgia, on April 12, 1945. The war ended that September. Many historians consider Roosevelt one of the greatest US presidents because of his leadership during the Great Depression and World War II, two of the most challenging crises the country has ever faced.

Original Text

# Great Depression - Document - Gale In Context: Middle School
Source: https://link.gale.com/apps/doc/BJJXBI592133078/MSIC?u=sunnyvale15&sid=bookmark-MSIC&xid=20bc99b2
Reading Levels: 810L / 1030L

## Full Text

The Great Depression was a time of economic crisis in the United States and other countries. After a 1929 stock market crash, many people lost their savings. Businesses suffered and unemployment skyrocketed. Meanwhile, American farmers faced devastating droughts. Problems in the United States spread overseas, creating a global depression. President Franklin D. Roosevelt relieved some of the problems with a program called the New Deal. However, only the start of World War II (1939–1945) returned vigor to the economy.

### Fast Facts

- During the New Deal years, almost nine million Americans found new jobs working for government projects.
- During the Great Depression, industrial production in the United States declined by almost half.
- Some other countries hit hardest by the global depression included Germany, Italy, Canada, Poland, and Czechoslovakia.
- The economic depression that began in the United States in 1929 had spread to Japan, India, and South Africa by 1930.

### Background and Beginning

After World War I (1914–1918) ended, the United States experienced a time of great prosperity. The country had participated in the final two years of the war and had helped to bring victory to the Allied nations. The war greatly boosted America’s reputation and standing around the world. In addition, all of the economic activity involved in training and equipping the armed forces had created many new technologies, businesses, and jobs. With the war over and victorious soldiers returning to domestic work, the American economy was booming.

The 1920s saw many Americans growing wealthy and enjoying more relaxed and luxurious lifestyles. During this time, many investors turned to the stock market, a system by which individuals may purchase small pieces, or shares, of ownership in companies. People poured millions of dollars into the stock market, and the value of stocks rose for several years. Investors made huge profits during this time, and many people believed the stock market increases would continue.

In September 1929, the rise of the stock market halted, making investors panic. The following month, the market crashed, or dropped sharply and continuously. The value of stocks dropped so much that many stocks became practically worthless. Many investors across the country lost all of their investments and were now bankrupt.

### Effects in the United States and Beyond

The stock market crash of 1929 was the major cause of the Great Depression, although other previously overlooked weaknesses in the economy added to the problems. Following the crash, many people were left broke. They were unable to repay debts, pay employees, or even buy food. Banks began to fail because many people were unable to return borrowed money and many others rushed to withdraw their savings. Bank failures caused even more grief. People had difficulty withdrawing their money from banks that had closed, or lost their money entirely.

The lack of money and the panic caused economic activity to drop enormously. Many businesses downsized or shut down because nobody could afford to buy their products. Closing businesses led to high unemployment, with about twelve million Americans out of work by 1932. The hardship extended far beyond the industrialized cities, however. In the American Great Plains, the agricultural heartland of the country, farmers faced their own trouble. A long drought dried out their farms and killed their crops, leaving the land a so-called Dust Bowl.

The economic woes of the United States quickly spread to other countries. American companies could not afford to produce exports or buy imports. Many countries that routinely traded with the United States found themselves without a prized partner. Countries that relied on financial aid and loans from the United States were suddenly left without help. Many of these countries reacted to their economic damages rashly. They reduced their own trading and production, leading to a cycle of loss and failure.

### Connections: The Dust Bowl

In the 1930s, in the midst of the economic problems, a huge drought struck the Great Plains. Large parts of Colorado, Kansas, Texas, Oklahoma, and New Mexico dried up. Millions of farm crops withered. Fields turned to dust. Windstorms crossing the Great Plains kicked up huge dust storms that looked like brown blizzards. This land, formerly the breadbasket of the nation, was now known as the Dust Bowl. Many farmers went out of business and migrated to other areas to restart their lives. Slowly, however, the situation improved. Government agencies sent help and taught farmers new planting methods to help retain water in the soil. In the 1940s, the lengthy drought ceased. The Great Plains once again came alive with crops and farms.

### The New Deal and End of the Depression

After years of suffering and with no hope in sight, Americans sought a political change. In 1932, they ousted President Herbert Hoover and elected Franklin D. Roosevelt to the presidency. Roosevelt promised major improvements through a wide-reaching platform he called the New Deal. The New Deal contained many programs that were meant to address the immediate problems as well as safeguard against future problems.

The immediate relief from the New Deal came in the form of new jobs. The government created these jobs to both help the country’s resources and infrastructure and to provide unemployed workers with living wages. Large government organizations such as the Works Progress Administration and the Civilian Conservation Corps oversaw the new jobs and laborers. These groups operated in many public places, including national forests, highways, public buildings, and communities. New Deal workers performed tasks as varied as building dams, paving highways, painting murals, and cleaning state parks.

Other New Deal innovations aimed at strengthening the core of the American economy and avoiding future financial disasters. One such policy was to create the Federal Deposit Insurance Corporation (FDIC). This organization would protect people’s money in the case of bank closures. Another policy was Social Security, a system that uses citizen investments to support retired or disabled workers.

Even with the overall success of the New Deal, the road to recovery for the United States proved slow. The lingering effects of the Great Depression lasted throughout the 1930s. Only in 1941, when the United States entered World War II, did the Great Depression truly end. Much like the previous war, World War II provided America with a huge economic stimulus, and again factories and other businesses thrived as they created food, supplies, and weapons for the armed forces.

### Words to Know

**bankrupt** — Not having any money.

**depression** — A time of decreased financial activity.

**infrastructure** — The systems inside a country that allow it to operate efficiently, such as roads and airports.

**unemployment** — Not having jobs for all workers.

HistoryEpisode 175:59

The Industrial Revolution: Machines That Changed the World

Maya and Leo explore the Industrial Revolution: how the flying shuttle, spinning jenny, cotton gin, and steam engine replaced handmade goods with factories, how consumerism and huge cities grew, and the dark side of child labor and the Luddites, plus ten vocabulary words.

mechanization

Original Text

Industrial Revolution - Document - Gale In Context: Middle School
Source: Source link
Gale Document Number: GALE|GFJQHX766524044

The Industrial Revolution was a period during the 1700s and 1800s when the methods by which people created material goods changed greatly. Prior to this time, most goods were created by hand. The invention of advanced machines allowed production materials (such as cotton) and final products (such as clothing) to be made at faster rates. The Industrial Revolution led to the rise of factories and consumerism in much of the world. It helped shape human history in many ways.

Fast Facts

- The Industrial Revolution began in Britain and spread to the United States. Most other countries lagged behind due to lack of money for business, political turmoil, and other problems.

- Not everyone was happy about the Industrial Revolution. In the 1800s, a group of disgruntled British workers called the Luddites began destroying textile factories. The Luddites felt they were freeing society of oppression by machines.

- During the late 1800s, the United States and Japan became world industrial leaders. However, many other places, such as China, India, and Russia, did not become truly industrialized until the mid-1900s.

Background

Prior to the 1700s, people crafted most products by hand. With most families living in small rural communities, stores and workshops were limited in number and offerings. People had few material wants, and most of these could be met at home. Families often built their own houses using logs, stones, or other easily gathered materials. People also made their own foods, clothing, and simple tools.

Skilled craftspeople made most specialized items, such as metal tools and firearms, in small workshops. Even relatively complex goods were produced by hand, with each part crafted individually. Each finished product was created from start to finish by one person or a very small number of workers.

Because of the great amount of time and skill that went into each item, material goods were generally rare and costly. Advances in technology, however, drastically and permanently changed the means of production.

Beginning of the Industrial Revolution

The first innovations in the Industrial Revolution appeared in Britain's textile industry. Spinning wool and weaving fabric by hand were very time-consuming. The introduction of a simple but ingenious machine called the flying shuttle in 1733 greatly reduced the time and effort required to create good cloth. The creation of the spinning jenny in 1770 further increased textile production, making Britain the world leader.

Although British authorities guarded their new machines, word spread, along with the spirit of innovation. Inventors in the United States began creating their own revolution. Foremost among these inventors was Eli Whitney, who in 1793 introduced the cotton gin. This machine cleaned cotton much faster than any person could. The cotton gin turned the cotton industry in the American South into an international powerhouse.

The next major stride in the Industrial Revolution also originated with Eli Whitney. This innovation was interchangeable parts, a concept that involved workers creating many identical parts during the manufacturing process rather than creating each part individually. Identical parts meant greater standardization and faster production. It also allowed less-skilled people to replace worn or broken parts on goods with ease.

Industrial machines grew increasingly large and powerful. The creation of the steam engine in the later 1700s brought power to many of these great machines. Increased understanding and use of industrial materials such as coal, iron, and steel fed the Industrial Revolution.

Connections: The Second Industrial Revolution

Some historians believe a second wave of industrial innovations in the 1900s may be considered the Second Industrial Revolution. During this time, new production materials and energy sources meant that products could be created more quickly and cheaply than ever. The introduction of plastics and new metallic alloys ramped up production to unseen heights and allowed the creation of increasingly powerful and complex goods. The Second Industrial Revolution helped usher the world into the digital era of mass-produced computer technology.

Development and Effects

New industrial machines and interchangeable parts contributed to the beginning of the factory system. Factories began to replace workshops in many parts of Britain, the United States, and other countries. In factories, groups of workers, generally less skilled than the craftspeople of prior eras, would use machines to produce large quantities of identical products. The supply of goods increased, and their prices fell. Now even common people could afford a variety of mass-produced goods. Consumerism slowly grew into a defining feature of many societies.

The ideals of mechanization spread to other fields as well, notably agriculture. Whereas agriculture had once been an extremely laborious hands-on task, new farming machines made the work much faster and easier. New inventions allowed farmers to plow, plant, and harvest with great speed. At the same time, innovations in transportation, such as the steamboat and railroad, allowed crops and other goods, along with people, to travel with speed and ease.

The rise of factories and the reduction in manpower needed on farms led to a major social shift. Millions of people who had traditionally lived in rural communities moved to cities in search of factory jobs. This caused cities to grow to vast sizes. Although cities offered people many new opportunities, early cities were often overcrowded, dirty, and depressing. In addition, newcomers to cities often scrambled to cope with low factory wages, leading to a variety of ills including child labor.

Words to Know

consumerism - The social idea that buying goods is an important part of life.
interchangeable - Able to be switched or replaced easily.
mechanization - The widespread use of machines and technology.

HistoryEpisode 166:04

Adolf Hitler and the Nazi Regime

Maya and Leo trace how Adolf Hitler rose from a failed artist to Germany's dictator — and why historians say studying his regime means learning to recognize the warning signs of prejudice and unchecked power.

Full episode

6:04 · Story, knowledge, and vocabulary

Download full episode

Words in This Episode

1:54 · Slow vocabulary review

Download words review
Words in this episode
dictator
a ruler with absolute power“Adolf Hitler was the dictator of Germany in the nineteen thirties and forties.”
regime
a government, especially one that rules in a strict or harsh way“The Nazi regime controlled all the media in Germany.”
propaganda
information, often biased, that is designed to persuade or influence people“The Nazis used propaganda films to spread their ideas.”
coup
a sudden, violent takeover of a government“Hitler's nineteen twenty-three coup attempt failed, and he went to prison.”
censorship
the suppression of speech or media by those in power“Under Nazi censorship, newspapers could not criticize the government.”
chancellor
the head of the government in Germany“Hitler became chancellor of Germany in nineteen thirty-three.”
prejudice
an unfair dislike of a group of people“Nazi prejudice against Jewish people fueled the Holocaust.”
anti-Semitism
hatred of, or discrimination against, Jewish people“The Nazi regime promoted anti-Semitism through laws and propaganda.”
persecution
cruel treatment of a group over a long time“The persecution of Jewish people in Nazi Germany grew into the Holocaust.”
surrender
to give up in a conflict or war“France surrendered in nineteen forty, and Germany surrendered in nineteen forty-five.”
Original Text

Source: Gale In Context: Middle School — "Adolf Hitler" (GALE|ZMWWAR439188455)
URL: https://link.gale.com/apps/doc/ZMWWAR439188455/MSIC?u=sunnyvale15&sid=bookmark-MSIC&xid=5dc4c477

Born: April 20, 1889 in Braunau, Austria
Died: April 30, 1945 in Berlin, Germany
Nationality: German
Occupation: Dictator
Other Names: Der Fuhrer

Adolf Hitler was the leader of the Nazi Party and the dictator of Germany for much of the 1930s and 1940s. He was a central figure in World War II (1939–1945). He was also largely responsible for the Holocaust, a massive genocide that claimed the lives of six million Jews, among other groups.

FAST FACTS
- Early in life, Hitler had dreams of becoming an artist.
- Hitler fought for the German army in World War I.
- Hitler published his autobiography, Mein Kampf, in 1925.
- In 1933, Hitler became chancellor of Germany.
- Hitler committed suicide at the end of World War II.

EARLY LIFE
Adolf Hitler was born on April 20, 1889, in Braunau am Inn, Austria, to Alois and Klara Hitler. Growing up, he enjoyed a comfortable life. He attended school until the age of sixteen. Hitler wished to become an artist, so in 1907 he attempted to get into the Vienna Academy of Fine Arts in Vienna, Austria, but was rejected. After his mother died, he moved to Vienna. There, he lived an unusual life, painting postcards and staying in hostels, places where people can rent beds cheaply.

Hitler moved to Munich, Germany, in 1913. The following year, World War I (1914–1918) began. Hitler enlisted in the German army, fought in the war, and was wounded on two separate occasions. Germany ultimately surrendered in 1918 and subsequently signed the Treaty of Versailles, an international peace agreement that strictly limited Germany's power after the war. Hitler vowed to restore the nation's power.

LEADER OF THE NAZI PARTY
Following the war, Hitler was sent to Munich to spy on the German Workers' Party, which was a growing new political group that had extreme nationalist and anti-Semitic views. Hitler realized that he agreed with the group's views, so he joined. The group soon changed its name to the National Socialist German Workers' Party, or the Nazi Party. In 1921, Hitler became the party's leader.

As leader of the Nazi Party, Hitler turned the party into a military group. In 1923, he attempted a coup, or takeover of the government, but failed. He was arrested for high treason and sentenced to five years of imprisonment, but he served only nine months. While imprisoned, Hitler wrote Mein Kampf (My Struggle), his autobiography. Published in 1925, the book outlines Hitler's political views, including his belief in military expansion and his desire for the elimination of the Jewish people. Hitler believed that the Aryan race, or people of Northern European racial background, was supreme and all other races were inferior and should serve the Aryan race. Furthermore, he believed Jews were unfit to serve and should be eliminated. Hitler eventually acted on his views from Mein Kampf.

Quotes from Mein Kampf (given in the article, for reference):
- "If you want to shine like sun first you have to burn like it. I do not see why man should not be as cruel as nature."
- "When diplomacy ends, War begins."
- "To conquer a nation, first disarm its citizens."
- "It is not truth that matters, but victory."
- "Anyone can deal with victory. Only the mighty can bear defeat."

BECOMES CHANCELLOR OF GERMANY
The Nazi Party quickly rose to power in Germany. Hitler himself was becoming a powerful political figure and was appointed chancellor, or head of the government, of Germany in 1933 by President Paul von Hindenburg. Less than a month after he became chancellor, Hitler found a way to further consolidate his power. On February 27, 1933, the Reichstag building caught fire. Hitler declared that this was an attack by communists on the German government. He used the alleged arson as a reason to declare a state of emergency. As part of his new policies, he suspended free speech, freedom of the press, and the rights of those accused of crimes. Next, he declared that the central government would now control state and local governments as well. This allowed him to push Nazi policies on all parts of Germany and led to total Nazi control of all media.

In the fall of 1933, Hitler held his first Nuremberg Rally as chancellor. These annual gatherings of the Nazi Party — and especially young Nazis — allowed him to directly influence his followers. The rallies were days of marches, speeches, and other events, all devoted to promoting Hitler's ideas about a German state based on racial purity. In 1934, Hitler hired Leni Riefenstahl to make a propaganda film about the rally. The result, Triumph of the Will (1935), became a landmark in the art of propaganda. It was shown across Germany and helped Hitler gain even more support. In 1935, Hitler used the Nuremberg Rally to announce his new slate of anti-Jewish laws.

From June 30 to July 2, 1934, Hitler ensured that he alone would control the future of the Nazi Party. The event, known as the Night of the Long Knives, resulted in the elimination of Ernst Röhm, head of the SA paramilitary group. On June 30, Hitler announced that Röhm was planning a coup. While this was a lie, it gave him an excuse to launch a massive purge of the Nazi Party. Hitler's men killed Röhm, other SA leaders, and other political opponents. On July 2, Hitler became the sole leader of the Nazi Party, and only those loyal to him remained in positions of authority.

WORLD WAR II
Hitler set to work expanding the German empire. In 1938, he sent Nazi troops into Austria and quickly assumed control of the nation. Later, he sent troops into the Sudetenland — the German-speaking regions of Czechoslovakia — and annexed those regions as well. Hitler's next military move would lead to World War II. In 1939 he invaded Poland, and in response, England and France quickly declared war. Within a year, Hitler and the Nazis captured Poland, and France officially surrendered. Over the next two years, Hitler made a pair of serious miscalculations that would dramatically change the course of the war. In 1941, he ordered an invasion of the Soviet Union that ended in a stunning defeat for Nazi forces. In 1942 Hitler declared war on the United States. This move awakened a powerful enemy and helped set the stage for Germany's eventual defeat.

Hitler then focused on eliminating the Jewish people of Europe. He established concentration camps, which were designed to murder Jews and other groups the Nazis believed were inferior. In what came to be known as the Holocaust, six million Jews were murdered.

Despite its early success, Germany found itself losing the war by the mid-1940s. Hitler realized this and began hiding with his closest associates and staff members inside a hidden shelter in Berlin for safety. In April 1945, he married Eva Braun, a woman with whom he'd had a longtime romantic relationship. The following day, Hitler and Braun committed suicide to avoid capture.

WORDS TO KNOW (from the article)
- anti-Semitism: Hatred of and discrimination against the Jewish people.
- coup: The violent seizure of political power from a government.
- dictator: A ruler of a nation that has absolute power.
- holocaust: The mass murder of millions of people in Europe by Hitler and the Nazis.
- nationalism: The belief that one's nation is superior to other nations.

HistoryEpisode 156:30

Concentration Camps: Imprisonment Without Trial

What concentration camps are, how they appeared in history from Cuba to Nazi Germany to wartime America, and why a fair trial matters.

Full episode

6:30 · Story, history, and vocabulary

Download full episode

1:43 · Slow vocabulary review

Words in this episode
regime
a government in power, especially one with strict control.“The Nazi regime ruled Germany from 1933 to 1945.”
propaganda
information spread to shape what people believe.“The Nazis used propaganda to blame Jewish people for Germany’s problems.”
persecution
unfair and cruel treatment of a group of people.“Jewish people and other groups faced persecution under the Nazis.”

a smaller group of people within a larger population.“Ethnic minorities have often been targeted in history.”

Original Text

Source: Source link
(Gale In Context: Middle School — "Concentration Camps")

A concentration camp is a place where people are imprisoned without a trial. These camps "concentrate," or confine, people in a small space. Throughout history, political prisoners and ethnic minorities have been held in concentration camps. Some are held for security reasons. Others are exploited by being forced to work under harsh conditions.

Fast Facts:
- More than one hundred thousand Japanese Americans were held in internment camps. In 1988, President Ronald Reagan signed the Civil Liberties Act. It gave each survivor a formal apology and $20,000.
- Anne Frank and her sister Margot are believed to have died of typhus fever in 1945. They were being held in the Bergen-Belson concentration camp.
- It was reported that children in a family detention center in Clint, Texas, had to sleep on concrete floors. They were also denied soap and toothpaste.

A concentration camp is different from an extermination camp. The purpose of an extermination camp is to gather together many people to murder them. Both concentration camps and extermination camps were common in Nazi Germany from 1933 to 1945.

Early Concentration Camps:
Most people associate concentration camps with the Nazi Party. However, concentration camps existed long before this. The very first may have been established in the 1890s during the Cuban "reconcentration." At this time, Cubans wanted independence from Spain and were planning to rebel. To weaken their rebellion, the Spanish military forced thousands of Cubans into concentration camps, where they had little food and lived in crowded conditions. Most of them contracted diseases in the camps and died.

In 1831 and at other times, US forces made Native Americans live in concentration camps. During the "Trail of Tears," tens of thousands of Native Americans were forced from their homes at gunpoint. They were made to march to concentration camps in Alabama and Tennessee. These camps were overcrowded and lacked sanitation. Many people died during the march or in the camps.

During World War II (1939-1945), Japan was an enemy of the United States. This made the US government suspicious of innocent Japanese Americans, who lived and worked in the country. The government forced more than one hundred thousand Japanese Americans into internment camps. These camps were enclosed in barbed wire. Those who tried to escape were shot.

Nazi Germany:
Adolf Hitler became the leader of Germany in 1933. He also ruled the Nazi Party, a political party that had existed in Germany since 1921. Soon after Hitler became the leader of Germany, the first concentration camps were established. They were in Dachau, a city in Germany. At first, the camps held only political prisoners. These were individuals who disagreed with the Nazi Party.

The prisoners in the concentration camps were treated harshly. Some were beaten and others were killed. Hitler declared that this was not against the law. He said that the administrators of concentration camps did not have to follow German laws and could discipline prisoners any way that they chose.

The administrators of these camps used the prisoners as forced laborers. They had to perform difficult physical labor that was sometimes dangerous. In some cases, they were forced to expand the concentration camps in which they were being held.

In 1938, Germany took over Austria. Many Jews lived in Germany and Austria. The Nazis considered them to be an inferior race. They blamed the Jews for problems in Germany. They also considered disabled individuals, homosexuals, Christians, and Roma (gypsies) to be inferior. Hitler and the Nazis wanted to exterminate them all. Thousands of Jews were arrested and sent to concentration camps.

The concentration camps in Dachau and other places were turned into extermination camps, where prisoners were sent to be murdered. This time in history is known as the Holocaust.

Then Germany invaded Poland in 1939. This was the beginning of World War II. By this time, the Nazis had established concentration camps throughout Germany and in Europe. Many of the prisoners now worked to produce weapons that the Germans needed during the war.

The Holocaust ended in 1945 when the Allied powers won World War II, defeating Germany and the other members of the Axis powers. This ended the Nazi Party. Those in camps were rescued and freed.

About 1 million people died in concentration camps during the rule of the Nazi Party, which lasted from 1933 to 1945. Many died of diseases or starvation. Others died from being overworked. This number does not include the number of people murdered in extermination camps.

Words to Know:
- administrators: People who carried out governmental affairs.
- exterminate: To kill all members of a race or species.
- internment: To be interned, which means to be held as a prisoner.

HistoryEpisode 145:42

The Columbian Exchange: When Two Worlds Connected

Maya and Leo trace how crops, animals, people, and diseases crossed the Atlantic after 1492, transforming diets and economies while bringing conquest, slavery, and devastating epidemics.

5:42 · Story, knowledge, and vocabulary

Original Text

Columbian Exchange — Source Reading

The Columbian Exchange describes the widespread movement of plants, animals, people, culture, institutions, and disease between the Eastern and Western Hemispheres after Christopher Columbus’s voyage in 1492. Some transfers were deliberate, while others—especially disease—were unintended.

Crops from the Americas included potatoes, corn, tomatoes, sweet potatoes, squash, cassava, cacao, chili peppers, peanuts, pineapple, and tobacco. These new foods supported population growth in parts of Europe, Africa, and Asia. Europeans carried wheat, rice, oats, bananas, olives, sugarcane, and coffee to the Americas, along with domesticated horses, cattle, sheep, and pigs.

The exchange also brought devastating consequences. Indigenous peoples in the Americas had little or no immunity to smallpox, measles, influenza, and other diseases brought by Europeans and their animals. Epidemics killed millions. European conquest also displaced Indigenous communities, while plantation systems and the transatlantic slave trade forced millions of Africans into bondage.

The Columbian Exchange transformed diets, economies, cultures, and ecosystems around the world. Its history includes both new connections and extraordinary suffering.

HistoryEpisode 134:48

World War II: The War That Changed the World

Maya and Leo explore World War II: why it started, the war in Europe and the Pacific, how it ended in 1945, and the lesson that talking and cooperating beat fighting, plus ten vocabulary words.

Original Text

Source: Gale In Context: Middle School — "World War II" (Document Number GALE|EZWUWH731251588)
URL: Source link
Retrieved: 2026-09-09

Omaha Beach after D-Day [Image caption: Protected by barrage balloons, ships delivered trucks loaded with supplies during World War II in Normandy, France, on June 7-10, 1944. Everett Collection/Shutterstock.com]

World War II was an international war that took place from 1939 to 1945. It started when Germany invaded Poland on September 1, 1939. Adolf Hitler, the leader of Germany, wanted to take over Europe to create more space for the German people to live. At the same time, he wanted to rid the continent of people he considered inferior, including Jewish people and other groups.

The United Kingdom, Soviet Union, United States, and other countries joined forces as the Allied powers. They defeated the Axis powers of Germany, Italy, and Japan in 1945. About sixty million people died in World War II. This included about six million Jewish people killed in Germany's concentration camps. These figures make World War II the deadliest war in history.

Fast Facts

- The Soviet Union's Red Army suffered more losses than any other national military in World War II, with more than 7.5 million deaths.
- If the United States had not dropped atomic bombs on Japan, the American war plan would have called for a land invasion of the Japanese mainland.
- In 1948 the United States created the Marshall Plan, which delivered billions of dollars of food and other aid to war-torn Europe.

Causes of World War II

World War II began for several reasons. World War I had ended in German defeat in 1918. The Allies, consisting of France, the United Kingdom, United States, and others, forced Germany to sign the Treaty of Versailles as punishment for its role in the war. The treaty required Germany to pay reparations to countries it had harmed during the conflict. These reparations embarrassed Germany and hurt its economy.

Another cause of World War II was the rise of dictatorships in Germany, Italy, and Japan in the decades after World War I. The most central of these to World War II was the rise of Adolf Hitler and his National Socialist German Workers' Party, or Nazi Party, in Germany. Hitler wanted to glorify Germany again. He believed the German people were better than all other people. He wanted to take over Europe so Germans could spread out over more land. At the same time, Hitler sought to eliminate Jewish people, disabled people, and anyone else he thought was inferior.

Hitler built up a large army during the 1930s. In 1939 Germany made a nonaggression pact with the Soviet Union. The countries agreed not to attack each other and to divide control of Poland between them. The German army invaded Poland on September 1, 1939. This event began World War II. The United Kingdom and France declared war on Germany on September 3.

Six Years of War

Hitler waited until the spring of 1940 to continue invading other European countries. By June, Germany had invaded Denmark, Norway, Belgium, Luxembourg, the Netherlands, and France. Hitler never tried to occupy the United Kingdom, although German planes heavily bombed London. Germany invaded the Soviet Union in June 1941. Hitler had once been friendly with Soviet leader Joseph Stalin, but he had always intended to invade the Soviet Union. However, the Soviets fiercely fought the Germans over the next several years and prevented them from ever truly occupying Soviet land.

In addition to invading foreign countries, Hitler also arrested Jewish people across Europe. He put them in concentration camps, where they worked without rest or were sent to gas chambers to die. This mass killing of Jews during the war is called the Holocaust.

Timeline: World War II

- 1939: Germany invades and occupies Poland on September 1, beginning World War II.
- 1940: Germany invades and occupies Denmark, Norway, Belgium, Luxembourg, the Netherlands, and France.
- 1941: Germany unsuccessfully invades the Soviet Union.
- 1941: Japan bombs Pearl Harbor, Hawaii, on December 7, drawing the United States into World War II.
- 1944: The Allies invade the beaches of Normandy, France, on June 6.
- 1945: Germany surrenders to the Allies on May 7.
- 1945: The United States drops atomic bombs on Hiroshima and Nagasaki, Japan, in August.
- 1945: Japan surrenders to the Allies on September 2, ending World War II.

The War in the Pacific

In 1931, Japan invaded Manchuria in China. Japan was under economic pressure due to the worldwide Great Depression. It needed raw materials so that it could industrialize and become an equal trading partner with the United States and Europe. From Manchuria, Japan attacked other parts of China. While the Soviet Union tried to halt Japanese expansion, most Western nations sought a diplomatic solution, using trade embargoes to put pressure on Japan.

Japan allied with Germany and Italy, and on December 7, 1941, they launched an attack on the American naval base at Pearl Harbor in Hawaii. Congress declared war on Japan the next day, and Germany and Italy responded by declaring war on the United States. As a result, the United States sent troops to the Pacific and to Europe.

The war in the Pacific was difficult and focused on a strategy known as island-hopping. The United States attacked Japanese islands–even very small ones–to secure supply lines and create new air and naval bases. The US Marines played a major role in the campaign, engaging in brutal combat to take and retain very small areas of land.

End of the War

After careful planning, the Allied powers of the United States, United Kingdom, Canada, and other nations invaded Normandy, France, on June 6, 1944. They planned to defeat the German army there and move east to Germany itself. Meanwhile, Soviet troops would fight Germany in the east and move west. The Allies freed prisoners from the Nazi concentration camps as they progressed.

The Allies had surrounded the German capital of Berlin by April 1945. Rather than be captured, Hitler killed himself in his bunker in the city on April 30. Germany surrendered to the Allies in early May. This ended World War II in Europe.

Japan refused to surrender until the summer of 1945. In August, the United States dropped two atomic bombs on the Japanese cities of Hiroshima and Nagasaki. The bombs killed about 220,000 people. Japan surrendered to the Allies on September 2, 1945. World War II officially ended that day. With about sixty million people killed around the world from 1939 to 1945 due to the conflict, World War II was the most destructive war in history.

Words to Know

- atomic bomb: A bomb that releases a great amount of destructive nuclear energy.
- bunker: A strong underground shelter meant to be used during war.
- concentration camp: A prison camp that holds a large number of people and has poor conditions.
- dictatorship: A form of government in which one person has total power.
- embargo: An official ban on trade with a particular country.
- reparations: Money or other assistance provided to wronged parties by an offender.

HistoryEpisode 125:47

World War I: The Great War

Maya and Leo explore World War I: how the assassination of Franz Ferdinand ignited a chain reaction of alliances, how trenches froze the war for years at Verdun, and how tanks, poison gas, airplanes, and U-boats changed war forever, plus ten vocabulary words.

a situation where nobody can move forward or win“The war became a deadly standoff that lasted for years.”

Original Text

World War I
Source: Gale In Context: Middle School, Document TGBFAC373248689
URL: https://link.gale.com/apps/doc/TGBFAC373248689/MSIC?u=sunnyvale15&sid=bookmark-MSIC&xid=28f59edf
Retrieved: 2026-09-09. Copyright 2026 Gale, a division of Cengage Learning, Inc.
(Note: The article dates the end of the war to November 18, 1918; historically the
armistice was signed on November 11, 1918. The script below uses the historically
correct date.)

FULL TEXT:

Postcard of British Cavalry During World War I. This French postcard shows an
English cavalry regiment heading to the front during World War I. Cavalry units
ceased to be a major offensive factor after World War I.

World War I was a destructive and deadly conflict fought mainly in Europe from
1914 to 1918. More than sixty-five million soldiers from more than forty nations
fought in the war. On one side were the Allied powers which included Great
Britain, France, Russia, the United States, and others. On the opposite side were
the Central Powers of Germany, Austria-Hungary, Bulgaria, and the Ottoman Empire.

World War I was the first time weapons such as tanks, airplanes, and poison gas
were used in battle. The new technology made the war the deadliest the world had
ever seen at the time. By the time the war ended, more than eight million
soldiers and almost seven million civilians had been killed.

FAST FACTS
- German fighter pilot Manfred von Richthofen shot down eighty enemy planes
during the war. That was more than any other pilot on either side. He flew a red
airplane and was known by the nickname the Red Baron.
- About 25,000 miles (40,000 kilometers) of trenches were dug by both sides
during World War I.
- While the British were working on the first armor-covered vehicles, they tried
to keep them a secret. To hide their true purpose, the British called them
"water tanks." The name caught on and soon the vehicles were called "tanks."
- The phrase "over the top" had its origins in World War I. It was used to
describe soldiers who went "over the top" of the trenches to run into battle.
- German submarines were called U-boats, which is short for unterseeboot
(undersea boat). During the war, U-boats sank more than 5,500 ships.

BEGINNING OF WAR
In the late 1800s, Great Britain, France, Russia, and Germany were among the many
nations in Europe that battled each other for power and land. Germany had formed
alliances with several other nations promising to help each other fight in future
wars. At the same time, Germany had also started to build up its military
forces. This caused nations such as Great Britain, France, and Russia to worry
that Germany was planning a war. In response, many of these nations signed their
own alliances to protect themselves against Germany.

On June 28, 1914, Archduke Franz Ferdinand, a member of the royal family of
Austria-Hungary, was shot to death while visiting Bosnia, a nation under the
control of Austria-Hungary. The man who killed Ferdinand was part of a group
that wanted independence for Bosnia. He supported the neighboring country of
Serbia, which thought Bosnia was part of its territory. The rulers of
Austria-Hungary blamed Serbia for the archduke's death. So, on July 28, 1914,
Austria-Hungary declared war on Serbia.

As a result of the many alliances that the nations of Europe had signed, Russia
came to the aid of Serbia. In return, Germany kept a promise to Austria-Hungary
and declared war on Russia. By August 4, France and Great Britain had honored
their alliances and joined the fight against Germany and Austria-Hungary. Other
nations such as Japan and Italy later joined the Allied cause. Bulgaria and the
Ottoman Empire also entered the war on the side of Germany.

TRENCH WARFARE
German leaders believed they would win the war easily if they could quickly
defeat the French. With France out of the way, they thought they could then focus
on war with Russia. In August 1914, the German army began a fast-moving campaign
that moved through Belgium and invaded France. The Germans got very close to
capturing the French capital of Paris before French and British troops stopped
them. The French and British drove the Germans back into northern France.

The machine guns used during the fighting were so deadly that soldiers on both
sides were forced to dig deep trenches in the ground to protect themselves. The
trenches provided some defense, but they also made it difficult for either side
to advance. Soldiers would often run out of the trenches to fight only to be
killed by enemy machine guns. As a result, the Allies and Germans remained locked
in a deadly standoff for years.

Attempts to break through enemy lines often resulted in many deaths with very
little ground gained. For example, during the Battle of Verdun in 1916, more
than seven hundred thousand soldiers were killed or wounded in ten months of
fighting. When the battle ended, both sides were close to the same spot they had
held when it started.

TIMELINE: WORLD WAR I
- June 28, 1914: Archduke Ferdinand is killed in Sarajevo, Bosnia.
- July 28, 1914: Austria-Hungary declares war on Serbia.
- August 1, 1914: Germany declares war on Russia.
- August 3-4, 1914: Germany and France declare war against each other. Germany
invades Belgium in an attempt to reach France. Great Britain responds by
declaring war on Germany.
- April 22, 1915: Germany first uses chemical weapons during the Battle of
Ypres, Belgium.
- May 7, 1915: A German U-boat sinks the British ocean liner Lusitania.
- February-December 1916: The Battle of Verdun in northeastern France. The
battle was one of the longest and most deadly in the entire war.
- July-November 1916: The Battle of the Somme River which marked the first use
of tanks in warfare.
- April 6, 1917: The United States declares war against Germany.
- December 3, 1917: Russia signs a peace treaty with Germany.
- July-August 1918: The Second Battle of the Marne. This was the last major
German attack of the war and ended with an Allied victory.
- November 3, 1918: Austria-Hungary signs peace treaty with Allies.
- November 18, 1918: Germany and the Allies sign an agreement to stop
hostilities, officially ending the war. [See note above: the historical date of
the armistice is November 11, 1918.]

NEW TECHNOLOGY
Because trench warfare was so difficult, both sides looked to develop new weapons
that could give them an advantage. In 1916, the British first used an
armor-covered vehicle known as a tank in battle. The first tanks did not work
very well as they were hard to drive and many broke down. By 1917, however, the
British had improved the tank designs and the vehicles became effective weapons
on the battlefield.

In 1915, the Germans began using poisonous chemical gas as a weapon. The first
attack killed more than five thousand soldiers and injured more than ten
thousand. The French and British saw how deadly the weapon was and began
developing their own forms of poison gas. By the time the war ended, almost one
hundred thousand soldiers had been killed by chemical weapons.

Airplanes were a fairly new invention when the war began. They were first used
in battle as spy planes that were flown over the battlefield to see the position
of enemy troops. Later in the war, planes were used to drop bombs on enemy
targets. Eventually, planes were armed with machine guns that could fire at enemy
aircraft. This led to a form of air-to-air combat that became known as
dogfighting.

THE WAR'S END
When World War I started, the United States decided to stay out of the fighting,
but did support the warring nations by sending supplies to both sides. However,
German submarines often attacked any ship that was trying to reach Great
Britain. In May 1915, a British passenger ship called the Lusitania was sunk by
the Germans. Almost two thousand people were killed, including many Americans.
The sinking angered the United States, but the Germans continued to attack
civilian ships.

After several more US ships were attacked, the United States declared war
against Germany in 1917. The first US troops began fighting in October 1917, and
by 1918, more than two million US soldiers were fighting in Europe. The
reinforcements came just in time. In 1917, Russia left the war and signed a
peace treaty with Germany, which allowed the Germans to make one final push to
try to capture France.

With the help of the United States, the Allies were able to push the Germans
back and recapture much of France and Belgium. Other members of the Central
Powers were also defeated in important battles and forced to sign peace treaties
with the Allies. By late 1918, Germany also realized the war was lost and
decided to surrender. World War I officially ended on November 18, 1918 with the
signing of an agreement to stop fighting. [See note above: the historical date
of the armistice is November 11, 1918.]

WORDS TO KNOW
- alliance: An agreement between two or more countries to join forces or help
each other in time of war.
- chemical weapons: Weapons that use chemicals that can cause death or severe
injury.
- machine gun: An automatic gun that can fire many bullets very quickly.
- Ottoman Empire: An empire that ruled parts of Europe, Asia, and Africa for
centuries. It was broken up after World War I and became the modern nation of
Turkey.
- trench: A long, narrow ditch dug into the ground.

Original Text

Robert E. Lee (1807-1870)
Gale In Context: Middle School — Gale Document Number: GALE|FCQKPZ134286435
Source: Source link

Born: January 19, 1807 in Stratford, Virginia, United States
Died: October 12, 1870 in Lexington, Virginia, United States
Nationality: American
Occupation: General

Full Text:

Robert E. Lee started his career as a soldier in the United States Army, but he took up arms against the Union in the Civil War. He was the commander of the Army of Northern Virginia, which was one of the largest and most effective Confederate forces. When Lee surrendered to Union Army commander General Ulysses S. Grant at Appomattox, Virginia, the war was essentially over. Although Lee is considered a genius by military standards, some modern Americans view him as a traitor who fought for a racist cause.

FAST FACTS:
- Robert E. Lee graduated second out of forty-six students in his class at West Point.
- In 1859, Lee led forces that stopped abolitionist John Brown's raid at Harper's Ferry.
- When Lee resigned from the US Army in 1861, he had achieved the rank of colonel.

EARLY LIFE:
Robert E. Lee was born on January 19, 1807. He was the fifth of six children in his family. His parents were Colonel Henry Lee and Ann Hill Carter. His father had fought during the Revolutionary War. After the war Henry Lee made numerous bad business decisions. He put his family into debt and his family name suffered. The family had to move from Stratford Hall in Stratford, Virginia, to Alexandria.

In 1818, Lee's father died. Lee was only eleven years old. He took on new responsibilities since his father was gone. Lee wanted to be in the military as his father had been. When Lee was eighteen years old, he attended West Point Military Academy in New York. Lee graduated from the school in 1829. He never received a demerit while at the school and graduated near the top of his class. Lee chose to work in the Army Corps of Engineers after leaving school. In 1831, he married Mary Anna Randolph Custis, who was a relative of US President George Washington.

LIFE IN THE MILITARY:
Lee continued to work in the Army Corps of Engineers and moved often with his family. They lived in Virginia, New York, and Michigan. Lee was often away from his family when he was working projects, and Mary and Lee wrote many letters to each other. He was promoted to commander in 1838. When the United States entered the Mexican War in 1846, Lee served on the staff for General Winfield Scott and General John Wool. In 1855, Lee was given command of troops in the field.

By the early 1860s, Lee had been in the US Army for thirty years. As the country drew closer to war, Lee stated that he did not want the South to leave the Union. He also said that he was against slavery. Nevertheless, the southern states, including Lee's home state of Virginia, seceded and formed the Confederacy. President Abraham Lincoln asked Lee to command Union forces, but Lee said he could not fight against Virginia and left the US Army. He accepted a position in the Confederate Army, and in 1862, he took command of the Army of Northern Virginia.

CIVIL WAR SERVICE:
Lee was an important figure in the Confederate Army throughout the war. Many military historians believe he was the best general in the Confederate Army. Some believe he was the best general in US military history. Lee took risks and made important decisions quickly. Many men under his command liked him. He was known for living in the same conditions as the rest of his men. Lee oversaw numerous important Confederate victories, including Fredericksburg and Chancellorsville.

By 1865, it had become clear that the Confederacy had little chance of winning the war. Lee had many troops in his Army of Northern Virginia die or become deserters. In April 1865, Lee was low on supplies and did not have a large enough force to continue to fight. He surrendered to General Grant at the Appomattox Courthouse on April 9, 1865. Lee wrote a farewell address to his troops, but he did not admit defeat. His declaration set the stage for how other Southerners would see the outcome of the war for decades to come.

CONNECTIONS: MARY ANNA RANDOLPH CUSTIS:
Mary Anna Randolph Custis was born on October 1, 1807. She was the daughter of George Washington Parke Custis and Mary Fitzhugh Custis. George Washington Parke Custis's father, John Parke Custis, was US President George Washington's stepson. The family owned a farm, Arlington House, which they ran with the labor of enslaved people — including Mary Anna's half-sister, Maria. When Mary Anna and Robert E. Lee married, they lived for a time at Arlington House. Union forces took control of Arlington House during the war, and the land was eventually turned into Arlington Cemetery. Mary Anna was angry about the loss for the rest of her life.

LIFE AFTER THE WAR:
Lee left the military after the war. He stated publicly that all people in the United States should try to help restore the Union. He and many other Confederate leaders were charged with treason. However, President Andrew Johnson officially forgave Lee and other southern leaders in 1868.

Lee did not make many public speeches about his political beliefs. Nevertheless, Lee has drawn much criticism in modern times for his actions during the period. He is seen as a traitor because he led an Army against the United States and was responsible for the deaths of thousands of soldiers. Others see him as a symbol of racism because he personally commanded the South's army in a war to continue slavery. He also believed in a common idea among southerners that enslaved Black people were somehow inferior to whites. Lee remained an important figure after the war, and he received a position at Washington College, in Lexington, Virginia. He died at the college on October 12, 1870.

WORDS TO KNOW (from the article):
- commanded: Acted in charge of something.
- demerit: A bad mark on a school record.
- seceded: To decide to leave something.
- treason: The crime of trying to defeat your own country in war.

Charter Schools and School Choice

Maya and Leo unpack charter schools and school choice in the US: how publicly funded charter schools work, who supports and who criticizes school choice, and ten useful words.

a document you can exchange for goods, services, or money“Parents can use a voucher to help pay private school tuition.”

Original Text

Charter Schools and School Choice
Gale In Context: Middle School
https://link.gale.com/apps/doc/EZWFLO934099110/MSIC?u=sunnyvale15&sid=bookmark-MSIC&xid=1f0cac7e
Accessed 2026-09-09. COPYRIGHT 2026 Gale, a division of Cengage Learning, Inc.

Full Text:

Rally for School Choice [Image caption: Students, educators, and policy makers rally for school choice at the Texas Capitol in Austin, advocating a voucher plan where parents could choose to remove children from low-performing public schools into better charter schools.]

Most kindergarten-to-twelfth-grade (K-12) students in the United States attend their local public school. Public schools are publicly funded, which means they are paid for using money raised from taxes. However, parents have other options for their children's education. School choice is the ability to access an education option other than their local public school. Students may choose to do this if they have different learning needs.

The most common school choice options are private schools and charter schools. Private schools are not publicly funded and students must pay tuition to attend. However, many school choice programs allow for the use of public funds to help pay for private school tuition. Charter schools are publicly funded, but are independent of the public school system.

Fast Facts
- During the 2021-2022 school year, 83 percent of pre-K-12 students go to public schools, according to Pew Research Center.
- Seven percent of pre-K-12 students go to public charter schools.
- Ten percent of pre-K-12 students go to private schools.
- The percentage of students in charter schools has risen from 4 percent in 2011 to 7 percent in 2022, according to Pew Research Center.

Why Families Choose School Choice
School choice allows students and their parents to choose education options other than their local public school. Students and their parents may do this for several reasons. Some students may be gifted or excel in a certain subject. They may have a learning disability or developmental disability. In these cases, the student may benefit from attending a school better equipped to meet their needs. Supporters often describe school choice as allowing public education funds to follow students as needed.

Private Schools
Some students use school choice options to attend private schools. Private schools charge tuition. Public funding can be used to help cover tuition fees in several ways. The student's parents in some states may receive vouchers or tax credits or get money from the state government in a special bank account known as an Education Savings Account (ESA). Parents then use those funds to pay the student's tuition.

Charter Schools
Charter schools are another popular school choice option. These schools are publicly funded but accept students on the basis of school choice. They are called charter schools because they operate under a charter, which is permission from the government to run a school.

The first charter school was the City Academy, located in St. Paul, Minnesota. It started operations in 1992. The founders - long-time public school teachers - were frustrated with the public school systems since it did not allow them to create programs that they believed would be beneficial for low-performing students.

Special standards apply to charter schools. These schools have more freedom than regular K-12 public schools and are run and managed differently. At the same time, charter schools must also follow certain rules. They cannot charge tuition fees and are not allowed to have religious affiliations.

Charter schools have since grown in popularity across the United States. As of 2025, charter schools operate in forty-four states plus the District of Columbia, Guam, and Puerto Rico.

Other School Choice Options
The school choice model also supports other options. Students can use school choice to attend a different school in the public K-12 system. School choice also extends to magnet schools, microschools, online schools, and homeschooling.

Magnet schools are public schools for students with special, shared interests or skills. They have different curricula than regular K-12 public schools. Microschools are schools with very small classes that host students in different age groups. Teachers use specially developed curricula for each class in a microschool.

Online schools deliver education over the internet with personalized learning programs for each student. Homeschooling involves parents and tutors teaching students at home. Homeschooling may also include learning pods, which are small education programs led by groups of parents.

School choice became more widely used during the COVID-19 pandemic. As of 2023, school choice programs are available in thirty-two states. They are also supported in the District of Columbia.

Why It Matters
School choice allows students to attend schools that best meet their needs. Supporters say it improves learning outcomes for students. It also gives parents and students more ways to take control of their education options. This makes the public school system more accountable.

The Debate over School Choice
Supporters of school choice argue that it will force more competition among schools. This means that schools will be forced to innovate to attract students. This, in turn, will result in better outcomes for students. In 2023, Education Week reported on studies that showed long-term outperformance of charter schools over public schools. Second, supporters of school choice argue that for low-educational-outcome districts, allowing parents to send their children to charter schools is more fair. Third, those who support school choice often argue that it makes schools more accountable to parents.

Critics of school choice say that public schools can be improved through proper public investment. They question the evidence that supports better student outcomes in charter schools. Additionally, they question the correctness of using public funding to support essentially private schools with little government oversight. They say that school choice does not necessarily meet the purposes of a public school system.

Charter schools also have been criticized for having lower teacher retention due to lower pay. Additionally, there is criticism that money should flow to public schools over private schools. However, supporters note that for most charter schools, funding is on a per-pupil basis, so the money follows the student.

Words to Know
accountable - To answer for an action or decision.
affiliation - Ties or links.
curriculum - A sequence of subjects and topics taught to students in a school.
developmental disability - A disability affecting a person's physical or mental growth.
learning disability - A disability affecting a person's ability to learn.
tax credit - A sum of money that can be used to reduce the amount of taxes a person owes.
tuition - Money that students must pay to attend a school.
voucher - A document that a person can exchange for goods, services, or financial credits.

Source text supplied with episode 10

Checks and Balances: Sharing Power in America

Maya and Leo explore how America’s three branches of government check each other so no one branch becomes too powerful, from the presidential veto to judicial review.

Download full episode

Words in this episode
legislature
a lawmaking body.“Congress is the legislature of the United States.”
veto
an official rejection of a proposed law.“The president can veto a bill passed by Congress.”
override
to vote again and pass something despite a rejection.“Congress can override a presidential veto.”
impeachment
the process of formally accusing an official of misconduct.“The House can bring impeachment charges against the president.”
nominee
a person chosen for a position.“The Senate must approve the president’s nominees for the Supreme Court.”
judicial review
the power of courts to declare laws unconstitutional.“Judicial review began with the case of Marbury versus Madison.”
unconstitutional
against the Constitution.“Courts can strike down laws that are unconstitutional.”

to officially forgive a crime.“The president can pardon people convicted of federal crimes.”

democracy
government by the people.“Checks and balances help protect democracy.”
Original Text

Checks and Balances
Source: Gale In Context: Middle School (Source link)
Retrieved 2026-09-09.

Checks and balances are the rules and regulations behind the separation of powers in the US federal government. Written in 1787, the US Constitution created a democratic government divided into three separate parts: the legislative branch, the executive branch, and the judicial branch. The Constitution includes a system of rules designed to prevent any one of these branches from becoming more powerful than the others. In effect, these rules ensure that the government remains balanced by providing each branch with the tools to check the other two branches and keep them from becoming too powerful. For this reason, checks and balances are an important part of American government that play a key role in protecting and promoting democracy.

Fast Facts
- The idea of including a system of checks and balances as part of the organization of the American government was first mentioned by founding fathers James Madison and John Jay in the Federalist Papers, a series of articles the two wrote between 1787 and 1788 as a way of creating support for the Constitution.
- Because of the role he played in creating the system of checks and balances, the governmental gridlock that the system sometimes causes is known as Madison's Curse.
- The president's veto power is probably the most well-known example of checks and balances in the federal government, but Congress also had veto power for a time in the twentieth century. Congress' veto power gave it the ability to include special measures in a bill that would prevent the president from altering it in certain ways after it was passed. Most of the methods Congress used to exercise this power were found by the Supreme Court to be unconstitutional in 1983.

History
The history of checks and balances starts with the concept of separation of powers, which is the idea that government should be divided into separate branches. The notion of separation of powers was first suggested by the ancient Greek philosopher Aristotle. He suggested that the most successful type of government would be a mixed government that combined the best parts of monarchy, aristocracy, and democracy. Aristotle's ideas about the separation of powers inspired later political philosophers, including English thinker John Locke. In 1690, Locke wrote that to protect the peoples' rights, it was necessary to separate legislative and executive powers in government. Nearly sixty years later, French political philosopher Baron de Montesquieu added to this idea by suggesting that the judiciary, or the court system, should also be separate from the legislative and executive branches of government. When America's founding fathers set to work on writing the Constitution in the late eighteenth century, they followed Locke's and Montesquieu's advice as they built their new democracy. As a result, the American government was designed to include three separate branches with powers that would be kept equal through a system of checks and balances.

Legislative Branch
The legislative branch, which includes both houses of Congress, has many special abilities it can use to check the power of both the executive and judicial branches. When it comes to the executive branch, Congress has the ability to control how much power executive departments and agencies have and how those powers are used. Congress also has the ability to override a presidential veto and pass a bill that the president rejected. When the president puts together a budget proposal, he or she must submit it to Congress for approval. The House of Representatives and the Senate also have some separate responsibilities related to checking executive power. The Senate is responsible for approving or rejecting the president's nominees for cabinet posts. The Senate is also responsible for impeaching the president or other officials when necessary and approving all treaties with other countries. The House, meanwhile, is responsible for bringing impeachment charges against the president or other officials.
When it comes to the judicial branch, Congress is responsible for approving Supreme Court nominees and impeaching judges when necessary. Congress also has the power to create new court systems, do away with existing court systems, change the size of the Supreme Court, and determine which courts can try different types of cases.

Connections: Marbury v. Madison
The judicial branch's most important role in the system of checks and balances is the concept of judicial review. The Supreme Court's power of judicial review got its start thanks to the 1803 Marbury v. Madison case. That historic case centered around William Marbury, a businessman who President John Adams appointed as justice of the peace in Washington, DC, just before he left office. When the next president, Thomas Jefferson, refused to deliver Marbury's commission, Marbury and several other people in the same position filed a lawsuit that eventually reached the Supreme Court. Ultimately, the Court decided that Marbury and the others were entitled to their commissions, but held that it did not have the authority to force Jefferson to deliver them. In the long run, Marbury v. Madison established the idea that it was the court's responsibility to interpret the law. This, in turn, gave the court the power of judicial review and the ability to strike down laws that are found to be unconstitutional.

Executive Branch
The executive branch is responsible for checking the power of the legislative and judicial branches. Its legislative branch checks include the president's ability to veto bills passed by Congress and the ability to recommend possible legislation to Congress. In addition, the president can also pardon people convicted of federal crimes, and grant amnesty to those who may be tried for such crimes. Finally, the executive branch can also attempt to force the legislature to address important issues by calling special sessions of Congress when needed.
The executive branch's ability to check the power of the judicial branch basically boils down to one responsibility: appointing judges. The executive branch appoints all federal judges, including the justices who serve on the Supreme Court. Although Congress must approve these appointments, the president's ability to choose nominees for judicial positions allows him or her the power to influence the way the court system is likely to lean on important issues.

Judicial Branch
Although the judicial branch has only a few ways to check the powers of the legislative and executive branches, the abilities it does have to do so are especially important. Where the legislative branch is concerned, the court system holds the power of judicial review, which means that it can invalidate the laws passed by Congress if they are deemed to be unconstitutional.
The executive branch is also subject to checks from the judicial branch. Most often, this happens when judges in the court system rule that executive actions issued by the president are unconstitutional and unenforceable as law. In the event that Congress brings impeachment charges against the president or another official, the Supreme Court is tasked with presiding over the trial that follows.

Words to Know (from the article)
aristocracy: Government by a group of elite people.
bill: A proposed law.
democracy: Government by the people.
impeachment: A process by which an official is accused of misconduct and faces removal from office.
legislature: A lawmaking body.
monarchy: Government by one person.
philosopher: A person who seeks to learn about life, nature, and knowledge.

HistoryEpisode 84:48

Power to the People: The Story of Democracy

How rule by the people began in ancient Athens, fell to kings and dictators, and returned to shape the modern world.
