Original Text

Democracy - Gale In Context: Middle School (GALE|HNYGNC982173131)
Source: https://link.gale.com/apps/doc/HNYGNC982173131/MSIC?u=sunnyvale15&sid=bookmark-MSIC&xid=52d04cd8
COPYRIGHT 2026 Gale, a division of Cengage Learning, Inc.

Full Text:

Voting is a critical element of democracy.

A democracy is a form of government in which the citizens of a state or country have the overall power. Citizens usually express their will through voting, although many governments restrict the kinds of citizens who can vote. Democracies are unique among other forms of government, such as monarchies and dictatorships, in which all power is invested in one person or a small group of people. The idea of democracy was first demonstrated in ancient Greece, but it was soon replaced by widespread monarchy. Only in the 1600s and 1700s did democracy reemerge, and only by the twentieth century did its practice become truly widespread.

Fast Facts

- While democracy was catching on, so too was the idea of the political party, groups that choose leaders and promote their election to government. Today, political scientists see these parties as an essential part of a healthy democracy.

- The word democracy comes from Greek words meaning "rule by the people." This term originated around 450 BC with the rise of democratic ideas in Athens.

- Democracy became globally widespread in the twentieth century. By the twenty-first century, about one-third of all countries were democracies and an additional one-sixth observed some forms of democratic ideas.

Types of Governments

Over many hundreds of years, the countries of the world developed different forms of government. Long ago, one of the most widespread forms was the monarchy, a government ruled by a king or queen. A similar type of government, known as despotism or dictatorship, involves absolute rule by one individual. Other governments allowed more than one person to rule. These include oligarchies, in which a few people rule, and aristocracies, in which the entire social class of wealthy citizens rules.

Until the 1700s, the democratic form of government, in which the citizens of a country are in control of the government, was extremely rare. In most cases, democratic citizens make their opinions and desires known through voting. However, not all citizens always have the right to vote. In many democracies throughout history, strict and often unfair regulations have excluded certain groups of citizens from voting.

In modern times, democracy is one of the most popular and widely employed kinds of government around the world. Other forms of government common in the past have proven ineffective. Monarchies mostly crumbled after World War I (1914-1918) saw royal-led countries struggle and fail. Later in the twentieth century, dictatorships such as Nazi Germany and the Soviet Union also collapsed. In addition, growing economic freedom and opportunity helped to spread democratic attitudes globally.

Connections: An Ancient Criticism of Democracy

Not everyone agrees that democracy is the best policy. In ancient Greece, the birthplace of that form of government, the philosopher Plato spoke out against democracy. According to Plato, the regular citizens of a country are unlikely to provide suitable leadership to the country because most lack the intelligence, knowledge, and courage required to make wise decisions. Rather, the huge responsibility of decision-making should fall to nobles who are well educated in history, philosophy, culture, and many other fields.

Types of Democracies

Democracies can fall into two categories. The original and purest form of this government is known as a direct democracy, in which all voters physically meet in one place to determine new laws and make important decisions. This government usually worked best in small states or cities in which all interested voters were able to meet. The most common form of democracy in modern times, and the one practiced in the United States, is representative democracy. In this form of government, voters choose representatives to make laws and decisions in their place.

Democratic countries may take other forms as well. Most common in modern times is the democratic republic, a form of government in which citizen voters choose their leaders, usually through voting. However, some democratic countries still use elements of other forms of government, such as monarchies. Countries like the United Kingdom still have kings and queens, but these monarchs have little actual say in government, and most decisions are made democratically by elected leaders.

History of Democracy

The idea of democracy first manifested on a large scale about 2,500 years ago in ancient Greece. At that time, Greece was divided into a large assembly of independent but related government units called city-states. Each city-state could develop its own form of government, and some, most notably Athens, embraced different forms of democracy. Thinkers throughout Greece, such as Athenian leader Pericles and Greek philosopher Aristotle, praised the merits of democratic thought and action.

Despite the major contributions of the Greeks, their experiment in democracy did not last long. Greece and many other lands would come under the rule of monarchs and despots for many centuries. However, some countries attempted democracies, and the idea of democracy persisted. Some Native American groups developed democracy-like systems. In the 1600s and 1700s, during the Enlightenment period, the idea began to flourish as philosophers and political scientists such as John Locke and John Stuart Mill dared to criticize the systems of kings and monarchies.

The new call for democracy became a reality in 1776, when British colonists in North America rebelled against their king and created a new, democratic form of government called the United States. After a long and punishing war, the United States became an independent republic and the envy of many nations that still struggled under monarchies and dictatorships. Only in the twentieth century did those forms of government begin to collapse and open the way for a more democratic world.

Words to Know

economic - Pertaining to buying, selling, and other ways people use money.

Enlightenment - A period in which people began questioning traditions and finding new ideas about how to live.

philosopher - A person who thinks deeply about and questions the ways of the world.

regulations - Rules that establish how something may be done or who may participate.

HistoryEpisode 075:08

Storming the Bastille: The French Revolution

Follow the French Revolution from an unfair monarchy and the storming of the Bastille to the Reign of Terror and Napoleon’s rise.

Full episode

5:08 · Story, history, and vocabulary

Download full episode

Words in This Episode

1:47 · Slow vocabulary review

Words in this episode
monarchy
a government ruled by a king or queen.“Before the revolution, France was an absolute monarchy.”
revolution
It means the forceful overthrow of a government.“The French Revolution overthrew the king.”
resentment
It means a feeling of anger when something feels unfair.“The third estate felt deep resentment toward the nobles who paid no taxes.”
bankrupt
It means having no money left and unable to pay your debts.“After spending billions on the American war, France was nearly bankrupt.”
equality
It means everyone being treated the same.“The Declaration of the Rights of Man promised equality before the law.”
republic
It means a government run by elected leaders instead of a king or queen.“In seventeen ninety-two, France became a republic.”

It means a crime against your own country.“Louis the Sixteenth was charged with treason.”

It means extreme fear.“The Reign of Terror brought ten months of mass executions.”

Original Text

URL: https://link.gale.com/apps/doc/YXFNQJ179124173/MSIC?u=sunnyvale15&sid=bookmark-MSIC&xid=94abbef9 Reading levels: 800L, 950L

Pre-revolutionary France

While America rebelled against a constitutional monarchy — in which the monarch is the head of state but the ability to make laws resides with an elected government — France was ruled by an absolute monarchy, in which the monarch has complete power over the people. The French people were divided into three estates (levels). The first consisted of the clergy, the second, aristos (nobility), and the third included everyone else, from the wealthiest merchant to the poorest farmer. The first two estates made up a tiny part of the population, controlled most of the wealth, and paid no taxes. By the eighteenth century, a great deal of resentment against the church and the nobility existed among the members of the third estate.

Fast Facts

- People identified King Louis XVI after he escaped using coins that had his face printed on them. - The revolutionaries had their own calendar and means of telling time. They used this calendar in France during the revolution. - Few people were being held at the Bastille when the mob attacked it, but the mob did find and take weapons and supplies from the building.

The National Assembly

When the American Revolution started, King Louis XVI's (1754-1793) government offered secret aid to the colonists in the form of money, arms, and uniforms. The French did not share the colonists' belief in democracy. Rather, they wanted revenge for their embarrassing loss in the Seven Years' War. In 1777, with the war starting to turn against the British, France entered into an open alliance with America.

France's assistance to the Americans was expensive. The monarchy spent more than two billion livres on the American Revolution and still had debts from the previous war. By 1789, the state was virtually bankrupt. The harvests had been poor and much of the country was starving.

The king's ministers tried to remedy matters. Yet, the aristocrats were unwilling to surrender any of their privileges and resisted all attempts at financial reform. With no other option, Louis called for an Estates-General, an assembly in which representatives from the three estates could air their complaints.

It was the first time an Estates-General had been called in more than 170 years. As a result, the structure of the legislative body was not clearly defined. The first and second estates tried to establish rules to their own advantage. They insisted that each estate should have only one vote. Under those conditions, they would win any measure two to one.

The angry third estate broke away and declared itself the National Assembly. After this rebellion, the king ordered that the assembly hall be locked. Undisturbed, the new assembly met in the tennis courts across the street, where they swore to create a national constitution.

Bastille Day

In the meantime, an episode called the "Great Fear" gripped France. Riots swept across the country, fueled by hungry, angry peasants. Rumors spread that the aristos planned to subdue the third estate by force. On July 14, 1789, armed Parisians attacked the Bastille prison, killing the warden and guards. The prison was nearly empty at the time. But the Bastille had previously held political prisoners, often indefinitely and usually without trial. Its fall held great importance for the revolutionaries.

In response, the king legalized the National Assembly and ordered the other estates to participate in it. On August 11, the assembly produced the Declaration of the Rights of Man and of the Citizen, which declared all French males equal in the eyes of the law. The assembly then turned its attention to creating a constitution.

Timeline: French Revolution

- May 5, 1789: Estates-General meets in Versailles. - July 14, 1789: A mob attacks the Bastille. - June 20-21, 1791: King Louis and Marie-Antoinette escape their palace in Paris. - September 14, 1791: France adopts its first constitution, giving more power to the French people. - September 22, 1792: France becomes a republic. - January 21, 1793: King Louis XVI is executed. - September 1793: The Reign of Terror, a ten-month period of mass executions, begins. - July 28, 1794: The Reign of Terror ends. - November 9, 1799: Napoleon Bonaparte takes control and brings the French Revolution to an end.

War

Over the next two years, many French aristocrats immigrated to England and Austria. In France, they were called emigres and traitors. Abroad, they stirred up anger against the new French government. In June 1791, Louis XVI also tried to escape the country with his family. However, they were recognized before they could reach the border. The assembly forced them to return to Paris and put them under house arrest.

By 1792, Austria and Prussia had formed an army led by emigres who were bent on attacking France and restoring the old administration. To prevent this, France declared war on Austria. The opening battles went horribly for the French. Austrian forces overwhelmed them at Verdun. The desperate Legislative Assembly passed a measure to recruit a larger army. Hoping to be rescued by the Austrians, Louis vetoed the bill.

The invasion increased the anger Parisians felt. They blamed French losses on the constitutional monarchy and on the king in particular. On August 9, revolutionaries stormed the palace, killing all the defenders. The next day, the king's power was suspended.

Answering the call of patriotism, thousands of men joined the French army. In September, the Austrian army was pushed back at Valmy. The victorious revolutionary government abolished the monarchy entirely and declared the First French Republic, led by an assembly called the National Convention. Louis XVI was charged with treason (crimes against one's country). He was convicted and then beheaded by guillotine in early 1793.

The Reign of Terror

The king's death helped push Great Britain and Holland into the war because they feared the revolution would spread to their countries. In addition, people in the western province of the Vendee, who were angered by the military drafts and food shortages, rose up against the new government. Threatened on all sides, the National Convention created the Committee of Public Safety. In September 1793, the committee was given almost unlimited power to prosecute suspected counterrevolutionaries.

The committee's rule, known as the Reign of Terror, horrified the world. Led by Maximilien Robespierre (1758-1794), the committee tried and executed more than 17,000 men and women. The victims included the former queen, Marie Antoinette, and members of the old nobility. Commoners were also executed. The terror did not end until Robespierre himself was executed in July 1794.

Napoleon

After the Reign of Terror, a five-member directory governed the country. The war raged on as France attempted to "liberate" its neighbors from their rulers. After four years, spirits were low, the economy was bad, and the constitution was unstable. In 1799, the directory fell in a military takeover. General Napoleon Bonaparte (1769-1821) established himself as the new head of state. By 1804, he crowned himself emperor. After fifteen years, the French Revolution had shaken off monarchy only to fall into dictatorship.

Words to Know (from the article)

- aristocrats: Members of a noble privileged class. In France, this was the aristos, or second estate. - Bastille: A prison in Paris. The storming of the Bastille by angry peasants marked the beginning of the revolution. - dictatorship: A government that ruled by an individual who has absolute power, a dictator. - monarchy: A form of government that has a monarch (such as a king or queen) as its head. - republic: A form of government in which power is held by elected representatives of the people. - revolution: The forcible or violent overthrow of a government by people who want a new form of government.

Transcript

Maya: Here's a riddle for you, Leo. Legend says this city was founded by twin brothers raised by a wolf. At its peak, it ruled up to ninety million people. What city is it?

Leo: Twins raised by a wolf? That has to be Rome.

Maya: That's right. Today we're going back more than two thousand years to ancient Rome.

Leo: Was Rome always a huge empire?

Maya: No. It started as one small city on the Italian Peninsula in seven fifty-three B C. First it was a kingdom, ruled by kings. Then in five oh nine B C, the Romans threw out their last king and started a republic.

Leo: What's the difference between a kingdom and a republic?

Maya: In a kingdom, one royal family rules. In a republic, the people choose their leaders. Rome had a Senate, a group of leaders who debated and voted on laws.

Leo: That sounds like the United States.

Maya: It should. The United States modeled its Senate on Rome's. The Romans also invented checks and balances, rules that stop any one leader from getting too much power.

Leo: But Rome didn't stay a republic, did it?

Maya: No. The republic grew rich and divided, and that led to a civil war, a war between people in the same country.

Leo: Is that where Julius Caesar comes in?

Maya: Yes. Caesar was a famous general who won huge wars. He grabbed nearly unlimited power and became a dictator. Some senators feared him, so they murdered him.

Leo: What happened next?

Maya: More fighting. Then Caesar's adopted son, Augustus, took charge. He called himself emperor, and the republic became the Roman Empire.

Leo: How big did it get?

Maya: Enormous. At its peak, it stretched from England to North Africa, and from Spain to Syria. More than two million square miles of land.

Leo: Did the Romans build anything, or just conquer?

Maya: They built a lot. Roman architecture gave us arches, domes, and the Colosseum, where gladiators fought. They also built straight roads and aqueducts that carried fresh water into cities.

Leo: What did Rome leave behind for us today?

Maya: Its language. Latin turned into Spanish, French, and Italian. Roman law shaped our courts. Our twelve-month calendar comes from Rome's calendar. And Christianity, once a banned religion, became legal under Emperor Constantine.

Leo: So with all that power, why did Rome fall?

Maya: It got too big to manage. Civil wars, weak emperors, and invasions by tribes the Romans called barbarians wore it down. In four seventy-six A D, the western half of the empire collapsed, though the eastern half survived a thousand more years.

Leo: Kingdom, then republic, then empire. I've got the big picture.

Maya: Let's lock it in with our vocabulary spotlight. We'll go slowly, so listen and repeat each word with us.

Maya: Let us slow down and learn our words together. Word one is republic, which means a country where the people choose their leaders instead of a king or queen. Rome was a republic for almost five hundred years.

Leo: Word two is senate, the group of leaders who debated and voted on laws in ancient Rome. The United States has a Senate too.

Maya: Word three is empire, a group of many lands ruled by one leader. At its peak, the Roman Empire ruled fifty to ninety million people.

Leo: Word four is emperor, the single ruler of an empire. Augustus was the first emperor of Rome.

Maya: Word five is dictator, a ruler with nearly unlimited power. Julius Caesar became a dictator before he was murdered.

Leo: Word six is civil war, a war between people in the same country. After Caesar's murder, a civil war broke out in Rome.

Maya: Word seven is barbarian, what the Romans called people outside their empire. We know that word is unfair today, and we should not use it that way.

Leo: Word eight is peninsula, land surrounded by water on three sides. Italy is a peninsula in the Mediterranean Sea.

Maya: Word nine is architecture, the art of designing buildings. Roman architecture gave us arches, domes, and the Colosseum.

Leo: And word ten is collapse, which means to fall down or fall apart. The western half of the Roman Empire collapsed in four seventy-six A D.

Maya: That is all ten words. Say them with us once more as we use them.

Maya: Now a quick quiz. Question one. True or false: in a republic, one king rules the country.

Leo: Think it through. False. In a republic, the people choose their leaders.

Maya: Question two. Who became the first emperor of Rome after Julius Caesar died?

Leo: Take a guess. Augustus, Caesar's adopted son.

Maya: Last one. The word empire most nearly means a small village, a group of lands ruled by one leader, a type of building, or a kind of food?

Leo: It means a group of lands ruled by one leader.

Maya: You did great. So remember, Rome grew from a small kingdom into a republic with a Senate, then into a mighty empire under Augustus. It gave us Latin, our calendar, and the Colosseum, before the west collapsed in four seventy-six A D. See you next time on The World, Word by Word.

Ancient Greece: Gods, Games, and the Birth of Democracy

Maya and Leo travel back over two thousand years to explore city-states, the first democracy, the Olympic games, Mount Olympus, and the Trojan Horse legend.

Original Text

Ancient Greece https://link.gale.com/apps/doc/ZWNSXN165560554/MSIC?u=sunnyvale15&sid=bookmark-MSIC&xid=ee5812fb Fetched 2026-09-09

Few civilizations in history ever had as great an impact on human society as ancient Greece. Many of the ideas that played a key role in the development of the modern world first came from, or were greatly advanced by, the ancient Greeks. Some of these include philosophy (the study of the nature of knowledge, reality, and existence), democracy (government ruled by the people), and mathematics. The history of ancient Greece is divided into three different eras: the Archaic age (700-480 BC), Classical age (480-323 BC), and the Hellenistic age (323-30 BC). In just this short period, however, ancient Greece forever changed the world and set the stage for the future of all humankind.

Fast Facts - The Greek writer Homer wrote the world-famous tale of the Trojan War around 700 BC. The tale had likely been passed down by storytellers for hundreds of years before Homer wrote it down. - For centuries, many people believed the Trojan War story was just a legend. However, modern archaeologists have found remains suggesting that the conflict really happened on a hilltop in Turkey. - Ancient tales claim that Mycenaean Greek forces won the Trojan War by hiding soldiers in a huge wooden statue of a horse. When the Trojans took the horse into their city, the soldiers leapt out to attack.

The Roots of Ancient Greece The story of ancient Greece begins with the Minoans and the Mycenaeans. The Minoans were the first people to live in the region known today as Greece. Historians are not sure where they came from, but the Minoans arrived in Greece sometime before 2000 BC and settled on the island of Crete. The Minoans had one of the world's great civilizations. They built great structures, had their own language, and traded with other cultures. After some sort of disaster that happened around 1450 BC, however, the Minoans were wiped out.

The Mycenaeans came to Greece from the area around the Black Sea in about 2800 BC. They also built a great civilization, but always lived in the shadow of the Minoans. When the Minoans disappeared, however, the Mycenaeans began to thrive. Mycenaean culture grew and improved until Macedonian barbarians invaded from the north around 1100 BC. After the invasion, the Mycenaean civilization collapsed and the Greek Dark Ages began. During the Dark Ages, most of the advances the Greeks made up to that point were lost.

Archaic Greece Greece started coming out of the Dark Ages around 700 BC. By that time, the Greeks were starting to see themselves as one culture, even calling themselves the Hellenes. One of the main reasons why this happened was religion. Most Greeks believed in a group of gods who lived high atop Mount Olympus. The king of the Greek gods was Zeus. Some of the other gods included Poseidon (Zeus's brother and the god of the sea), Hades (another of Zeus's brothers and the god of the underworld), and Hera (Zeus's wife and the goddess of women and marriage).

Many important things happened during the Archaic era. For one, the Greeks began to trade more with other people in the outside world and increased their wealth and knowledge as a result. For another, two of the many separate Greek communities known as city-states began to grow more powerful than the rest. These were Sparta and Athens. While life in Sparta revolved around strict rules designed to create strong soldiers, Athens became home to the world's first democratic government. Together, Sparta and Athens twice stopped the Persian Empire from successfully invading Greece. While all this was happening, Greece was going through a period of growing interest in science, philosophy, and the arts that led to the beginning of the greatest period in Greek history.

Classical Greece Starting around 480 BC, Greece entered the Classical age, which is usually seen as the golden era of ancient Greece. During the Classical age, Athens became the leading cultural center of the entire world. For two hundred years, the people of Athens made new scientific discoveries, built great temples to their gods, and wrote and performed in the world's first plays. Athens was also the home of great thinkers such as Socrates, Plato, and Aristotle. Perhaps most importantly, Athens's government model of rule by the people introduced the world to the idea of democracy.

Although many good things happened in Greece during the Classical age, it was also a time of war. First, there was another war between the Greek city-states and the Persians. Later, there was a terrible conflict between Athens and Sparta called the Peloponnesian War. In the end, Athens lost the war with Sparta. This defeat brought about the end of Athens's golden era and left Sparta in power. When Sparta was later defeated in key battles in 371 and 362 BC, the Macedonians took control of Greece. In 336 BC, Alexander the Great rose to power and made Greece part of his empire.

Timeline: Selected Events from Classical Greece - 479 BC: Greek armies defeat the Persian forces of Xerxes. - 478 BC: The city-state of Athens forms the Delian League, an alliance of hundreds of Greek city-states. - 427 BC: Birth of the philosopher Plato in Athens. - 384 BC: Birth of the philosopher Aristotle. - 356-323 BC: Life of Alexander the Great.

Hellenistic Greece When Alexander the Great died in 323 BC, the Classical age ended and the Hellenistic age began. Although Greece itself was crumbling at this time, Greek culture spread throughout the Mediterranean and Middle East. This was mostly thanks to the strong Greek influence in the Egyptian city of Alexandria, which had been an important part of the empire of Alexander the Great. The Hellenistic age continued until Greece was eventually taken over by Rome. The Romans borrowed many of the Greeks' ideas and traditions, and Greek culture lived on to influence the way people live even in the modern world.

Words to Know - barbarian: An uncivilized person who is not a member of a community. - Black Sea: A body of water where Europe and Asia meet. - city-state: An independent city. - Crete: The largest Greek island in the Mediterranean Sea.

Source: Gale In Context: Middle School · Retrieved September 9, 2026

HistoryEpisode 45:45

Manhattan Project: The Race for the Atomic Bomb

How a secret wartime race to build the first atomic bomb ended World War II and started the nuclear age.

Full episode

5:45 · Story, knowledge, and vocabulary

Original Text

Manhattan Project - Gale In Context: Middle School (Document GALE|QISLOZ660260272, retrieved 2026-09-09)

Full Text:

The first atomic bomb exploded as part of the Manhattan Project on July 16, 1945. Scientists tested their device in what was known as the Trinity test in the desert south of Santa Fe, New Mexico.

The Manhattan Project was a secret project by the US government that led to the creation of the first atomic bomb. The project took place during World War II. The United States dropped atomic bombs on Hiroshima and Nagasaki in Japan. This changed the course of World War II and marked the start of the nuclear age.

Fast Facts

- The discovery of atomic fission and the resulting released energy led to the development of nuclear weapons.
- Scientists such as Albert Einstein encouraged US President Franklin D. Roosevelt to accelerate the United States's research of atomic energy.
- The Manhattan Project developed two types of atomic bombs. The United States used these weapons in World War II, dropping them on Hiroshima and Nagasaki, Japan.

The Beginnings of the Manhattan Project

In 1938, three German chemists, Otto Hahn, Lise Meitner, and Fritz Strassmann, discovered a process called nuclear fission. In their experiments, the scientists bombarded uranium atoms with neutrons. This resulted in nuclear fission, or the splitting of the nucleus of a uranium atom. The process of fission released tremendous amounts of energy.

This announcement of this discovery raised concerns in other countries that the energy released in nuclear fission could be used by Nazi Germany to create a powerful new weapon. Many scientists were concerned. Albert Einstein wrote a letter to US President Franklin D. Roosevelt in 1939. This letter warned of the potential dangers of nuclear fission. Concerns included the fear of Nazi Germany developing an atomic bomb. Einstein urged Roosevelt to increase its efforts in nuclear research.

Organizing the Project

In 1942, the US government launched the Manhattan Project. It fell under the leadership of the Army Corps of Engineers. General Leslie Groves was appointed as the military head of the project. Physicist J. Robert Oppenheimer became its scientific director. Together, they oversaw a large network of scientists, engineers, and workers, many of whom were unaware of the project's ultimate goal.

The project's name, "Manhattan," was based on the Manhattan Engineer District in New York, although much of the research and development occurred elsewhere. Key facilities included the primary site for designing and assembling the bombs in Los Alamos, New Mexico. Plutonium and uranium was produced for the bombs at three facilities in Oak Ridge, Tennessee.

The project provided jobs for over 130,000 people because it needed a massive workforce to run laboratories, factories, and testing sites. Ensuring security and secrecy was a top priority. To prevent information leaks, workers often were not told the full scope of their work. Despite these measures, Soviet spies managed to infiltrate the project. In doing so, they were able to steal critical information that helped accelerate the Soviet Union's own nuclear program.

Connections: The Role of Women in the Manhattan Project

While the Manhattan Project is often associated with prominent male scientists, many women played crucial roles. Female physicists, chemists, and mathematicians contributed to the research. For example, Leona Woods was a physicist who worked on the Chicago Pile (CP-1), the first nuclear reactor. She was the youngest member of her team and the only woman. In addition, thousands of women worked in factories and laboratories across the country. Women working in uranium processing plants and other facilities endured difficult and sometimes hazardous conditions.

Scientific Breakthroughs

The Manhattan Project achieved several groundbreaking scientific milestones. One of its most significant achievements was the development of two types of atomic bombs. They were named Little Boy and Fat Man. Little Boy was a uranium-based bomb. Fat Man was plutonium-based.

The first successful test of a nuclear device occurred on July 16, 1945, in the New Mexico desert using the Fat Man type of bomb. Known as the Trinity Test, the explosion was equivalent to about 20,000 tons of TNT. This event marked the start of the nuclear era.

The Trinity Test was not only a scientific achievement but also a profoundly emotional moment for those involved. The explosion carries with it a blinding flash of light, a wave of heat, a shock wave, a deafening roar, and a towering mushroom cloud. Many scientists were awed by the destructive power they had unleashed. Some expressed remorse. Others saw it as a necessary step to end the war.

The Bombings of Hiroshima and Nagasaki

On August 6, 1945, Little Boy was dropped on Hiroshima, Japan. An estimated 140,000 people were dead by the end of the year. Three days later, on August 9, Fat Man was dropped on Nagasaki, resulting in approximately 70,000 deaths.

These bombings led to Japan's surrender, effectively ending World War II. However, the bombings also led to ethical debates about using such destructive weapons.

In the aftermath of the bombings, survivors, known as the Hibakusha, faced immense challenges. Many suffered from radiation sickness, injuries, and psychological trauma. Their stories have since become a powerful testament to the human cost of nuclear warfare and a call for global disarmament.

Legacy and Impact

It was not until 1945 that officials released information about the Manhattan Project. The project demonstrated the power of science and technology to address global challenges. It also ushered in a new era of global politics dominated by the threat of nuclear warfare.

In 1949, the Soviet Union developed its own atomic bomb. With the United States no longer the only one with nuclear weapons at its disposal came decades of tension and competition between the two superpowers. This period was known as the Cold War. Additionally, the advancements in nuclear technology had far-reaching effects on medicine and other scientific fields.

Words to Know

atom - The basic particle of an element.
atomic bomb - A bomb that harnesses the energy released from nuclear fission.
nucleus - The center of an atom.
nuclear fission - The splitting apart of an atomic nucleus.
plutonium - A radioactive metal used as an explosive in nuclear fission weapons.
uranium - A radioactive metal used as an explosive in nuclear fission weapons.

ScienceEpisode 35:24

Black Holes: The Universe's Darkest Mystery

Black holes are born when giant stars die, their gravity traps even light, and the universe is stranger than any movie.

5:24 · Story, knowledge, and vocabulary

Original Text

Black Holes
(Gale In Context: Middle School)

Black holes are very dense objects in space with such high gravity nothing can escape their grip—not even light. Most black holes are the remains of giant, dead stars that have used up all their fuel. Famous scientist Albert Einstein first predicted black holes more than a century ago, but they were not discovered until the 1970s. Black holes are very strange and dangerous objects. They are so powerful that anything that got too close would be stretched out like spaghetti. Their gravity is so strong that it is even able to warp space and make time slow down.

Fast Facts

- The first black hole ever discovered is called Cygnus X-1. It got this name because it was in the constellation of Cygnus, the swan. The X stands for X-ray source.
- American scientist John Wheeler came up with the term "black hole" in 1967 to describe the mysterious objects.
- In 2019, scientists were able to take the first picture of a supermassive black hole at the center of our galaxy.

How Black Holes Form

Black holes are the objects left over after giant stars explode and die. Stars are giant balls of superhot gas with temperatures so high at their cores that atoms are fused together to form new atoms. The fuel for this process is the element hydrogen. When two hydrogen atoms are fused together, they form a new atom called helium. The energy given off by the process is what makes stars appear to shine.

A star about the size of our Sun turns about 600 million tons of hydrogen into helium every second. That may seem like a lot, but the Sun has so much hydrogen fuel it will take about 5 billion years for it to run out. When this happens, the Sun will begin fusing helium into other elements. The core will get even hotter and make the star's outer layers expand outward, turning the Sun into a red giant. Eventually, its outer layers will blow away into space. Its core will shrink to become a white dwarf star. These are small stars that slowly cool over billions of years.

Something very different happens to stars that are bigger than the Sun. These stars use up their hydrogen fuel very quickly. When these stars die, their cores undergo a violent explosion called a supernova. A supernova is so powerful that it can destroy an entire solar system and shine brighter than a galaxy. The star's outer layers are blasted into space and its core is squeezed so tightly that it shrinks down to just a few miles across. The remains of a supernova are very dense and have very high gravity.

If a star is about twenty times the mass of our Sun, something even stranger happens. When a very large star undergoes a supernova, the explosion squeezes its core so much that it becomes incredibly small. All the mass of the star is squished into a very small and very dense point called a singularity. Its gravity is so high that not even light can escape its pull. Astronomers call these objects black holes because they do not give off any light. The boundary, or mouth, of a black hole is called the event horizon. Once something passes this event horizon, it can never escape from the black hole's gravity.

Connections: Could You Survive Inside a Black Hole?

In 2014, a movie called Interstellar told the story of an astronaut who fell into a black hole. He was able to do strange things like travel back in time. The movie used real science to show what traveling near a black hole would be like. However, scientists do not believe anyone could really survive a trip into a black hole. Before an astronaut could get close enough, they would probably be killed by the very hot temperatures. If they somehow survived, they would be stretched out and spaghettified. An astronaut watching from a safe distance would never see any of this happen. To that astronaut, the visitor to the black hole would seem to stop, turn red, and slowly fade away over a long time.

Discovering Black Holes

When scientists first learned about gravity in the 1700s, they wondered if an object could have so much gravity that it could trap light. They did not know such objects actually existed. In 1916, German scientist Albert Einstein came up with a theory that predicted the existence of black holes. By using math, scientists found that black holes were real, but it was many years before they were able to actually view one.

Because black holes do not give off light, they are invisible to normal telescopes. However, when matter and energy are pulled into a black hole, the area around the event horizon gets very hot. This area gives off powerful radiation that can be detected on Earth. In 1971, scientists were able to use a special telescope to find a black hole for the first time. The telescope was able to detect a type of radiation called X-rays. Scientists used the X-ray footprint left behind by the black hole to discover its location.

The gravity of a black hole is so strong that it can actually bend, or warp, space. This is similar to how a bowling ball makes the surface of a mattress dip or pucker under its weight. If a spaceship were to fall into a black hole, gravity would squeeze the ship and stretch it out until it was completely destroyed. Scientists call this process spaghettification, because the ship would look like stretched out spaghetti. Gravity near a black hole also does something strange to time. Astronauts close to a black hole would see time slow down compared to an astronaut back on Earth.

Types of Black Holes

Black holes formed by the remains of dead stars are called stellar black holes. Scientists believe millions of them may exist in our galaxy. Some black holes are so huge that they are called supermassive black holes. These objects can be found at the center of galaxies where they gather large amounts of matter and grow bigger and bigger. Supermassive black holes can be millions or billions of times more massive than our Sun. Scientists still do not know exactly how they form.

Some objects are larger than stellar black holes but smaller than supermassive black holes. These are called intermediate black holes. Scientists think these could form when two smaller black holes join together. Or, they could form when many stars that are very close together collide. Another type of black hole is a miniature black hole. Scientists are not sure if these types of black holes really exist, but if they did, they would be about the size of an atom.

Words to Know

- element: A chemical substance made from a single type of atom.
- gravity: A force that attracts objects toward the center of a large body, such as a star.
- mass: The amount of matter in an object.
- radiation: A type of energy that moves in the form of waves or particles.
- theory: An explanation about something based on scientific facts and evidence.

(Source: Gale In Context: Middle School, Document Number GALE|VHUEUP981711201)

Source text supplied with episode 3

Original Text

City of Gold
(Source: Sadlier Unit 1, “City of Gold” <First-Person Narrative>)

Long ago in western Africa, bands of traders traveled to the city of Timbuktu to buy goods in exchange for gold. The following account is given by a boy describing his first trip to the famous city. He is with a group of friends in the year 1450.

My father has been carrying gold from our land to sell in Timbuktu since he was young. His father was a gold trader before him, and now I've joined him and my brothers. We joined other travelers with their goods and camels in a caravan. We brought gold to Timbuktu to sell, and now I've come home. I'll tell you about that city of gold, because soon you'll be old enough to go there with your fathers, too. I'm famished—I haven't eaten since morning. Let's enjoy this small repast together while I talk.

I'll start at the end: What a gainful expedition! You saw the great load of goods we brought home. Immense packages of salt, some expressly for our own use, but most for trade here and to the south. We brought back kola nuts to chew on, some palm oil for cooking, and fine cloth for clothing. We profited more than usual, because the buyers of gold wanted more than was available. One man even offered to lend my father gold at interest so we could trade more before having to leave. Father refused, saying, “Lend your money and lose a friend.”

Before the journey, I had imagined that we would take our bags of gold to Timbuktu and trade a handful here and there. What an inept trader I would become, if not for the wisdom of my father! My father is an ingenious man who knows his way around the world. Before we even entered the city, we stopped to rest by the river. We washed and set up a cooking fire. My father left and returned with a wealthy merchant and his men. They brought us much salt in exchange for our gold. My father and this merchant, who's a big man in Taghaza up north, chewed kola nuts and spoke like old friends. Then, the merchant dispatched his men, ordering them back to their camp. Their compliance was instantaneous, and I watched them recede into the distance, carrying gold this time instead of salt. Irked by my idling, my father told me to stop gawking like an oaf and start packing the salt.

We ate a quick meal and doused the fires. At last, I entered the city of Timbuktu. There are people in Timbuktu of every shape, size, and color, and they come from everywhere, wearing all sorts of apparel and speaking many languages. Many speak Arabic, and many speak Mande, like us. There are thousands of people in that city. It's a busy place, but exciting, with massive mosques and palaces and markets.

We exchanged the rest of our gold in the marketplaces. Because there was so much happening, our time in the city went quickly. Now that I am home, I confess that I had some misgivings before I went. I feared thieves would attack our caravan. I wondered if the city might be raided or besieged while we were there. But everything went smoothly on my first visit. What a place, that busy city—I can hardly wait till we return!

ScienceEpisode 014 min

How You Breathe

Follow oxygen from your first breath to every cell in your body, then test yourself with eight useful English words and a quick quiz.

Full episode

4 min · Story, science, and vocabulary

Download full episode

Words in This Episode

1:35 · Slow vocabulary review

Download words review
Words in this episode
microscopicsophisticatedadequateintricatecirculatesedentarydebriscortisol
Original Text

Oxygen from the air we breathe may be the most basic of human needs. People can survive for weeks without food and days without water but only several minutes without access to oxygen. The human body, comprised of microscopic cells, requires a constant flow of oxygen to produce the energy necessary to move, grow, maintain, repair, and reproduce. Human beings have sophisticated respiratory systems to make sure we access adequate amounts of this fundamental element.

Our respiratory system includes our noses, mouths, throats, and lungs; the tubes and passageways that connect these organs to one another; and our diaphragm and chest muscles that enable us to breathe. The brain, heart, and bloodstream are also involved in the process of acquiring and circulating oxygen. The brain directs the body's functioning, and the heart pumps blood at varying speeds throughout the circulatory system to ensure that our cells receive the oxygen necessary for survival.

This intricate system serves most of us very efficiently. However, the respiratory system can be damaged by inhaled smoke and toxic debris. A modern sedentary lifestyle that does not include much exercise can also stress the smooth functioning of our life-sustaining systems. Therefore, a clean environment and sufficient exercise are crucial to the optimal health of our respiratory systems and, ultimately, us.

Our respiratory systems are also affected by stress. When we are under duress, our endocrine system, which produces hormones, may secrete additional cortisol. Such stress hormones have many effects, some of which are increasing our heart rate and breathing rate. This means that not only exercise but also stress can make us breathe harder. It turns out that our breathing, heart rate, and stressful thoughts and feelings are more directly connected than many of us realize. Studying the respiratory system can thus lead to understandings of much greater importance than simply how oxygen circulates in the human bloodstream.

Source: Test Innovators SSAT Middle Level reading passage
